# Changelog

All notable changes to GAIA React are documented here.

The format follows [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/); the project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

- **Major**: breaking changes to skill/command API, Node/React/React Router major bumps.
- **Minor**: new skills, commands, or wiki concept pages; opt-in features, removed or renamed `.claude/` paths.
- **Patch**: bugfixes, docs, and in-range dependency bumps.

## Adopter-action convention

A release change that requires the adopter to act, run a command or hand-migrate, is authored as a `### Removed` or `### Changed` entry carrying an explicit **Action required:** line and/or a literal command to run (for example, `pnpm remove <pkg>`). That exact phrasing is the deterministic anchor GAIA's two CHANGELOG consumers key on: `/update-gaia` cross-references it during a merge to surface a documented, opt-in cleanup suggestion (it never auto-removes a dependency or deletes a file, the adopter decides), and the maintainer-only `release-notes` skill keys on it to tell an agent-automated cleanup (reframe as a benefit or drop) from a human-must-act migration (keep, plainly framed, with a pointer to the steps). Keep the marker and command literal so both consumers match the anchor instead of free-parsing prose. The `react-router-dom` removal below is the worked example. `/update-gaia`'s confirm gate shows the full baseline-to-latest CHANGELOG range, so an adopter several versions behind sees every intervening entry.

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- the rule GAIA ships for making a guard able to fail now binds the guard itself, not only the tests written beside it. Where a change's whole deliverable is a refusal, a gate, or a staleness check, its verification enumerates the artifacts the change produced and confirms each one exists and behaves, which for a guard confirms only that it passes. Passing is the state an inert guard is permanently in, so that pass answers the wrong question and answers it green, and the greenness is what makes it convincing. The rule now names the case beside the three stages it already covered, and prescribes the step that tells a working guard from an inert one, without displacing the input-set obligations it already carried: drive the deliverable into its failing state once, deliberately, and assert the refusal on the message carrying the claim rather than on the exit status alone, with one such fixture per guard the change ships. GAIA's plan template carries the same obligation as a task-doc acceptance criterion, so a verification task has to answer the question rather than reach it by inference. Both surfaces ship, so the addition reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1731)

- the shell shape that quietly makes a script uninterruptible is now caught by a check across the whole tree, and the last instance of it is repaired. Bash resumes at the point of interruption once a trapped signal handler returns, so a single cleanup arm bound to EXIT together with INT or TERM deletes the terminating disposition it replaced: the cleanup runs, Ctrl-C is swallowed, and the script carries on as though no signal arrived. Nothing about the handler looks wrong, which is why the shape survives review. It cannot be right either, and that is what makes it checkable rather than a judgment call: the handler either returns, which is the uninterruptible case, or it exits, which gives the wrong status on the ordinary exit path the same arm also serves, so there is no third behaviour to weigh and the check needs to read only the signal list. Two instances were repaired by hand recently and each was pinned by an assertion naming one hardcoded file, which is the hand-kept list GAIA's own guard rule warns about: armed for exactly two files, unable to find a third, and no help at all for the one that was actually sitting there. That third instance was in the mutex the SPEC and plan allocators take before they touch their ledger, where the swallowed interrupt held for the whole ten-second acquisition window, so an adopter interrupting `/gaia-spec` or `/gaia-plan` while another session held the lock watched the keystroke do nothing. It now releases the lock and terminates, which is what the default disposition would have done. The two per-file pins are retired in favour of the tree-wide check, which reads every tracked shell script, the extensionless hooks, the CI workflow and composite-action bodies, the adopter workflow templates, and the bats suites, and which errors rather than reporting clean when its own discovery comes back empty. The check and its suite are maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs them; the repaired mutex ships, so the interrupt fix reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1717)

- the two rules governing how GAIA's shell is written now reach every shell script in the tree, and a check keeps it that way. Each rule binds itself to the surfaces it governs with a path list, and a list like that loads the file carrying it and nothing else, so a shell family no entry names is a family whose author is shown neither rule. Two had been sitting in that gap since the day they were created: the SPEC extension's script library, which holds a linter whose green verdict is read as evidence that the linted construct holds, and the statusline scripts, one of which documents distinct exit codes and the messages reporting them. Those are the two shapes the rules define, on scripts nobody had ever been shown them for. The deeper problem was that fixing the lists by hand fixes nothing durable, because a hand-kept list is itself the failure the first rule warns about: it goes stale silently the next time a directory appears, and the staleness surfaces only when somebody happens to audit a script written without either rule in front of them, which is how both of these were found and how the `.github/` gap before them was found. So the lists are now armed rather than merely corrected. A new check fails when any tracked shell file is reached by neither rule, and it refuses to answer at all rather than reporting clean when its own discovery comes back empty, when a rule's path list parses to nothing, or when a path list uses syntax its matcher does not model. Arming it immediately turned up a third family nobody had counted as shell at all: the husky pre-commit hook, which is hand-written shell with no extension and no shebang, and whose change-detection arms decide whether the quality gate floor runs, so its green is read as evidence in exactly the sense the first rule defines. Both lists now name it, and the check's own discovery reads the extensionless hooks alongside the extensioned scripts rather than keying on the file suffix. What was deliberately not done is derive the lists from "every shipped shell surface", which would collapse a distinction that is real: the shipped rules may only name paths an adopter's clone actually has, while the maintainer-only surfaces are named from a release-excluded pointer that carries both rules to them, and one derived list cannot be both. Arming the explicit lists gets the property the derivation was wanted for without giving up the split. The two shipped rules reach adopters on their next `/update-gaia`, so an adopter's own SPEC library and statusline are covered too; the check and its suite are maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs them (#1701)

- the paths the app submits fetchers to are now defined once, in `app/action-paths.ts`, and read by the theme switch, the optimistic-theme matcher, the language select, and the router stub that stories and tests share. React Router derives each of those paths from a route file's name, so renaming that file used to change the served path and leave every copy of it stale with nothing failing: the submission 404s, the optimistic theme update stops applying while the POST still succeeds, or a story renders the router's error boundary in place of the component. A new test resolves the app's real route config and asserts every declared path is still served, so that rename now reds instead of passing green, and the stub registers whatever the app declares rather than its own hand-kept copies. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1712)

- two of the rules GAIA ships now load on the shell scripts under `.github/`, which they had never reached. Each rule binds itself to the surfaces it governs with a path list, and neither list named that directory: the guard rule reached `.gaia/scripts/`, `.claude/hooks/`, the Playwright specs, the workflows, and every `.bats` file, and the diagnostic rule reached the first two and the `.bats` files. The scripts sitting in that gap are guards in exactly the sense the first rule defines, each one's green result read as evidence on the merge gate's own critical path, deciding whether a member is still owed a clearance, whether the audit status is present, and what a trailer at HEAD says; and they are dense with the shape the second rule names, down to one refusal message written identically for two distinct conditions and one failed query reported to the operator as a timeout. The effect was that an author editing one of those scripts got neither rule while an author editing its sibling `.bats` suite got both, which is the guidance arriving for the test and not for the thing under test. The absence was invisible from either side, since nothing in the directory named a rule and neither rule named a surface it was missing. The shipped ones among them, the audit helpers and the merge-and-watch libraries, reach an adopter clone, so it carried the same gap and now gets both rules on it; the maintainer-only rest match nothing there (#1696)

- the rule GAIA ships for making a guard able to fail now names the case that fires on a guard's very first validation run. Its list of ways a guard's own input set silently drops an element covered globs, over-reaching prunes, and manifests that do not enumerate every member, all of them visibly about the discovery step, and omitted the one that does not look like a discovery problem at all: a discovery built from `git ls-files`, or any other tracked-file listing, cannot see a file that is not yet tracked, and a brand-new guard together with its sibling suite are exactly that on the run the author uses to check whether the tree is clean. Those two files are also the ones most likely to carry the class on purpose, since a guard's header quotes its own class as worked counter-examples and its suite spells the class out in fixtures, so the first run reports clean over a set that excludes precisely the files most likely to red, and that output is indistinguishable from a real clean pass. It was observed live on a guard that reported clean and exited zero, then reported eight hits on the same working tree immediately after a `git add`, six of them in the guard's own header and two in its suite, with no content change in between. The rule now names the case beside the other three, and prescribes the cheap countermeasure next to the existing input-set obligation: make the new guard and its suite visible to the discovery before the run that validates them, by staging them where the listing reads the index and by running the discovery command on its own and confirming the new paths appear where it reads a committed ref. The rule ships, so the addition reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia`, and it applies to any adopter guard whose discovery is a tracked-file listing rather than a filesystem walk (#1692)

- the check against a bare count in a comment now reads every file type its rule governs, rather than the shell and bats half it started on. The rule binds the product and tooling TypeScript, JavaScript and CSS as well, so the requirement was enforced over part of its own scope and merely asserted over the rest, and the unread part was not empty: ten live instances were sitting in it, among them two of the kind where the number is doing real work, telling a maintainer how many entries a block should have while nothing fails when a subject is added. What kept the gap open was that widening a pathspec was never the fix. Those files comment with `//` and `/* */` rather than `#`, so reaching them takes a second reader, and a block reader has to know where a comment starts or a `/*` written inside a string is read as prose and everything after it is scanned as a comment. The reader added here takes only a comment that owns its line, which is the same rule the original half already used and which happens to answer the string question without a tokenizer for it, since the quote that opens a string sits ahead of the `/*` on that line. It reaches the JSX comment container as well, the form nearly every comment inside a component body takes, because a surface read without that one is read in name only. One predicate stands behind both readers, so the vocabulary cannot come to mean two different things. Every instance is repaired, each by naming the set instead of counting it. The check's file list is now held against the rule's own list by its suite, and the continuous-integration filter that decides whether the check runs at all was widened to match, so a change to a comment in a TypeScript file no longer passes a green check that never opened it. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs the check or its suite; the comment rule that does ship now describes the wider surface it covers (#1693)

- the rule against writing a bare count into a comment is now enforced rather than only asserted. A number naming how many of something the tree holds is a claim nothing recounts: the set moves, the sentence does not, and the figure ends up read as authoritative precisely because it is specific. One recent feature shipped four wrong ones, each found by a person rather than a check, and the repair for one of them introduced another of the same kind. The obstacle was never the plumbing, it was writing a predicate narrow enough to stay quiet and broad enough to be worth having: matching a number beside a plural noun fires in the high hundreds across this tree and a gate nobody can keep green gets switched off. The discriminator that makes the class separable turns out to be grammatical rather than numeric. A bare cardinal is ordinary English describing the shape of the argument being made in the same breath, with the enumeration right there; a **definite** one asserts a cardinality for a specific population living somewhere else in the tree, which is what moves without touching the sentence. Every instance already standing in the tree carried one, as did both of the recent four that the scanned surface could reach at all. Restricted to those, to counts above a pair, and to nouns that name something the repository contains and a reader could go and recount, the check reports a set small enough to read and act on, and every one of those was a real instance. Markdown is deliberately outside it: this tree's markdown is mostly dated, frozen records where a cardinal is a true statement about the tree as it was on the day it was written, so recounting one would falsify a record rather than repair it. Every instance the check reports is repaired, each either gone in favour of naming the set or backed by something that recounts it; the shapes it deliberately cannot read, listed in its own header, are unchanged and still rest on the reader. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs the check or its suite; the comment rule it enforces does ship, and now says which half of itself is gated and which half still rests on the reader (#1666)

- three of GAIA's shell guards now read the tree's `*.bats` suites, which they had excluded wholesale. The exclusion was safe while a suite only demonstrated a defect class on purpose, writing a deliberately broken fixture so the guard could be proven to catch it, and it stopped being safe once the same suites also carried lines they actually execute, including a shell body held in a variable and handed to `bash -c`, on a surface bats runs entirely under errexit. So each guard was blind on the one surface where its own class is written down on purpose, and blind to real instances sitting beside them. A shared awk-source library now carries the discrimination that makes reading those files safe: it tells fixture data apart from executed shell by the shape of the call that writes it rather than by any list of files, so a new suite inherits the behaviour with no registration, and it reads a suppression pragma that lets a suite keep an unrepaired spelling when the reason given says what repairing it would break. Every genuine instance the newly-scanned surface exposed is repaired, a wiki decision page records the convention, and the guards now error rather than reporting a bats file clean when they lose track of shell state part way through it. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs the guards, the library, or the suites (#1620)

- the wiki now records that Claude Code's worktree isolation has two arms which refuse different things, and which kind of session is under each. A session the runtime switches into a worktree is held to a whole-command check and refuses capturing a command's output into a variable, while an agent dispatched into that same worktree is not held to that check and runs the capture normally, which matters because that capture is exactly what GAIA's code-review member specs prescribe for the audit gate handshake. With the split unrecorded, testing a construct by hand from the first kind of session and generalizing the refusal to the specs reads a prescribed command as broken for the audience that runs it fine, a wrong conclusion already reached once. The page also records the same rule's third face on the file-editing tools, and corrects a standing misattribution of that refusal to one of GAIA's own hooks, which in fact exempts the path and allows the very call the runtime refuses. It ships, so the correction reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1659)

- GAIA's code review can now name three more root causes instead of recording them as classless. The audit's `finding_class` vocabulary is a closed list on purpose, and a reviewer that cannot map a finding onto it stamps a fallback that says, honestly, that no seeded class fits. That fallback is not a candidate for anything: the policy-memory loop behind `/gaia-harden` refuses to draft a rule for it, because a rule needs a shared root cause and the fallback asserts there is none. Over the last ninety days the majority of merged pull requests carried at least one finding that took it, spread across nearly the whole surface, which reads as a vocabulary that does not fit rather than as one missing class. Three shapes were seeded out of that residue, each recurring across enough distinct pull requests to clear the bar and each separable from its nearest neighbour on something a reviewer can read off a single finding. The first is prose claiming more than the mechanism behind it establishes, which is not the same as prose that disagrees with it: it holds for the case the writer had in front of them and fails for a sibling case the same sentence covers, so a reader over-trusts rather than acts wrongly. The second is an enumeration presenting part of a set as the whole of it, distinguished from a stale count by naming members rather than counting them. The third is work repeated once per element, or once per call site, where a single batched call returns the same result, distinguished from an absent cost ceiling by having a ceiling and a multiplier. Several shapes that recur just as often were deliberately left out, including one this project has repeatedly decided not to act on and one whose instances are all findings on prose an earlier review round wrote, and the reasons and the conditions that would reverse them are written down beside the seeding evidence. The vocabulary and the reviewers' assignment criteria both ship, so adopters get the three new classes, and an adopter's own audit can assign them, on their next `/update-gaia` (#1645)

- Claude now carries two new auto-loading rules, plus an addition to the comment rule, covering three defects GAIA's own code review kept finding and nothing prevented. The first is that a guard has to be able to fail. An assertion, a lint script, or a CI condition is trusted because it went green, and green is only evidence when red was reachable; three independent things can quietly make it unreachable, the step that builds the guard's own input set, the condition deciding which inputs reach the check, and the region the check accepts. A guard sound at two of the three still proves nothing, and the failure never announces itself as a broken guard, it surfaces later as a construct nobody was defending. The rule names all three and prescribes the one cheap obligation that covers them together: break what the guard names, watch it go red, and only then rely on it. The second is that a diagnostic names every cause that reaches it. Where several conditions land on one branch and the message admits to one of them, the operator is not merely under-informed, they are sent to a repair that cannot fix what actually failed, and the message goes on asserting the cause they already ruled out. That is worse than a terse message, which at least sends them to read the code. The third retires the bare count. A number written into a docblock, a file header, or a bats test name is a name for a set that nothing recounts, so it decays into a false claim a reader trusts precisely because it is specific; the rule prefers the enumeration beside it, and asks that a number carrying a real claim be backed by something that recounts it. All three are path-scoped and load only on the files they govern, so none of them adds weight to unrelated work. They reach adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1646)

- a check now stops GAIA shipping a container image whose Node version sits below the floor its own `package.json` declares. Four files state that version independently, the `engines.node` floor, `.node-version`, `.nvmrc`, and the `FROM node:` lines in the Dockerfile, and nothing derives any of them from another, so raising the floor is a one-file edit that leaves the other three saying whatever they said. The failure is silent in the direction that matters: nothing here builds the Dockerfile, and the install inside the image only warns about an unsatisfied engine rather than failing, so a green run is not evidence about the image an adopter deploys, and a dependency needing the newer Node passes everywhere it is exercised and fails only in the container. It has already happened once, a floor raise that left the image two patch lines behind its own declared minimum, found by someone reading the file rather than by any check. The new guard holds the other three statements to the floor, holds a Docker tag to the lowest version it can resolve to rather than to whichever one it happens to pull today, and reds rather than passes on a version spelling it cannot read, since a tag that floats defeats the pin outright. It runs inside a required check, which now re-runs whenever any of the four files moves. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it; the four files it guards do ship (#1629)

- a check now tells the author at write time when they have written a script invocation GAIA's capability oracle cannot see. The oracle decides what a hook or an allowlisted script reaches for by matching two regular-expression anchors against a line of shell, and a shape those anchors reject produces silence rather than a finding: the reporting channel sits downstream of them, so nothing says the file just written reaches for something the capability manifests do not record, and on the hook surface that manifest is the artifact an adopter reads as complete. Half of that class was already guarded, the half where repairing the grammar adds grammar; the other half is the tree growing an idiom faster than the oracle models it, which no change to the oracle causes and no suite over the oracle sees. The new check closes it by differencing two readings of the same line: bash's own answer to whether a word is in command position, taken from the fact that it expands an alias only there and only at parse time, against whatever the anchors match today. Nothing restates which shapes the anchors miss, so there is no second approximation to drift and no enumeration to rot, and a file whose probe cannot be parsed is reported rather than skipped, because a file the check cannot read is one whose blindness is unmeasured. The scanned tree is clean today; the check exists to keep it that way. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it

- the guard that checks GAIA's capability oracle has not moved what its scripts can reach now follows the oracle rather than one change to it. Its before side was a fixture holding the pre-change bodies of the functions one past change rewrote, layered over the shipped checker, so it differed from the after side only for those names: a later change to any other function landed identically on both sides, and the comparison then held two runs that both carried the change and reported green. A reach regression could merge under it with a pull request citing it as the verification, and the guard written to keep the fixture honest iterated a hardcoded list of those same names, so it stayed green in the same case. A second arm now builds its before side out of the checker and the oracle as they stood at the branch's fork point, materialized together in a directory of their own, so a branch's rewrite sits on exactly one side whichever function holds it, and the checking script is covered as well as the oracle library. It skips with its reason rather than passing when the branch changes neither file and when no fork point resolves, which is what a shallow CI checkout leaves behind, so the only green it reports is a comparison it actually ran; the run that makes it load-bearing is the local pre-merge one, which has the history and is where that comparison was being done by hand. Two arms prove it can fail, one driving the fork-point resolution against a repository whose branch tip and fork point differ and one blinding a detector on the before side, and the arm that keeps the older fixture honest now also compares that fixture's own contents against the rewritten set it is supposed to hold, so a fixture that gains or loses a function reds instead of quietly narrowing what the pin swaps. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it (#1612)

- three controls in the suite that checks GAIA's capability-oracle boundary tests now match a whole name rather than a substring of one. Each control samples a name, respells the library so one derivation should stop seeing it, and greps the derivation's output for the name; the output is one name per line, and a name can be a strict prefix of a name added later, so an unanchored grep breaks the anchor-discovery control in both directions at once. The arm asserting the wide reader still sees the sampled name goes green off a longer name that merely contains it, which is a control that has stopped controlling, and the arm asserting the narrow reader no longer sees it matches that same longer name and reds a suite that is correct. An empty needle matched every line rather than failing closed. The three sites are whole-line matches now, the sampled name gets the short-read guard its sibling arm already had, and each refusal the tightening introduces was proven against a library carrying a name that contains an existing one, rather than on the suite staying green. Latent rather than live, since the library's three boundary anchors have no prefix relationship today. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it (#1606)

- the boundary tests GAIA's capability oracle uses to decide whether a word is a command are now covered by a suite that derives what it drives from those boundaries themselves, rather than only by hand-written cases. The oracle's existing tests pin one shape per idiom this tree writes today, which is the right instrument for a decision somebody made and blind to the one nobody wrote a test for: every boundary-side defect the oracle has carried was a case nobody had written, and the class is silent by construction, because a boundary that rejects a shape produces no finding at all, only an absence a clean run cannot be told apart from. The new suite reads the boundaries out of the library, derives each one's keyword vocabulary from its own value, and asserts per keyword that the boundary admits the call it claims to, that no arm reaches inside a longer word and hands the resolver a file that was never written, that a vocabulary two boundaries disagree on is written down in the header of the one that lacks it, and that every boundary says what it misses at all. Each derivation carries its own guard against reading a short set, since a derivation returning three of four entries leaves a green suite driving less than its name claims, and each assertion carries a mutation proving it can fail. It judges behaviour against what the library says about itself, not whether a boundary's vocabulary is the right one, which is argued per detector and is not mechanically checkable. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it (#1604)

- GAIA's capability oracle no longer reads a syntax-only parse check as a call. `bash -n <path>` compiles a file and executes none of it, but the oracle skipped every flag on the way to the script operand, so a parse check was indistinguishable from the real invocation: the checking script inherited the target's entire reach, and the repair a red check invites is to widen a declaration until it goes green. Those declarations ship, so a fabricated edge ends as a published overstatement of what a hook touches. It was latent only by spelling accident, because this tree writes its parse checks in a form the oracle's command-position anchor happens not to match at all, and simplifying any one of them would have made it live with nothing to warn the author. That anchor, the widest-reaching approximation in the oracle and the only one carrying no explanation of itself, now states what it matches, that a shape it rejects produces silence rather than a diagnostic, and why widening it is not the repair for a miss it produces. The oracle is maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it; the declarations it gates do ship (#1599, #1601)

- the rule GAIA ships for dispatching a subagent now points at a contract that describes the dispatch it prescribes, and the guard's last step has one stated owner. What to do after a dispatched agent returns nothing twice was restated across several shipped prose surfaces with more than one ending, and the one surface that departs on purpose, the pre-merge audit gate, stated its different ending flatly with no reason attached. An undeclared deliberate exception is worse than plain drift: the safe-looking reading of an unexplained difference is that the agreeing surfaces are right and the odd one is stale, and acting on that turns a fail-closed merge gate into an orchestrator that reviews on a reviewer's behalf and then merges. The general ending is now stated once, as doing the work inline wherever the caller may legitimately do it, the merge gate declares its departure together with the reason for it, that a clearance is the reviewer's own attestation and nobody may write one on its behalf, and the rule's pointer to the full contract now lands on prose carrying the ad-hoc dispatch shape and the flags that shape depends on rather than on a section documenting a different set. A test holds the surfaces in step from here, deriving them from the tree so a new surface with a third ending fails rather than going unnoticed; the test is maintainer-only and release-excluded, while the prose it guards ships, so the corrections reach adopters on their next `/update-gaia`. A wikilink on the merge-workflow page that hard-wrapped across a line, and so resolved nowhere, is repaired in the same pass (#1581, #1580, #1584)

- a check now stops a shell library load that can turn a hook into a gate denying every tool call. Under errexit, sourcing a file that is present but unparseable abandons the shell at the load, and in a `PreToolUse` hook that exit is the deny code, so an interrupted update, an unresolved merge conflict, or a truncated write refuses every matching call, including the edit that would repair the library. The class had been repaired one site at a time across several rounds and kept coming back for a mechanical reason: the repair reached for was a parse check on the file being loaded, which proves nothing about what that file itself loads, so each round left a residual one source level deeper and the next round found it there. The new check works on the errexit-reachable source closure rather than on one file at a time, so a library's own loads are judged whether or not any consumer parse-checked the library, and it demands a shape that costs nothing instead of a subprocess per load. It distinguishes a file that arms errexit itself from one that inherits it, because the restore that is correct in the first is a defect in the second: a flat `set -e` inside a library arms errexit in every caller that deliberately ran without it, killing that caller at its next non-zero command. Twenty-six live sites were repaired to satisfy it, among them the hook guarding self-heal edits, the three wiki hooks, and the audit status and stamp hooks; those ship, so the repair reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia`. The check itself is maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it

- the pre-merge sweep GAIA prescribes on a shipped wiki page is now scoped by the claim rather than by the diff, and the page says when the sweep is finished. It told you to sweep the round's touched files, which is the wrong set: the sentences that go stale sit in files the diff never opened, and re-checking a hand-written list of citations confirms the entries it holds while saying nothing about the ones it never had, so it fails while reading as thorough. The page now says to grep the whole tree for the behaviour the round changed, and states the convergence test that has actually ended these loops, which is not a green gate and not an empty round: a round whose findings are all pre-existing is terminal, a round that falsifies a sentence this branch wrote is not, and the correction that repaired the previous round's false claim is itself a sentence this branch wrote. Maintainer-facing process guidance on a shipped wiki page, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia`

- the auto-loading rule for `.bats` assertions now covers whether an assertion reaches its whole obligation, not only whether it can fail. Everything it said before was about mechanics, the bash 3.2 skip and the `set -e` exemption that make an assertion structurally unable to go red, and none of it said anything about a guard that fails correctly over one element while its name claims the set. That gap is what the rule now closes: a guard over a set derives the set from the artifact that owns it and asserts per element, a mutation control may sample one element and has to say it is sampling, a derivation that comes back short is more dangerous than one that comes back empty because the non-empty guard stays satisfied, and a count written into a test name rots the next time the set changes. Maintainer-facing discipline on a shipped rule file, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia`

- a tech-debt issue handed off to `/gaia-spec` now shows whether anyone has picked the handoff up. A single `debt:spec-pending` label had to stand for the whole life of the work, from the moment `/gaia-debt` prints the handoff block until the implementation lands weeks later, so an issue nobody had touched looked exactly like one whose SPEC was being written right now. Two people could each start the same drain and author a SPEC for the same debt, neither able to see the other. A second label, `debt:spec-active`, now marks the pipeline as running: the handoff block instructs the spec session to swap it in on arrival, and both labels park the issue identically, so what the split buys is a stalled handoff you can actually spot. Abandoning a SPEC strips the label and returns the issue to the backlog rather than closing it, since a fix that was never attempted is not a debt that is gone. `/update-gaia` and `/setup-gaia` create the new label on your repository through the usual label sync (#1539)

- plans generated by `/gaia-plan` now read CI after each phase's push, instead of finding out at the merge gate. Every check the per-phase gate ran was local and selected by the paths the phase's own task doc names, so an edit that reddened a guard living somewhere that doc never mentions was invisible to the loop, and stayed invisible for every phase after it, because nothing between the push and the next dispatch read the remote result. Each phase's gate was green and each phase's record truthfully said so, which is what let a red survive roughly nine hours of execution across five phases and five pre-merge audit rounds in the run that surfaced this. Detection already existed one stage later, in the pre-merge workflow, so what changes is when the signal arrives, one phase after the cause rather than after the whole implementation is built. The read carries the same four conditions that keep the merge-gate version honest: it describes the pushed commit, pending is not green and is not a reason to hold the phase, a red can be a flake wanting a re-run rather than a fix, and a branch with no pull request open yet has nothing to read. A red is explicitly not a stop condition; it folds into the next phase's dispatch as a repair, so a flake cannot halt a multi-hour run. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1530)

- deciding whether to fold an audit Suggestion into a pull request that is already clean and already marked now weighs the risk of an unreviewed repair, not the re-dispatch price alone. The guidance priced that fold purely as a small delta review, which read as licensing any short fix into a branch whose entire review budget is already spent, where nothing reads the repair before it merges and a defect in it costs another round plus whatever it does if it ships. The cheap-fold arm is now stated for the two forms that actually carry almost none of that risk, a comment-only or prose-only repair, while a repair introducing new logic is decided on the risk question instead. Maintainer-facing process guidance on a shipped wiki page, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia`

- the two layers that validate GAIA's label registry now have their enumerations compared by an executed check rather than kept in step by hand. `.gaia/labels.schema.json`, the shipped copy an adopter can point editor or CI tooling at, and the CLI's own constants each spell the same axis, audience, and feature lists, and a one-sided edit was silent in both directions: the CLI would accept an entry the published schema rejects, or the reverse, with every test still green. A parity test now reds on drift and names which enumeration diverged. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs the test; the file it guards does ship (#1504)

- GAIA now ships a single source of truth for its GitHub labels: a machine-readable registry, a generated wiki page documenting every label, and a `gaia labels` command family (`sync`, `docs`, `check`) that creates missing labels on your repository, regenerates the page, and verifies every label referenced in code resolves to a real registry entry. `/setup-gaia` and `/update-gaia` now run the sync automatically, so your repository's labels stay current without a manual `gh label create` pass (#1436)

- Claude now claims any GitHub issue it starts work on with an `in-progress` label, not only tech-debt drains, and releases the claim automatically once the pull request that closes it merges. Two agents or two people never start the same ticket, and no dedicated command is needed to claim or release it.

- GAIA's shell gate now parses the tree with a real bash 3.2 in CI, instead of resting on a maintainer remembering to do it locally. The gate already carried a pass that reads every tracked script with the interpreter those scripts declare support for, the 3.2.57 stock macOS ships as `/bin/bash`, because shellcheck models bash 5's grammar and a construct that is a syntax error only on 3.2 clears every pass it makes. Every runner in this project's CI is Linux, though, so that pass found bash 5 and skipped on 100% of runs. It skipped loudly, which is the right posture for a contributor's local run and no posture at all for enforcement: a banner that prints on every single CI run is the shape that trains a reader to stop seeing it, not the shape that gates, and a contributor authoring on Linux could introduce a 3.2-only syntax error, clear a green check, and merge. That is the same gap that let the class ship in the first place, where an unparseable library silently emptied the audit's review scope on every macOS clone and no mechanical check ever read the tree with the interpreter in question. The workflow now carries a second job on a macOS image, the one image in this CI whose `/bin/bash` is a real 3.2, and the harness grew a pass-selection flag so that leg runs the parse pass alone: it finishes in under a second, where running the whole harness there would duplicate the Linux leg's work at ten times the runner price. The leg also asserts the image's bash version before trusting its own verdict, because silently inheriting the pass's skip would recreate the same false clean one layer up. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1490)

- a check now stops a failure-handling branch the shell can never reach. An assignment takes the exit status of its command substitution, so under `set -e` a failing command ends the script on the assignment line itself and the `rc=$?` written below it never runs, which leaves every branch reading that status dead and turns the failure into a bare non-zero exit carrying none of the diagnostics the author wrote. That is not hypothetical: two instances shipped in the merge-and-watch composite action, and one of them silently disabled the whole post-merge revert escalation, because three later steps were gated on a step outcome that could no longer be reached, so a CI failure that could not be reverted escalated to nobody. Three separate oracles were measured against the shape and none of them sees it: shellcheck at its strictest floor is silent on a capture into a variable and does not model errexit assignment status at all, actionlint does not lint composite-action step bodies, and this project's own shell lint discovers files by extension, so the `run:` bodies both instances lived in were outside every pass it made. The new check covers the project's own shell, its CI workflows and composite actions, and the workflow templates adopter CI renders from, and it treats a `run:` body as having errexit on whether or not the body says so, because that is what Actions does and it is why both instances were live. It stays quiet on the shapes that are correct: the guarded form it recommends as the repair, the `local` spelling shellcheck already owns, the deferred `trap` capture, and any ordinary script that never turns errexit on at all, of which this tree has roughly twenty carrying no defect. The git hooks are the exception and arm regardless, because husky runs each one under `sh -e` whether the hook says so or not. Where the check cannot read a file to its end it says so and fails rather than reporting it clean, and the handful of shapes it still calls wrong are enumerated in its own header with the repair tracked separately. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1483)

- every script GAIA's settings pre-approve for prompt-free execution now declares what it reaches for, and a check fails the build when a declaration and the script's real reach disagree in either direction. A pre-approval names a script and says nothing about what that script does, and it keeps saying nothing as the script changes, so the standing grant and the behavior it covers were never coupled to each other. Each such script now carries an entry in a manifest listing its outbound calls, its writes, its git and GitHub mutations, and the other scripts it invokes, counting whatever those reach for as part of the caller's own reach, since a grant on a script that sources a second one grants both. The manifest and the schema describing it ship, so an adopter can read what a granted script is allowed to do without reading the script; the reconciling check is maintainer-only and runs on GAIA's own pull requests, because it judges GAIA's scripts rather than an adopter's. Declaring more than a script actually does fails too, so an entry cannot be quietly padded to stay ahead of the code (#1429)

- every hook GAIA's settings register to fire automatically now declares what it reaches for. A hook registration is a standing, prompt-free grant, different in kind from a pre-approved script: Claude Code fires the file on its trigger event with no invocation, no prompt and no line in any transcript that names it, and the registration itself says nothing about what the hook does, then keeps saying nothing as the hook changes. Each registered hook now carries an entry declaring its network calls, its writes, its git and GitHub mutations and the other scripts it invokes, counting whatever those reach for as part of the hook's own reach, and a check holds the declaration to the code in both directions, so an omission fails and so does a padded declaration. The manifest and its schema ship, so an adopter can read what a registered hook reaches for without reading the hook; the check itself is maintainer-only and runs on GAIA's own pull requests, because it judges GAIA's hooks rather than an adopter's. That leaves a record rather than a verifier: nothing on an adopter clone re-checks it, nothing covers hooks an adopter registers themselves, and because the settings file and the manifest update on different policies, an adopter who customizes their hooks block can end up with the two disagreeing. What this buys is detection at review time, not prevention: nothing sits between a trigger event and the hook that fires on it (#1527)

- the family of test helpers that read a step out of the audit CI workflow is now a declared roster the build enforces, instead of a set recovered by hand on every read. Several test files each carry their own near-copy of a helper that pulls one step's shell body out of the workflow, bats shares no functions across files, and the copies are kept in agreement by hand. Which files were in that set was written down as a `git grep` to re-run, keyed on how the copies happen to spell their parser today, and nothing checked that the grep's answer was the real set. That is not a hypothetical decay: three successive review rounds each recovered one more live member by re-tuning the search text, and each round's version was believed complete when it was written. A copy spelled any other way was invisible to the search and to CI alike, so the next maintainer to change what "extract the step body" means would update the copies the search found and leave the rest silently diverged, which is the hand-kept list the search replaced. The set is now enumerated from the two things a copy cannot read the workflow without, naming it and finding where one step ends, and compared against a roster that records every member and, for each near-miss the enumeration also catches, the reason it is out. An unregistered copy fails the build, and so does a roster entry for a file that no longer reads the workflow, since a list that only decays in one direction rots back into the problem. The criterion is stated once in the check rather than restated in each copy's comments, and it is deliberately not complete against every conceivable spelling, which the check says plainly instead of implying a guarantee it cannot make. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1464)

- the checks that read the whole tree now run as one named command, instead of whichever of them a currently-loaded rule happened to mention. Pre-merge self-verification picks its checks by the paths a change touches, and a checker whose input is the whole directory has no path that can select it, so which ones actually ran depended on which rule was loaded, and rule activation is itself path-scoped: structurally the wrong shape for the job. The cost of missing one is a full audit round, because CI finds it after the push and the repair commit rotates every dispatched reviewer's anchor and buys a re-read from each. Seventeen whole-tree checks now run together in about a minute, with each candidate's membership decided from its own header rather than its name, every deliberate non-member recorded alongside the reason it is out, and a guard that fails when a newly added checker joins neither list, since a hand-kept roster would otherwise rot into the same gap it closes. No new check is introduced here; the existing ones simply become findable by someone who does not already know they exist. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1457)

- proving a new guard can fail now takes a mutant per outcome rather than one per mechanism. The old criterion asked for one mutant per mechanism added, which a conditional with three reachable outcomes satisfies while two of them stay untested. What makes that reliably wrong rather than occasionally wrong is which mutant an author reaches for: it is the failure they were already imagining, which is the direction they just defended against, so the direction they did not consider is both the untested one and the likelier one to break later. The guidance now asks, for each outcome the new logic can produce, for a mutant to each of the others, which for a predicate means both return values plus the fall-through wherever all three are reachable, and it names that bias as a third trap beside the two already listed. Maintainer-facing process guidance on a shipped wiki page, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1458)

- audit members dispatched together no longer risk overwriting each other's evidence when one of them needs to prove a guard is real. Establishing that a check is not hollow means breaking the thing it watches and confirming it goes red, which needs real files on disk, while every member is required to hand the reviewed tree back untouched. Those two facts together force each member onto a scratch copy, and nothing said where that copy goes, so members invented names inside the one workspace a parallel dispatch shares and the names collided. The damage was silent in both directions: a clean copy landing over a mutated one makes the mutation look like it never tripped the check, which reads as a defect that is not there, and a mutated copy landing over a clean one reds a check that has nothing to do with the code under review. Because this is the evidence that decides whether a finding is real, a collided run misinforms the merge gate specifically. Members now take a private working copy from a helper that names it after both the audit and the member, so two members in one wave can never resolve the same directory, and each releases its copy when it finishes; whatever an interrupted run leaves behind is age-swept (#1447)

- a filer can now record that a tech-debt fix should ride another change rather than be drained on its own, with a `fold:required` label the drain shows you at the moment you pick what to work on. Some repairs are a sentence of work sitting behind a cost the repair itself does not justify: editing a file the audit gate treats as global discards every reviewer's incremental anchor across the whole repository, so that sentence is nearly free if it rides a change already paying the price and expensive if it does not. Filers were already writing exactly that instruction into issue bodies, in prose, and it reached nobody: the backlog ordering and the related-issue clustering are deliberately model-free, so they read fields and skip prose, and the instruction was honored only when a human happened to notice it. The new label is display-only and gates nothing. It shows up in `/gaia-debt list`, in `why`, and in the description of that issue's option when the drain asks which item to take, so the intent reaches the person making the choice. It could not have been a clustering rule instead: which change should carry the repair depends on what that other change touches, which nothing in the folded issue's own fields can know. Nothing is filtered, parked, or reordered, and an issue carrying the label drains normally when you pick it (#1424)

- work handed to a subagent no longer disappears silently when its reply never arrives. An agent asked to report its findings in its reply has exactly one delivery channel, and when that channel drops, the caller is left holding nothing, which reads exactly like an agent that found nothing worth reporting. That is the one conclusion a caller must not draw, and the failure is biased toward drawing it: dispatch three agents and lose all three and you notice, dispatch eight and lose one and you do not, so the loss that stays invisible is the one that quietly changes the answer. GAIA's spec and plan flows already avoided this by having each agent write its result to a file and return only a short digest, but that contract was written into those two flows as step-by-step instructions rather than stated anywhere as a general convention, so a dispatch composed in the moment inherited none of it. A short rule now states the convention, and the no-op detector gained a general-purpose check the rule points at, so a caller verifies the artifact on disk instead of trusting the reply. The check accepts a count the caller already knows, because a truncated write parses cleanly and reads as a complete result otherwise, which recreates the same ambiguity one level down inside a file that does exist. Because the artifact is on disk, a caller can also read it on its own schedule rather than waiting on a completion signal that may never come (#1409)

- the finding-class vocabulary now names root causes that recur on any codebase, an over-broad assertion, prose that disagrees with the code it describes, a stale count, a guard armed too narrowly, a scan that misses its own input, a diagnostic that names only one cause of several, a pointer to something that is not in the tree, one construct copied to several places with nothing keeping them in step, code that resolves what it acts on from wherever it happens to be running rather than from what it was handed, overlapping runs colliding on one shared file or key, and a call with no ceiling on what it can cost, so findings of those shapes become countable and actionable instead of landing in an unclassified pile. The workflow-security classes the schema already carried are now assignable, because the audit member that owns them names them. The unclassified recurrence nudge can now be discharged: recording it silences it until fresh evidence raises the count, so closing the vocabulary gap stops the nagging instead of prolonging it. **Action required:** if you have customized `.claude/agents/code-audit-frontend.md`, `/update-gaia` writes the drifted file as a `.gaia-merge` patch and skips it rather than overwriting your customization, so your clone keeps a ten-member holistic list against a bundle that now carries twenty-one; apply that patch so your member's vocabulary mirror matches the bundle it assigns against (#1415, #1575)

- a check now stops a regex escape that means two different things depending on which machine runs it. POSIX leaves a backslash before a letter undefined in an extended regular expression, and the two `grep` implementations this project runs on resolved that freedom differently: BSD `grep` on macOS expands `\r`, `\t`, and `\d` into a carriage return, a tab, and a digit, while GNU `grep` on the CI runner matches the bare letter instead. A pattern authored and verified on a laptop therefore inverts on the runner, and it inverts in the worst available direction, toward a check that quietly passes having matched nothing. That is not hypothetical: it was the most serious finding of the round that prompted this, where a `\r?` written to tolerate Windows line endings silently stopped matching what it was written for, and three green deterministic checks ran over that code without one of them seeing it. The new check flags every backslash-escaped letter in an extended-regex `grep` pattern across the project's own shell, its CI workflows and composite actions, and the workflow templates adopter CI renders from, and it names the portable spellings to use instead. It allows the six escapes both implementations were measured to read identically, so the existing call sites relying on those are untouched, and it demands a rewrite for everything else, including the letters the two happen to agree on today, because that agreement is a coincidence of two implementations rather than a promise either one makes. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1413)

- filing a tech-debt issue now checks its own metadata before the issue is created, and stops rather than filing something a drainer will trip over weeks later. Every rule the check enforces was already written down, and every one of them was being broken anyway, because a label set is the one part of a filing nothing reads back: an issue filed with no severity label silently sorts into the lowest band. The check verifies the label vocabulary and counts, the dedup key's shape, and that no label belonging to a later stage of the issue's life has been applied at filing time. It runs offline, so it costs one local call and cannot fail for a reason outside the filing. It also has advisory modes that audit one issue or sweep your whole open backlog, and those change nothing on their own, since repairing an existing issue is a decision per issue. What it deliberately does not check is whether a difficulty grade was chosen honestly: that is a judgment about code, so the mechanical half is enforced and the rubric half stays yours. Because the check sits in front of every filing, it is pre-approved in the settings your clone merges on update, so it runs without interrupting the flow to ask; it reads one local file and a label string, makes no network call, and writes nothing

- tech-debt issues on GAIA's own repository now carry a label recording who can observe the defect, `surface:adopter` or `surface:maintainer`, alongside the severity and difficulty labels. That axis classifies a defect against GAIA's own distribution boundary, so it is maintainer-only and no longer reaches your clone: the question it asks has no answer on an adopter project, which would have left you with a mandatory field whose value never varies and that nothing reads. Your filings carry severity and a handler class, plus a difficulty grade when the filer read the cited code, and the filing check requires exactly those (#1437)

- the CI workflows GAIA generates for your repository now pass every GitHub-supplied value into their scripts as data rather than pasting it in as text. Actions substitutes a `${{ }}` expression into a `run:` block before bash ever parses it, so a value carrying a quote or a backtick is read as shell syntax; binding it on the step through an `env:` block leaves bash seeing only a variable reference. Nine expressions across the update-deps, stale-branches, and pnpm-audit workflows and the shared auto-merge step were converted. Nothing exploitable was closed: every value involved is one GitHub itself supplies, the repository name or the default branch. What was wrong is that the safety rested on an unwritten promise about each of those values, which nothing enforced and which your clone inherited along with the workflows, one distribution hop past anywhere this project's own review could see it. The existing check for this pattern, until now limited to GAIA's own CI, covers the templates your workflows render from as well, so a later edit cannot reintroduce the shape. Your rendered workflows pick this up on your next `/setup-gaia --reconfigure`; what they do is otherwise unchanged (#1388)

- a check now stops a workflow value from being pasted into a CI script as code rather than passed as data. GitHub Actions substitutes a `${{ }}` expression into a `run:` block's text before bash ever parses it, so a value carrying a quote, a backtick, or a newline is read as shell syntax; binding it on the step through an `env:` block instead removes the hazard entirely, because bash then only ever sees a variable reference. Twenty-two expressions across three workflows were converted. Nothing exploitable was closed: every value involved was either a path the workflow's own scripts produced or a value GitHub itself supplies. What was wrong is that the safety depended on an unwritten promise about whatever produced each value, that it would never emit a shell metacharacter, which nothing enforced and which every later edit to those producers silently inherited. The new check demands the `env:` form uniformly rather than keeping a list of values judged safe, because such a list restores exactly the case-by-case judgement whose absence of enforcement was the defect, and it locates `run:` bodies structurally so the very form it prescribes is not itself flagged. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1287)

- the pre-merge workflow now reads the whole CI check list it was already fetching, instead of keeping one row and discarding the rest. The step that decides whether the audit has run pulls every check on the pull request and then greps away everything except the audit's own row, so a red check sitting beside it is thrown away unread. That red gets found later, typically after an audit round has already been spent, and repairing it moves the branch, which invalidates the clearance just earned and buys another round. This is not a new step; it is the answer already in hand. The pre-dispatch verification now says to read all of it and fold any red into the same repair batch as everything else found before dispatch, and the same reading is bound to every re-spawn between rounds, which is where it pays most, since each repair commit can red something new while the next round is already being paid for. The guidance carries the four conditions that keep the reading honest: the list describes the pushed commit rather than the tree about to be audited, pending is not a reason to hold the round, a red can be a flake wanting a re-run rather than a fix, and a branch with no pull request has nothing to read and is not an error. Maintainer-facing process guidance on a shipped wiki page, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1431)

- the pre-merge self-verification step now covers the prose a comment carries, not just the mechanisms a change adds. A comment asserting something about the repository, that a pathspec covers every surface or that no other caller does a thing, is a question with an answer, and answering it costs seconds against the price of an audit round that finds it wrong. The guidance now says to run that query or delete the sentence, to give a corrected comment the same scrutiny as the one it replaces, and to prefer a test over prose wherever the claim is about what a guard catches. Maintainer-facing process guidance on a shipped wiki page, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1385)

- adding a GAIA hook now states what else that obligates, instead of leaving it to be discovered from a red CI job. Every `.sh` under `.claude/hooks/**` needs a matching entry in `.gaia/hook-scopes.json`, and a library under `.claude/hooks/lib/**` additionally needs a tier in the audit reset-predicate partition, but neither requirement appeared anywhere an author reads: both lived only inside the checkers that enforce them, which belong to no quality gate and to no agent's oracle set. A path-scoped rule now names both obligations, and the command that verifies each, at edit time rather than after the push. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1377)

- the pre-merge fix loop now says to land a whole audit round's fixes in one commit rather than one commit per finding. Each commit rotates the reporting member's content digest and buys a re-dispatch to re-earn its marker, so a round repaired finding-by-finding pays for as many re-audits as the round had findings and clears no more than the single batched commit does. The page already named this failure mode and licensed accepting a finding instead of folding it in, but said nothing about how many commits a round takes, which is the part that was costing the rounds. Maintainer-facing process guidance on a shipped wiki page, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1356)

- a test suite that nothing ran now runs in CI, and a suite in that position can no longer hide. Twelve assertions pinning the countable-findings contract across the five audit member definitions, a wiki concept page, and three CLI sources sat in a directory the shard runner's six-directory seam does not reach, so no workflow executed them and a regression in that contract would have merged green. The suite moves in beside its sibling prose pins, where an existing shard picks it up with no seam change, and the wiki page it reads joins the workflow's changed-paths filter so an edit to that page alone re-runs it instead of skipping every test step. The gap was invisible by construction: the sharder's own partition checks compare its shards against a re-listing of the same six directories, so a suite outside all six is absent from both sides of that comparison and every assertion passes while the file runs nowhere. A new check now compares the shards against the repository's own list of every tracked suite, and any suite no shard resolves must name the runner that does execute it, with that runner's continued existence checked rather than trusted. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1345)

- seven Claude Code hooks now have behavioral test coverage, two of them guards whose whole job is to deny a forbidden edit. A hook of this shape fails silently in the one direction that matters: a logic slip leaves it exiting zero, printing nothing, and letting the write it exists to block simply succeed, so a dead guard looks exactly like a guard with nothing to do and can stay dead indefinitely. The lockfile-edit block, the vitest-globals tsconfig block, and five advisory or session hooks now each have a suite that drives the real hook with a crafted payload and asserts both that it fires and that it correctly abstains, and every suite was first checked against a deliberately broken copy of its hook, so the coverage is demonstrated rather than assumed. No hook behavior changes here; this is the proof that the existing behavior is still there. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1315)

- the release leak check now runs on every pull request, rather than only when the change happens to touch one of the distribution harness's declared inputs. That harness validates the entire shipped bundle, but it was gated behind a nine-path filter, so a pull request changing any other file that ships resolved the gate false, skipped the harness, and reported green having validated nothing; the leak then surfaced at the release tag instead, where it is far more expensive to fix. The gap was live rather than latent: a comment naming a maintainer-only path, added to a workflow file that ships, did exactly this, and was caught only because the same pull request happened to also touch the harness's own workflow. Closing it meant deciding whether an expensive suite runs on nearly every pull request, and the answer taken here is to split the cheap half out. Staging the tree and running the leak checks over it is about a second of work, so it gets its own job with no path filter at all: the only allowlist that honestly describes what it reads is every file the release does not exclude, and the complement of that is a denylist shape this repo rejects in two separate checks. The seventeen-scenario suite, which is the expensive part, keeps its narrow filter. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1314)

- a pre-merge check now asserts that a step gated on a changed-paths filter can actually see its own inputs, closing a class of false green that had recurred seven times and been caught by hand every time. Such a step runs only when one of its filter's globs matched the pull request, so if the step reads a file the filter does not list, a pull request touching only that file skips the step and the job reports green having run nothing. On a required check that green is what branch protection reads, and it is indistinguishable from the outside from the green a genuinely irrelevant pull request earns, which is why every instance so far was found by reading rather than by failing. The check compares the two sets for every gated step, and it found live instances on its first run: the shared setup action that three required checks invoke was listed in none of their filters, and neither were the runtime pin and install settings that action reads, so a change to how those checks install and build would have merged with every one of them green having exercised it zero times. Those filters are widened here, as is the fourth required check's hand-rolled equivalent. The check is deliberately a floor rather than a complete accounting, since a shell body can read anything: paths reached only through a script a step invokes, paths assembled at runtime, and the bodies of composite actions stay out of scope, and a workflow that computes its own changed-paths verdict instead of declaring globs is exempt by name so that a second one cannot be introduced silently. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1313)

- the release build now catches a dangling workflow reference inside a shipped composite action. The check that fails the build when a shipped file names a maintainer-only workflow an adopter never receives scanned the workflow directory but not the composite actions those workflows invoke, even though the sibling path check beside it already treats that directory as a shipped surface. A composite action's description naming such a workflow therefore reached adopters as a pointer to a file their clone does not have, and nothing failed. Nothing in the tree names one today, so this closes a latent gap rather than fixing a live leak; the check runs inside `gaia-maintainer release scrub` and is release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1306)

- the pre-merge Code Audit Team now has a completeness check, so a file no auditor owns can no longer merge unnoticed. The roster maps file globs to auditor members and the merge gate requires a clearance from every member a change dispatches, but nothing asked the inverse question: which paths dispatch nobody. Such a path resolved an empty member set, which the gate reads as "nobody is owed a clearance", so it merged having been reviewed by no one, and it did so invisibly, because an empty set is equally the right answer for a change genuinely outside every member's remit. Every tracked file must now either resolve an owner or be declared in a new `unowned:` list beside the roster, and that list is checked rather than trusted: an entry reaching a path some member already owns is rejected, as is one no unowned path needs, so a blanket exemption cannot pass and the list cannot quietly accumulate. The Playwright suite and the root tooling that decides what runs (registry config, the pre-commit format and lint config, the container build, the Node version pin, the environment sample) now belong to the default member, so a pull request touching them needs its clearance where it previously needed none. Running the checker by hand on your own clone additionally reports GAIA's own machinery as unowned, which is accurate there rather than a fault, and you clear it by adding `.gaia/**` and `.claude/**` to your `unowned:` list alongside whatever else your tree legitimately leaves unowned (#1245)

- a wiki landing now leaves your local base branch up to date instead of quietly stale. The landing opens its pull request and queues auto-merge, but the merge gate routinely outlasts any wait that fits in a single call, so the throwaway branch and the base branch it was cut from would sit behind until someone noticed and fixed them by hand. `/gaia-wiki` now waits in-session for the merge and finishes the local catch-up when it lands, capped by `GAIA_WIKI_AWAIT_CEILING_SECONDS`; setting that to `0` disables the wait entirely. Independently of the wait, the session-start janitor makes one bounded `git fetch --prune` of your own configured `origin`, and only when a local wiki-sync branch is actually present, so an ordinary session makes no network call at all; it is bounded at 5 seconds by default, rate-limited between sessions, and `GAIA_WIKI_FETCH_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=0` turns it off. It then fast-forwards the base branch with `--ff-only` when you are on base with a clean working tree, which can only advance base to a commit the remote already has, and it reports the refusal rather than skipping silently when it cannot, so an outstanding catch-up is something you are told about instead of something you discover

- the 7-day release-age window now states outright that a version breaching it has to be decided rather than admitted, instead of leaving that to a pnpm default. Both workspace files set `minimumReleaseAgeStrict: true`, and the drift guard already watching the window asserts it on both sides, so weakening it takes a deliberate edit a reviewer sees rather than a one-character one. Installs behave exactly as before on pnpm 11, which already infers strict enforcement wherever a release-age window is set; this pins that behaviour where the policy is written rather than leaving it resting on a default that could move. The setting is also now one `/update-gaia` carries, so it reaches existing adopters on their next update. Note the flag is pnpm 11 only, and pnpm 10 does not need it: the silent-admission behaviour it closes arrived with pnpm 11, so on pnpm 10 a breach of the window already fails the install and the setting is simply inert (#1208)

- the pre-merge Code Audit Team now reviews the CLI's own test and lint tool configs. No member's file ownership reached `.gaia/cli/vitest.config.ts`, `.gaia/cli/eslint.config.mjs`, or `.gaia/cli/prettier.config.mjs`, so a pull request whose only change was one of them dispatched no auditor, required no clearance, and merged with nobody responsible for it. The vitest config is the sharpest of the three, since the setup files it names run on every CLI test run. The Node member takes all three, alongside the rest of the CLI's build and config surface it already owns; the repository-root config files are unaffected and stay with the default member. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1244)

- the pre-merge Code Audit Team now reviews the CLI workspace's supply-chain policy. No member's file ownership reached `.gaia/cli/pnpm-workspace.yaml`, so a pull request whose only change lowered the minimum release age, dropped the trust policy, or added an exclusion entry dispatched no auditor, required no clearance, and merged with nobody responsible for it. The Node member takes it, alongside the rest of that workspace's dependency surface it already owns; the root workspace file is unaffected and stays with the default member. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1207)

- the pre-merge audit guidance now names the mutation-restore hazard and asks for a stopping rule before each round. Proving a guard means mutating its logic and putting it back, and the restore discards every uncommitted change to that file rather than only the mutation, so work unrelated to the guard disappears with no diagnostic; the guidance now says to commit first or mutate in a scratch worktree, which is what the audit members already do. It also resolves a tension the page carried: the fix loop said to re-audit until clean while the cost guidance beside it licensed accepting a finding instead, with nothing saying how to choose. That choice now has to be written down before the round runs and has to name an outcome for every way the round can come back, including a new defect in the logic the change itself authored, since deciding once a finding is already on the table reliably answers "one more round". Maintainer-facing process guidance on a shipped wiki page, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1226)

- a local rate card for models GAIA has not shipped a price for yet. When Anthropic releases a model, GAIA's cost figures price that model's share at zero until a GAIA release ships its rate, and the shipped rate file is one `/update-gaia` overwrites, so editing it either gets clobbered or turns into a conflict on every release afterwards. You can now add rates to `.gaia/local/token-rates.local.json` instead: it is machine-local, it survives every update untouched, and it merges over the shipped rates per model rather than replacing them. When a model has no rate, the cost readout now prints the exact file and the entry to paste. `/gaia-fitness` gained a cost-rate check that finds such models and offers to write the entry for you, always after showing you the rate it looked up and asking, because a wrong price is worse than a missing one. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1089)

- maintainer-only measurement of Code Audit Team re-spawns. The spawn oracle now records why each member is dispatched, and a reporting command turns the accumulated records into a count of clearances lost to a peer's merge rather than to a real content change, so the decision to re-key the marker can be made on evidence instead of anecdote.
- a filed tech-debt issue now records the work that surfaced it, so a triager reading the backlog months later can tell which branch was under review, what that work was doing, and whether the cited file was one the reviewed change had already touched. The record is an HTML comment, so nothing changes in what a human reads on the issue, and it is diagnostic only: nothing matches on it, nothing gates on it, and a filing is never blocked because a field could not be determined. There is no rollout step for you here: tech-debt filing and this record arrive in your clone together, so provenance is writing from your very first filing and there is no earlier backlog to tell apart from a failed write.
