Note that this changes the audit agent definitions, which invalidates any clearance already earned on an open pull request; re-run its audit members before merging.
- the sandbox conformance suite now runs in CI. Nothing executed that tree of tests, so a failing assertion in it blocked no merge and could sit on the main branch indefinitely, which is how a stale assertion about a permission rule survived there until someone opened the file for an unrelated reason. It runs behind its own path filter rather than joining the existing one, because one of its checks reads every documentation and configuration file in the repository: as a separate filter, arming it costs a test step instead of two dependency installs and the concurrency meter. Two checks self-skip by design, the one needing a sandbox-capable machine and a documentation-link reachability check that would otherwise let a third-party outage block every merge in the repository, and the tests README now states for each tree whether CI gates it or a human runs it. One assertion was removed rather than wired in: it required history a CI checkout does not fetch, asserted something untrue of the repository, and contradicted another required check that instructs you to do the very thing it forbade. Also fixes the suite runner's fallback installer, which named a package that does not exist and so failed instead of falling back. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1079)
- Serena's `activate_project` call is now guarded against silently activating a different checkout of the same clone. Working in a linked worktree while Serena is registered without automatic per-directory activation, a bare project name resolves through Serena's own global registry to the main checkout with no error, so every symbol lookup afterward answers from the wrong tree's files; the guard now refuses that activation and names the worktree's own path to use instead.
- the pre-merge Code Audit Team now reviews the husky commit hooks. No member's globs matched `.husky/**`, so a pull request changing `.husky/pre-commit` alongside any other owned surface dispatched auditors for the other files only and the hook itself merged with nobody responsible for it, even though it is the Quality Gate floor for every commit and a change to it can silently disable typecheck, lint, and tests for a whole class of commits. The shell member takes it: the hooks are extensionless POSIX shell that no `*.sh` glob reaches, it already holds the shellcheck oracle, and it is advisory-only, so it can never repair the very gate that would catch its own bad repair. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1008)
- the pre-merge spawn oracle now warns when the branch has fallen behind `origin/main`. A branch that drifts behind main during a long run audits clean, and the rebase it then needs in order to merge pulls main's changes into files the audit roster owns, rotating those members' content digests and invalidating every marker the audit just earned, so the whole round is spent again. The oracle already runs immediately before every dispatch wave, so it now names the commit count and advises rebasing first; it never blocks, never changes the member set it emits, and does no network fetch, so a deliberate audit of an older tree still works and the oracle stays offline-safe. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1010)
- the PR Merge Workflow now tells the operator to verify an audit Suggestion's technical claims before applying it. The existing guidance prices the re-audit a fix costs but assumes the Suggestion is right, and a member can assert a mechanism it inferred rather than verified; implementing such a claim verbatim turns a reviewer's error into documented guidance that reads as reviewed. The content digest still forces a re-review of the applied fix, so this buys one round rather than closing a hole (#986)
- passing your own deterministic checks is now stated as the entry condition for dispatching the pre-merge audit team, rather than something the audit discovers for you. Every dispatched member costs real time and budget, so a defect you could have caught yourself spends the round that should have gone to what you cannot see, and two or three rounds spent that way is the ordinary failure. The guidance now names what to run first, and corrects what proving a new guard takes: breaking the thing a guard watches only proves the fixture, while mutating the guard's own logic is what proves the assertion, and a change that adds two mechanisms needs both mutated separately or the second one ships unproven (#1030)
- each Code Audit Team member's declared file ownership is now generated from `.gaia/audit-ci.yml` instead of hand-transcribed into its agent definition, so the two can no longer drift. The roster check fails whenever a member's generated remit disagrees with the roster entry that dispatches it, and every such failure names the one command that repairs it, `bash .gaia/scripts/write-audit-remits.sh`. Both the check and the writer are run by hand on an adopter clone (#978)
- GAIA now documents how to assert that a page load produced no errors, on the `wiki/dependencies/Playwright.md` page, with the Playwright conventions rule pointing at it. React splits its error reporting across two channels: an attribute mismatch arrives as a direct `console.error`, while a throw-path failure goes through `window.reportError`, which Playwright delivers as `pageerror` and never as a console message, so a spec watching one channel silently guards nothing. The guidance covers collecting both and asserting on their union, resetting the collector before the load under assertion and proving that load did not self-heal so a cold dev server's recovery cannot fail the test on noise it produced, and origin-scoping the collector rather than weakening the assertion when third-party scripts are in play. `.playwright/e2e/hydration.spec.ts` ships as the worked example (#975, #977, #983)
- the pre-merge Code Audit Team and the comprehensive health check no longer regenerate the debt backlog they exist to drain. When the audit reviews a fix to its own gate machinery, an out-of-scope finding about that machinery can now take a new `machinery_waived` disposition, recorded and listed in the PR body but not filed, instead of opening a tech-debt issue the next machinery PR re-surfaces; a deterministic path-is-machinery abuse-check, enforced by both the merge gate and the disposition backstop, keeps the waive from becoming an escape hatch for ordinary findings. The comprehensive health check now offers only the top findings by consequence for filing and defers the tail rather than restocking the backlog wholesale, and the PR Merge Workflow now documents when applying the audit's own Suggestions in the same PR is worth the re-audit it costs. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#964)
- the release build now fails when a shipped wiki page names a release-excluded wiki page's title in bare prose, a Title-Case reference that is not a `[[wikilink]]`, a backticked path, a fenced code block, or a stripped maintainer-only block, closing the last dangling-reference gap the wikilink, path, and dead-path checks each miss. Such a reference would otherwise reach an adopter as a dangling pointer to a page their clone never received; the new check runs inside `gaia-maintainer release scrub` and is release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#911)
- the release build now fails fast on two silent staleness classes it previously shipped. A `wiki/hot.md`/`wiki/log.md` left unrendered is caught by a new read-only `release scrub-wiki --check` that compares the committed files against a fresh render (dates normalized out) before the tarball builds; and a committed `.gaia/cli/templates/` file drifted from its `src/` source — invisible to the binary-bytes check because templates resolve at runtime, not through the bundle — is caught by extending the rebuild-freshness step to regenerate and `diff -rq` the templates. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#900, #901)
- the distribution test harness now runs on pull requests, not only at release. A change to `.gaia/cli/**`, `.gaia/release-exclude`, or `.gaia/release-scrub.yml` that breaks the adopter bundle (a leak, a marker-strip regression, a manifest drift) previously shipped through PR review and surfaced only at tag-push, where it is far more expensive to fix; a new path-filtered `cli-tests.yml` job runs the harness Layers 0+1 plus the no-Docker adopter-flow scenarios on every such PR. Bundled-binary coverage is added for the three highest-blast-radius subcommands (`update`, `update-deps`, `ping`), and a new scenario drives the marker-bearing `file-tech-debt` skill against a bundle-scrubbed staging tree to catch prose that loses a load-bearing step when its maintainer-only block is stripped. Maintainer-only and release-excluded (`.gaia/tests/` and the CI job never reach an adopter), so no adopter clone is affected (#899, #902, #903)
- the conventional-commit grammar and the permitted commit-type vocabulary are now declared once and read by everything that classifies a commit subject: the release bump, the CHANGELOG draft, and the wiki sync's first-pass filter. Three private copies of the same regex had already drifted apart, and the vocabulary they implied was never written down anywhere, so a commit type in steady use could contribute nothing to a version bump and be invisible to the wiki filter while every subject in the range was correctly formatted. `debt` and `build` commits now count toward a release, and `wiki` sync commits deliberately do not. Adding a type is now a compile error until the release, changelog, and wiki behavior for it are each decided. Squash-merge PR titles, which become the subject on `main` and are therefore what all three consumers read, can be validated against the same list with `gaia ci-check-subject` (#946, #947, #937)
- the wiki sync's commit filter now reports its own health, and its path vocabulary is configurable per repo via `gaia.wikiClassify` in `package.json` (defaulting to today's behavior, so nothing changes without opting in). The filter exists to keep the expensive per-commit read pass small, but when its rules stop matching the subjects or the layout a repo actually uses, it keeps emitting a plausible reason for every commit while quietly promoting all of them to the expensive pass, which is how one sync came to deep-read an entire backlog. Each run now reports what share of commits reached a fail-open default and warns when that share means the rules have gone inert (#945, #936, #948)

- tech-debt issues now carry a `difficulty:easy|medium|hard` label beside their severity when the cited code is read at filing time, graded from that code by whoever files it. The rubric grades the fix rather than the model or tooling that would perform it, asking how many design decisions the fix carries and whether the surrounding code answers them, which is the dimension the existing handler class does not capture: it grades how far a change reaches, not how much design it needs. `/gaia-debt`'s `list` and `why` report the grade, so picking what to drain no longer means opening each issue to learn whether it is mechanical or genuinely undecided. Nothing requires a grade to be present: a filing made without reading the cited code (an operator's yes on a written report, a relayed hand-off) carries none and orders, clusters, and drains exactly as a graded one does (#930, #967)
- The Deliberate Configuration Asymmetries decision page now also records the seeded `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS` flag, which GAIA ships enabled to every clone because its core workflows are multi-agent fan-outs. The entry states the dependency honestly rather than overclaiming it: no shipped surface calls an agent-teams-specific API today, so the flag is recorded as retained capability rather than as a current dependency, which is what stops a repeat audit from re-filing it as accidental drift (#920)
- A maintainer-only local pre-flight that catches an unanswered distribution question before it costs a CI round trip. Adding a file that would newly ship to adopters without a `.gaia/manifest.json` answer fails the Distribution Audit CI job, which can only speak after a push, a pull request, and a runner boot; a new `PreToolUse` hook now runs the same check locally and denies `gh pr create` when the branch's changed files intersect the manifest's unanswered set, naming the paths and pointing at `/distribution-audit`. It mirrors the CI job deliberately and fails open on every uncertainty, so CI stays the authority and the hook is only ever a cheaper way to learn the same answer. Withheld from adopters, who have no release manifest to answer, and registered per-machine rather than in the committed settings, so each maintainer clone opts in locally until the release scrub can strip a single hook registration (#916)
- New wiki decision page, Deliberate Configuration Asymmetries, recording three configuration choices that differ from their siblings on purpose (#913)
- an abandoned plan's local working folder now reaps on the same 30-day clock as a merged one, closing the last status/lifecycle asymmetry between the plan and SPEC ledgers. A `.gaia/local/plans/PLAN-NNN/` folder whose ledger row is `abandoned` had no counterpart to the merged-folder age reap; a new sibling script, wired into the session-start janitor's sweep, reaps it once past `GAIA_SPEC_RETENTION_DAYS` and cost-represented, the same fail-closed gates the merged path uses, mirroring #889 on the plan side. No shipped path stamps a plan row `abandoned` today, so the sweep currently has no candidates to act on (#895)
- an abandoned SPEC's local working folder now reaps on the same 30-day clock as a merged one. A `.gaia/local/specs/<SPEC-ID>/` folder whose ledger row is `abandoned` (a falsified premise, a change that shipped the same intent elsewhere) previously had no counterpart to the merged-folder age reap and sat forever; a new sibling script, wired into the session-start janitor and the `/gaia-spec` pre-flight sweep, reaps it once past `GAIA_SPEC_RETENTION_DAYS` and cost-represented, the same fail-closed gates the merged path uses (#889)

- `/gaia-spec`'s pre-flight now detects when a draft SPEC is being authored live in another terminal and reframes the resume prompt accordingly: it recommends **Start new** and moves the unsafe Resume/Discard behind explicit, warned overrides, instead of offering a last-writer-wins Resume that could clobber, or a Discard that could delete, the live session's shared draft. Liveness is decided by a live-process check on a recorded session-host process (never a file mtime), so a session parked on a Socratic question still reads live. The lock is advisory and fail-open: any lock-subsystem error degrades to the existing resume-first flow and never blocks authoring, and a genuinely dormant draft behaves exactly as before (#885)
- the `/gaia-harden` policy-memory loop now records countable findings instead of silently dropping every one. A finding carrying a valid `finding_class` counts at any severity, so severity becomes a ranking signal rather than an eligibility gate and recurring Suggestions finally register; a recurring finding that maps to no seeded class surfaces as a distinct `unclassified` seed-a-class-or-investigate signal, delivered through the statusline nudge and the `/gaia-harden` review but never drafted as a candidate. A new `PreToolUse` hook posts the machine-readable findings block deterministically on every local-mode `gh pr merge`, so the loop's input comes from a code path instead of an agent remembering a prose snippet. The closed finding-class vocabulary, the three-distinct-PR recurrence threshold, the 90-day window, the covered-class suppression, and the human approval gate are all unchanged (#881)
- the bash empty-array lint gate (`.gaia/scripts/lint-hook-array-guard.sh`) now scans every shipped `.gaia/scripts/**` script in addition to the `.claude/hooks/` bodies it already covered, closing the gap where a bare `"${arr[@]}"` over an empty array under `set -u` could abort a shipped script on stock macOS `/bin/bash` (3.2.57) mid-body, a class no bash-5 test suite can see. The expansions the widened scan flagged were all already safe behind a cross-line count-guard or a provably-non-empty literal and now carry the zero-exception offset-guard form the gate recognizes, so no shipped-script behavior changes. Maintainer-only enforcement (the gate and its bats suite are release-excluded), though the hardened scripts reach adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#879)
- the session-start janitor gains a ninth sweep that reaps off-pattern residue no other sweep owns: aged, off-allowlist entries at the top level of `.gaia/local/` and the direct children of its `audit/` and `cache/` dirs are removed once past a retention window (default 7 days, floor 2), while OS junk goes at any age. It is allowlist-gated so no live writer's output is mistaken for residue, and it never touches what an existing sweep already owns, any never-traverse working-state dir, or anything nested deeper. Two dedicated retention knobs also age out `audit/*.findings.json` and `cache/gh-artifact-pr.json`, and the `Local Working State` wiki page now enumerates all nine sweeps (#869)

- a `PreToolUse` guard (`.claude/hooks/block-invalid-yaml-write.sh`) that denies an Edit/Write/MultiEdit call that newly breaks a YAML region, a `.yml`/`.yaml` file or a Markdown `---` frontmatter block, closing the recurring footgun where a mid-sentence `: ` or a stray ` #` in an unquoted scalar reads as structural YAML instead of prose and is only caught later by a downstream parser or lint, sometimes on an already-saved artifact. The check is regression-only, so a file whose frontmatter was already broken before the edit never gets locked out (#867)
- a `PreToolUse` guard (`.claude/hooks/block-worktree-path-mismatch.sh`) that denies an Edit/Write/MultiEdit call whose target resolves to a different git worktree than the session's current one, closing a silent-wrong-write footgun where a stale pre-switch absolute path applied to the wrong checkout with no error signal. `isolation.md`'s `RESOLVED_ROOT` guidance now explicitly covers Edit/Write/Read alongside Bash and sub-agent dispatch (#842)

- New wiki concept page, Registering a Code Audit Team Member, documenting how to add a roster member (agent def, roster and builtin-fallback lockstep, machinery wiring, `finding_class` bucket, and recurrence-tally integration) and the required local-gate checklist a member add must run locally. (#817)
- Advisory prose-complexity lens for the Code Audit Team. A new maintainer-only, non-blocking member reviews changed instruction-prose under `.claude/skills/` and flags gratuitously complex prose (reducible length, deep nesting, high indirection, redundant instruction) as advisory comments plus a countable `prose/*` recurrence signal, holding GAIA's own instruction machinery to a legibility standard without ever blocking a merge.
- `/gaia-debt` gains a third handler class, `Handler: spec`, for tech debt whose fix has to begin with a design SPEC rather than a direct code change. A spec-class issue stays fully in the backlog (ordered, clustered, counted), but drains by printing a copy-pasteable `/gaia-spec` handoff and stopping instead of opening a fix PR; once handed off it parks under a durable `debt:spec-pending` label so it stops nagging until its SPEC and implementation land, or a maintainer removes the label. The handler value stays advisory like `prompt`/`plan`, so a drainer can still override it after reading the cited code, and the `Run /gaia-debt` count excludes parked issues (#815)
- the pre-merge Code Audit Team now reviews the maintainer CLI's build and config surface. `code-audit-maintainer-node` claimed only `.gaia/cli/src/**`, so an edit to the bundle build scripts and runtime dependencies (`.gaia/cli/package.json`), the resolved dependency tree (`.gaia/cli/pnpm-lock.yaml`), or the build config (`.gaia/cli/tsconfig*.json`) reached the required merge gate with no auditor ever reading it, exactly how a shell-portability defect in the bundle shebang once shipped to `main`. Those files now dispatch the member, kept in lockstep between `.gaia/audit-ci.yml` and the built-in fallback roster by `verify-audit-roster.sh`. Under that new coverage the orphaned `ulid` dependency left standing by the SPEC-038 CLI removal is drained, its own `package.json` edit now audited; nothing imported it, so the rebundled binaries are byte-identical. Maintainer-only and release-excluded: the maintainer-node member is scrubbed from the adopter bundle, so no adopter clone is affected (#813)
- the merge-time audit's recurring-finding tally now counts the GitHub Actions supply-chain findings the `code-audit-github-workflows` auditor previously could only report in prose (script injection, unsafe `pull_request_target`, unpinned actions, over-broad permissions). A closed `workflow/` finding-class bucket gives them a stable class so they feed the `/gaia-harden` recurrence loop, and a PR-time CI gate now verifies the committed CLI binary is reproducible from its source, so the audit and harden tooling always runs current code (#811)
- the pre-merge Code Audit Team gate now audits **locally by default**: a repo with no override runs the audit on the contributor's own machine instead of in CI, trading CI minutes for faster local feedback, and the default stays tunable per-team or per-author in `.gaia/audit-ci.yml`. A pull request from a fork still always resolves to CI, which runs under a scoped token rather than a contributor's full local credentials. A new `code-audit-github-workflows` roster member reviews GitHub Actions workflow and composite-action YAML for injection, permission, and secret-handling defects and reaches every existing adopter automatically on their next `/update-gaia`, and a new deterministic check verifies the roster's file-ownership rules are unambiguous before any of it ships (#810)
- a maintainer-only drift check for the branch ruleset's required status checks (`.gaia/scripts/verify-required-checks.sh`, plus an advisory-only CI job on pull requests that touch workflows). The set of checks that actually blocks a merge lives GitHub-side and can drift silently from the set the team believes is required, as #800 showed when a red `distribution-audit-pr.yml` run turned out not to be in the required set at all. The script declares the deliberately-required contexts in-repo and diffs them against the live ruleset, then separately flags other check-producing jobs that are not required so an intentional omission is a visible decision rather than an accident. It never mutates the live ruleset: promoting a check to required stays a deliberate, maintainer-run call, matching the ask-first cutover precedent for registering `GAIA-Audit`. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, since the declared contexts and the ruleset they describe are specific to the GAIA team's clone (#808)
- a per-PR distribution gate that fails a pull request introducing a newly-shipping file the committed `.gaia/manifest.json` does not answer, so each feature makes its own ship-or-withhold decision at its own PR instead of letting the manifest accumulate an unanswered backlog left for a later, unrelated `/distribution-audit` run. A maintainer-only `distribution-audit-pr.yml` CI job runs `gaia-maintainer release manifest --check` and fails only on the intersection of its unanswered set with the files the PR touches, so a pre-existing backlog never blocks an unrelated PR and each PR answers exactly the shipping surface it introduced. The PR-time complement to the release path, which regenerates the manifest with an explicit escape hatch by design. Maintainer-only and release-excluded: the maintainer binary and the manifest exist only on the GAIA team's clone, so the gate has no adopter surface (#785)
- a team-level git isolation policy, so GAIA stops asking branch-vs-worktree on every plan orchestration and every `/gaia-debt` run. A project owner picks the team's strategy once, when `/gaia-init` scaffolds a new project or when `/setup-gaia` runs on an existing one, and the choice is stored as an `isolation_policy` key in the committed `.gaia/automation.json`, so it travels with the repo instead of living on one machine. Choose to always use worktrees, to prefer them, or to prefer branches; the question explains what worktrees buy and what they cost before you answer. Answering is entirely optional and a team that never answers sees exactly today's behavior: the key stays absent, the prompt keeps asking, and the question stays available to answer later. The policy governs `/gaia-plan` and `/gaia-debt` only; `/gaia-audit`, `/gaia-harden`, and `/gaia-wiki` continue to work in the main checkout (#760)
- the six maintenance commands (`/gaia-audit`, `/gaia-debt`, `/gaia-fitness`, `/gaia-forensics`, `/gaia-harden`, `/gaia-wiki`) now record what they cost. Each run appends exactly one `kind: "command"` row to the local cost ledger and prints its spend as the last line of its report, closing the gap that let six of GAIA's nine token-spending commands burn a full session and account for none of it. Cost rows also carry the pull request or issue the run produced, so spend ties back to the artifact it bought; a command binds only the artifact its own run opened, never one it looked up or inherited, so an unattributed run records no artifact rather than a fabricated one. Producer-side only and fully back-compatible: ledger `schema_version` stays `1`, every new field is optional, and existing consumers are unchanged. A new `PostToolUse` hook (`.claude/hooks/capture-gh-artifact.sh`) watches `gh pr create` so plan execution, the one surface with no agent in the loop, can bind its pull request with no network call on the commit path; it exits 0 unconditionally and degrades silently. `wiki/concepts/Cost Data Contract.md` pins the shapes a dashboard can consume without reading the source (#758)
- `/distribution-audit`, a maintainer-only command that makes the ship-or-withhold decision for a newly-shipping file cheap and explicit at the moment the file appears, instead of leaving it to be noticed on release day or not at all. It names each file that would newly reach adopters and asks the maintainer to ship or withhold it, one file at a time, and it drives the release CLI, which now refuses by default, in every output mode, to produce a manifest at all while any shipping file lacks an explicit answer. Withholding writes the entry into the distribution boundary through the CLI, which is what makes the literal-path rule a mechanism rather than a promise. The release path keeps regenerating unconditionally via an explicit escape-hatch token a reader can see in its source. Maintainer-only tooling, so no adopter clone is affected (#686)
- a deterministic `shellcheck` gate over GAIA's own framework bash (`.gaia/tests/shell-lint.sh`, run by a maintainer-only `shell-lint.yml` CI job on every PR that touches a shell script). `shellcheck` was already the `code-audit-maintainer-shell` agent's authoritative oracle, but that agent is model-dispatched and advisory-only, and no other surface ran the tool: not CI, not the Quality Gate, not the pre-commit hook, which fires only on `app/`, `test/`, and `.storybook/` and so skipped shell changes entirely. A hand-maintained lint is a ratchet with no pawl. The gate lints every tracked `*.sh` at severity `warning` and fails the PR on any finding, while the agent keeps the lenses `shellcheck` cannot model (hook fail-open, stdin-JSON shape, `jq -n` injection safety). Turning it on surfaced a malformed `shellcheck` directive that had been silently aborting the linter's parse of an entire file, plus dead code in two shipped scripts. Maintainer-only and release-excluded: adopters run GAIA's bash but never author it, so the linter guarding it has no adopter surface. The gate also folds in the hook array-guard (`.gaia/scripts/lint-hook-array-guard.sh`) as a third pass, so every shell-lint caller, local runs, plan per-phase gates, and manual invocations, enforces the bash-3.2 empty-array class locally instead of only the dedicated audit CI bats job (#689, #876)
- `/gaia-spec` is now terminal in enforcement, not just in prose: a session that authors a SPEC can no longer chain into `/gaia-plan`. Planning always starts in a fresh session, because a spec session ends with an enormous context and the planner needs a clean one, but a skill-invoked `/gaia-spec` carries an outer goal ("spec and build X") that outlives the skill body, and the printed handoff was the only thing asking it to stop. A new `PreToolUse` guard (`.claude/hooks/block-spec-plan-chain.sh`) stamps the session when a SPEC id is allocated, then denies both routes into planning: the `Skill` call, and the `Read` of the plan reference an agent would otherwise follow inline. It is scoped to the session that ran `/gaia-spec` and released by `/clear`, so a fresh session still reaches `/gaia-plan` by any route. The block is strict: a `/gaia-plan` you type yourself in that same session is denied too, and the deny names the one-keystroke path (`/clear`, then paste the handoff) (#684)
- a `file-tech-debt` skill that is the single source of truth for filing an out-of-scope code-review finding as a deduplicated, severity-labeled `tech-debt` GitHub issue (dedup key, `--body-file` filing, idempotent labels, self-contained body schema, and the debt-count sentinel touch). The `code-review-audit` agent and `/gaia-audit` now reference the skill's recipe by path rather than each carrying an inline copy, and it auto-triggers in conversation on natural-language asks like "file this finding as debt". The wrapped `gaia-debt-key` format and label spellings stay a contract shared with the deterministic debt-count consumers (#628)
- the Code Audit Team: GAIA's pre-merge code review becomes a config-driven roster in `.gaia/audit-ci.yml` that maps file-globs to specialized auditors, all feeding one shared `GAIA-Audit` merge gate that fails closed until every dispatched member clears. The local gate is now a real AND-aggregator, so one cleared member can no longer satisfy the gate while a co-dispatched member withholds its marker over a Critical finding, and the member-aware status POST holds the github.com UI merge button shut on the same condition; `GAIA-Audit` is confirmed as the required check under both classic branch protection and repository rulesets. Two maintainer-only members now review the framework source the frontend auditor never could, `code-audit-maintainer-shell` (framework bash, with a `shellcheck` oracle and a hook-contract lens) and `code-audit-maintainer-node` (framework `.gaia/cli/src` TypeScript), both advisory-only and scrubbed from the adopter bundle. The frontend auditor is renamed `code-review-audit` → `code-audit-frontend`; the adopter-clone rename and roster migration are deferred to the v1→v2 update session (#626)
- a maintainer-only Comprehensive Audit phase for `/health-audit`: after the integrity loop terminates and before the final verdict, a deterministic gauge picks depth (skip / scoped / full) from the diff since the last release tag, then one specialist auditor per dimension (feature coherence, distribution integrity, `.gaia` tidiness, and the audit's own machinery) fans out over its slice, every material finding is adversarially verified by a refuter, and a delegated writer composes a prioritized report the maintainer dispositions by hand. Report-only (no auto-heal, files nothing) and entirely release-excluded, so nothing ships to adopters (#617)
- audit-phase spend is now attributable in the local cost ledger. The adversarial spec/plan audit (the `/gaia-spec` and `/gaia-plan` fan-outs) records as a nested `audit.adversarial` subset annotation on its phase cost record, carrying its own token buckets, dollars, elapsed time, and lens set as a strict drill-down that never changes the phase total; and each `code-review-audit` run records exactly once as a standalone `kind:"review"` cost row, associated to the SPEC or plan on the branch (or left unattributed when ad-hoc) through a Stop and `gh pr merge` trigger and tagged with its `source`. Producer-side only and fully back-compatible: `schema_version` stays `1`, every field is optional, and the existing cost-ledger consumers are unchanged. Dashboard rendering of the new drill-down is a fenced follow-on (#613)
- a two-tier OS-sandbox enablement preference. `/gaia-init` records a committed, GAIA-namespaced `sandbox_recommended` intent in `.gaia/automation.json` (never Claude Code's real `sandbox.enabled`), and `/setup-gaia` resolves it per machine: it detects sandbox capability (macOS ready, Linux/WSL2 needs `bubblewrap`+`socat`, native Windows/WSL1 unsupported), prompts once with the owner's recommendation as the default, and writes the real enable only to the gitignored `.claude/settings.local.json` with a minimal starter config and a per-machine resolution marker. `gaia ping --event setup` gains a `--sandbox on|off` field, and a new `OS Sandbox` wiki concept page plus a setup message state honestly how far the `.env` deny-merge actually protects secrets. Composes with the SPEC-028 read-side guard without modifying it (#607)
- linked git worktrees now share the main checkout's gitignored root `.env` / `.env.*` files (excluding the committed `.env.example`) through the same symlink mechanism that already shares `.gaia/local/` state, so a worktree's `pnpm dev` server and Playwright read the same local secrets with no manual copy. The symlink stays gitignored in the worktree and the read-side `.env` guard still denies it by basename, so the security posture is unchanged; the manual `gaia setup link-worktree` command gains the same behavior (#608)
- a static lint gate (`.gaia/scripts/lint-hook-array-guard.sh`) that flags unguarded bare `${arr[@]}` / `${arr[*]}` expansions in `set -u` hook bodies, the bash 3.2.57 empty-array abort class the bash-5 test suites cannot see, so a hook that would silently abort on stock macOS `/bin/bash` is caught before merge on every bash version. A sibling bats suite runs it over `.claude/hooks/**` in the existing audit CI job and self-tests the detector against a known-bad fixture; the one provably-safe expansion the scan flags in `block-rm-rf.sh` is offset-guarded so the gate stays zero-exception (#604)
- a read-side `.env` secret guard (`.claude/hooks/block-env-read.sh`) that closes the real gaps the literal `Read(.env)` deny leaves open. It denies the Read tool and residual Bash reads (sourcing, redirection, readers outside the recognized cat/head/tail/sed set) against the `.env.<env>` variant family (`.env.local`, `.env.production`, nested) the exact-`.env` rule never matched, plus recognized readers against those variants and bare `env`/`printenv` environment dumps, while keeping `.env.example` reachable and never echoing the matched command into the deny reason. The two canonical wiki homes and the hook header now state the honest posture: what `Read(.env)` already enforces against the Read tool and recognized Bash readers at any depth, the residual gaps closed, that the guard is heuristic defense-in-depth and not a sandbox, and that the OS-level `sandbox.filesystem` deny-read (merged with the Read/Edit deny rules) is the airtight opt-in that reaches arbitrary subprocesses (SPEC-028) (#601)
- git-arbitrated phase-level resume for the plan orchestrator: a cold-restarted plan run detects a prior attempt from its `RUNNING` sentinel, reconnects to that run's branch by isolation mode, and a deterministic helper (`.gaia/scripts/plan-resume-point.sh`) proves which phases are genuinely committed (each phase's recorded commit an ancestor of the reconnected HEAD) so the run resumes at the first incomplete phase behind a Resume / Restart / Abandon gate instead of re-dispatching sub-agents against work already in the tree. Git is the arbiter and any ambiguity re-runs the phase; the `RUNNING` sentinel gains a `mode:` line and each `SUMMARY.md` phase block leads with a machine-readable `Commit:` anchor, with the plan reference the single source of truth for that block format (#600)
- the `.gaia/cli` maintainer package is now linted to GAIA standards: a dedicated `.gaia/cli/eslint.config.mjs`, a `pnpm lint:cli` script, and a `cli-tests.yml` CI step that enforces it on every push, so the CLI holds the same quality bar as the app (#592)
- adoption pings now cover `setup` and `update` in addition to `init`, unified under a shared `gaia ping` subcommand, and carry a stable per-install `projectId` (#585)
- a per-agent reliability guard on the shared adversarial-audit fan-out that `/gaia-spec`, `/gaia-plan`, and the pre-merge `code-review-audit` all run: a shared deterministic no-op detector (`.gaia/scripts/audit-noop-detect.sh`) classifies each dispatched auditor's on-disk output without loading a finding body into the orchestrator's context; a silently no-op'd auditor gets exactly one hardened first-tool-call retry and then a guaranteed inline fallback that records degraded coverage instead of counting the lens as "found nothing"; and every audit dispatch prompt now leads with a positive first-tool-call guard line (#579)
- spec-less one-off plans now receive a monotonic `PLAN-NNN` id allocated from a local, per-machine `.gaia/local/plans/ledger.json`, replacing the description-derived slug folder. This gives an ephemeral plan the same stable ordering and allocation record a SPEC gets, so "which was plan #3" is answerable and the folder name is a clean telemetry/feature key. (#565)
- `/gaia-serena-sync`, a consent-gated command plus a passive statusline nudge that keeps Serena's indexed languages in step with a polyglot project. Serena picks one language at first startup and never re-detects, so a project that later grows Go or Python keeps getting single-language symbol intelligence with no signal. When a git-tracked high-signal manifest names a language absent from the `languages:` list in `.serena/project.yml`, the statusline surfaces the drift within a refresh cycle and, on an explicit yes, the command additively appends the missing language(s) to that list in place and byte-identically, then prompts a Serena restart. It never mutates the file without consent, refuses any config form it cannot edit safely, and stays inert for adopters who do not run Serena (#559)
- a per-phase dollar-cost figure in the cost sidecar the token tally writes: planning, execution, and spec-authoring costs each carry an estimated-USD figure, priced from that phase's own per-model token usage using a committed rate table and effective-dated pricing shared with the merge roll-up via one lib (`.gaia/scripts/token-pricing-lib.sh`) that both the tally and the roll-up source. Pricing never guesses a rate and never blocks: input it cannot price degrades to an `unavailable` marker, undercounted input to a marked `lower bound`, and the tally always exits 0 (#558)
- a dollar-cost estimate on the full-cycle token roll-up: it appends an `Est. cost (USD)` block that prices each action's tokens per model and per billing bucket from a committed, adopter-tunable rate table (`.gaia/scripts/token-rates.json`), applying cache read/write multipliers and effective-dated introductory pricing. It never fabricates a figure: input it cannot price degrades to an `unavailable` marker and undercounted input to a marked `lower bound`, and the roll-up always exits 0 (#547)
- a per-action ground-truth token and wall-clock cost readout for `/gaia-spec`, `/gaia-plan`, and KICKOFF plan execution. At each action's completion point a new `.gaia/scripts/token-tally.sh` helper sums the four billing buckets (fresh input, cache write, cache read, output) plus a total across the session's main transcript and every sub-agent sidecar, deduped to what the API actually billed, reports the wall-clock elapsed over billed model turns with the start and end shown in the machine's local timezone, writes a `cost.json` sidecar beside the spec or plan (a plan's sidecar keeps planning and execution costs distinct and never overwrites or sums them), and appends a durable machine-local ledger record (`.gaia/local/telemetry/cost.jsonl`) resolved to the main checkout so it survives deletion of the plan folder and a linked worktree. The tally never blocks or fails its action and degrades to a partial figure with a marker on unreadable input rather than fabricating a number (#536, #546)
- a Serena code-search enforcement guard (`.claude/hooks/serena-code-search-guard.sh`): a PreToolUse hook that blocks a bare-identifier `Grep` over `app/**` or `test/**` TS/TSX and routes it to Serena's LSP-backed symbol tools, closing the gap where the edit-scoped routing rule was absent from context during exploration. Re-running the identical grep passes for a genuine string or comment search, and the guard no-ops unless Serena is a registered MCP server with a `tsconfig.json` present, so adopters without Serena are unaffected (#511)
- `gaia.updateDepsHold`, a committed per-package version hold for `/update-deps`: a `{ package: ceiling }` map in `package.json` that caps a package to a version-prefix line (`"8.0"` holds the highest `8.0.x`, `"8.0.16"` freezes exactly). Unlike the CI-ignored local snooze ledger, the hold is committed, so it applies in interactive and CI runs until the maintainer lifts it, and fails closed when the ceiling matches no published version. The `/update-deps` preview surfaces active holds (#500)
- output-verification hardening for three GAIA self-audit workflows, so a single-pass judgment can no longer drive an edit or a clean verdict unchecked. `/gaia-audit` gains an optional classification-verification round before its Apply / Discuss / Decline gate (biased toward keeping any flagged memory delete it cannot confirm, since those deletes are irreversible); `/health-audit` (maintainer-only) gains a false-clean challenger that can revoke a clean exit; and `/gaia-fitness`'s final verify cycle now re-runs all seven categories, so a regression in a zero-finding lane is caught before it reports A+. All rounds are recommended-but-optional and never block (#472)
- a SessionStart janitor (`.claude/hooks/local-janitor.sh`) that garbage-collects dead working-state residue under the gitignored `.gaia/local/` on every startup/resume. It removes only residue whose death is provable, orphaned merge-gate markers and audit re-run ledgers, completed-but-unswept plan dirs, stray empty dirs, local `wiki-sync/*` landing branches whose upstream is gone, and orphaned merged worktrees left by a crashed session, so it is a backstop, not an owner; any unprovable death is skipped and the hook always exits 0 so it can never block a session from starting. It also owns retention for stale cache artifacts nothing else reaps, age-rotating them out of `.gaia/local/cache/` after 14 days. Adds the `Local Working State` wiki concept page (#468, #506, #673, #727)
- a forced-disposition gate on out-of-scope `code-review-audit` findings, plus the `/gaia-debt` drain skill. Debt the audit opens within its review radius but the PR did not change no longer slips through, non-security findings file as deduped, severity-labeled `tech-debt` GitHub issues, while security-class findings are fail-safe classified and diverted off every public or enterprise-readable channel (a private issue only on a confirmed private repo). The gate is the audit's fourth marker precondition, and `/gaia-debt` drains one `tech-debt` issue per invocation (deterministic severity-then-age ordering, a fresh branch through the normal audit gate), surfaced by a `Run /gaia-debt (N issues)` statusline indicator. Adds the `Audit Disposition and Debt Drain` wiki concept page (#465, #475, #495, #496)
- a maintainer-only CHANGELOG gate in the PR merge workflow: before any `gh pr merge`, evaluate whether the change warrants a `## [Unreleased]` entry and, if so, land it on the PR branch before merging, re-checked on every merge (including PRs resumed across sessions) so an entry promised at authoring time can't be lost. Lives in the `PR Merge Workflow` wiki page and the always-loaded `pr-merge` rule, both maintainer-only (#446)
- `/gaia-react-perf`, a measure-only React render-performance diagnostic: it drives a micro-interaction, captures per-render attribution through a committed bippy Playwright harness, and presents a ranked `memoDefeated` diagnosis with a react-doctor cross-reference and a structural-first fix. v1 is measure-only, the human applies the fix in conversation. Adds the React Perf Diagnostic wiki concept page and registers the skill so `/update-gaia` ships it (#442, #443)
- a React 19 idiom gate in the `react-code` skill (Gate 4): ref-as-prop over `forwardRef`, boolean-guarded `&&` over the numeric-0 leak (coercion mandatory, with react-doctor's `rendering-conditional-render` rule as the pre-merge backstop), `use()` and the `<Context>` shorthand over `useContext`/`Context.Provider`, never `null` in render, and staying in React Router's lane for forms. `references/hook-patterns.md` gains `useEffectEvent` and ref-callback cleanup, and the `typescript` skill prefers `!!` over `Boolean()` for inline coercion (#441)
- a `remix-utils` awareness map (`wiki/dependencies/remix-utils.md`): a curated, risk-sorted decision map (hydration-safe client render, SSE, CSRF, honeypot, safe-redirect, debounced fetcher) reachable from the surfaces Claude loads before hand-rolling a primitive. `MetaHydrated` now derives its hydration signal from remix-utils' `useHydrated()`, so `remix-utils` becomes a genuine import and drops out of knip's `ignoreDependencies` (SPEC-007) (#427)
- an adversarial multi-agent SPEC-audit in `/gaia-spec` that runs automatically on every spec before the final gate at a gauged Standard or Deep tier, with no prompt and never blocking save (the only non-run path is a fan-out-unavailable fallback): low-overlap lenses verify the draft's checkable claims against the repo and `node_modules` rather than on faith, a refutation pass keeps severity honest, and each surviving finding becomes either a plan-time directive (recorded in a sibling `AUDIT.md`) or a contract fix folded into the draft pre-save. It runs as a parallel `general-purpose` Agent fan-out so it works headless, and keeps the main thread lean by holding finding bodies in a per-spec on-disk cache while a delegated applier folds the fixes (SPEC-011) (#423, #483, #874)
- a lightweight adversarial decomposition audit in `/gaia-plan` that runs automatically after the plan is generated on any non-trivial plan and is skipped on a trivial single-phase one, decided by a gauge with no prompt and never blocking the handoff: three parallel lenses verify the decomposition itself, the one artifact neither the upstream SPEC-audit nor the downstream pre-merge `code-review-audit` can see, checking task-graph soundness, that every file, export, type, and signature named in a task contract resolves against the real repo and `node_modules`, and SPEC coverage. It is deliberately narrower than the SPEC-audit (no refutation pass, since the findings are checkable); localized findings fold into the task docs, a structural finding re-spawns the planner (#424, #874)
- `react-code` skill leads with a platform-first ladder (existing GAIA code → web platform like `Intl`/`URL`/`crypto.randomUUID` → already-installed dep → new dep → custom code) to walk before adding a dependency or hand-rolling a primitive
- `/gaia-harden` weighs an efficacy lens (Axis 3) before recommending a form: a recurring finding proves the problem, not the fix, so when the recommended form is prose and no cheap before/after evidence shows it would change behavior, that surfaces as a defer/decline signal for the human, never an auto-decline
- point Zod schema work at Zod's official LLM docs and treat them as authoritative over training memory, so valid Zod 4 forms are not rejected from stale v3 recollection
- a TDD testing-strategy foundation (SPEC-006): an AST determinism classifier scopes the hard RED commit gate to deterministic tests, so affirmatively-emergent test files are no longer forced to show a natural RED; a second `gh pr merge` gate proves the worthiness extractor ran over changed emergent tests; an advisory two-axis (honesty and worthiness) audit surfaces keep/fix/delete proposals with every delete human-gated; and `new-component` scaffolds a non-degenerate a11y test that can actually fail. Adds the Determinism Classifier, Worthiness Audit, and Worthiness Presence Gate wiki pages (#408)
- per-author `code-review-audit` mode (local vs CI): the pre-merge audit gate keys on a `GAIA-Audit` commit status resolved per PR author, so a developer in local mode runs the audit on their machine while CI stands down, the branch stays protected, and the gate fails closed to CI whenever a local run cannot confirm the required check. `gaia-init` and `setup-gaia` gain audit-mode prompts, and `/update-gaia` field-merges `.gaia/audit-ci.yml` so a committed `audit_authors` entry never forces a whole-file conflict (#407)
- restore the wiki hot cache after a context compaction through two GAIA-owned hooks (a `PostCompact` sentinel writer plus a `UserPromptSubmit` re-injector), so the "where we left off" cache survives compaction without depending on a claude-obsidian hook that some Claude Code builds reject (#386)
- a manifest-write guard that keeps GAIA's own workflows from cataloging an adopter project's files into `.gaia/manifest.json`. The manifest is a release-generated catalog of the files GAIA ships; a path absent from it is adopter-owned. A new PreToolUse hook (`.claude/hooks/block-manifest-write.sh`) denies manifest writes through the edit tools and common Bash vectors (best-effort defense-in-depth, not airtight), and a new `.gaia/**`-scoped rule (`.claude/rules/gaia-folder.md`) declaring the whole `.gaia/` tree GAIA's own machinery rather than part of the app built on it (the manifest prohibition folds into it), plus the `/gaia-plan` reference's task-authoring and audit-lens sections carry an enforced prohibition so a planner or auditor is steered away from inventing a "register every net-new file" obligation. A polluted manifest misleads the tools that read it (the fitness per-file drift check, forensics capture), wastes a plan phase per feature, and is simply wrong data; `/update-gaia` never overwrites an adopter file from a manifest entry

### Changed

- reading a `.env`, key, certificate, or credential file is now denied by GAIA's hook layer alone, and the five `Read()` rules in `permissions.deny` that used to do it are gone. Recent Claude Code versions carry a bypass-immune circuit breaker that, whenever any `Read()` deny rule is configured at all, forces a manual approval prompt for every search or copy command whose target it cannot statically prove is safe. A rule written with a `**` glob can match something inside any directory, so the four `**` rules GAIA shipped made that prompt unconditional on every recursive search in the tree, and the remaining literal `Read(.env)` kept it armed for any search of the repository root and for any read written after a directory change. No allow rule waives it and bypass permissions mode does not skip it, so the only way to stop paying it was to stop configuring the rules that arm it. The protection did not move to a weaker tier for that: `block-env-read.sh` already covered the Read tool and the common Bash readers against `.env` and every variant, and the deny rules only ever applied to commands Claude Code statically recognises as reads, which is the same limit the hooks have, so at the tool tier the exchange costs no coverage that was real; the sandbox tier those rules also fed is preserved separately, below. What the hooks genuinely lacked was the grep family, left out until now because `grep PATTERN FILE` puts a non-path in first position and sweeping every token as a path would deny `grep '.env' .gitignore`, an ordinary search of a committed file. A shared operand grammar now reads grep and ripgrep arguments properly, skipping the pattern operand and the values of flags that carry one, while treating `-f`, `--file`, `--exclude-from` and `--ignore-file` as the file reads they actually are. A new `block-secrets-read.sh` carries what the four `**` rules covered, matching `*.key`, `*.pem`, any name containing `credential`, and anything under a `secrets/` directory; the last two are deliberately wider than the globs they replace, since a guard replacing another guard should not be the narrower of the two. The dotenv guard is held to that same standard: a glob spelling of the read, `cat .env*` or `cat .env.*`, is now denied alongside the literal path, because either one dumps every dotenv file in the directory including the exempt `.env.example`. Both hooks emit a structured deny rather than merely exiting non-zero when they cannot load that grammar, so a broken or missing library fails closed and loudly instead of allowing every read: a non-zero exit other than 2 is non-blocking in the hook contract, and the call would otherwise proceed with a stderr line as the only trace. The guards also run on the Grep tool's `path` and `glob`, which return file contents in content mode and so read a path exactly as the Read tool does.

- the subprocess tier the removed `Read(.env)` rule used to carry is declared directly rather than dropped. A `Read()` deny rule merges into the OS sandbox boundary, where it reaches a subprocess spawned by sandboxed Bash, which no hook can see, so removing the rules would have quietly narrowed protection for every adopter who enabled the sandbox through `/setup-gaia`. `.claude/settings.json` now declares `sandbox.filesystem.denyRead` for `.env` and its variants, for the key and certificate classes, for any name carrying `credential`, and for anything under a `secrets/` directory, with `.env.example` re-opened by an `allowRead` entry. Every one of those classes is denied at any depth in the project rather than at its root alone, so a dotenv in a monorepo package is covered by the same boundary as the one beside `package.json`, and the subprocess tier agrees with the hook tier about which files are secret. That key is not a permission rule, so it arms nothing, and it stays inert until a machine enables the sandbox, which preserves the owner-recommends / machine-resolves split GAIA already documents. All of it ships, so adopters stop paying the prompt, and keep the sandbox tier, on their next `/update-gaia` (#1765).

- GAIA's own executable prose is now reviewed by the Code Audit Team rather than declared exempt from review. The roster answers who owns a path; a list beside it answers which paths are meant to have no owner at all, and the coverage invariant reports green because of that list rather than in spite of it, so a wrong entry there fails in the one direction the roster structurally cannot report. Only the structural health of those entries had ever been checked, whether each still matches a tracked file, reaches nothing another member owns, and stays inside the classifier's dialect; whether the judgment behind an entry was right had never been asked, and asking it one path at a time had already been tried repeatedly, turning up a fresh instance each time. Read as a population instead, the list showed one shape running through it. Commands, agent definitions, rules, health lenses and prompt templates are prose a language model executes, which is the artifact type the prose member exists for and already held for the skill files, and several more families of exactly that were sitting in the exemption list asserting they were meant to have no reviewer: the slash commands, the one-shot instruction runbooks, the stray agent definition beside the ones already owned, the CLI health lenses, the forensics prompts, and the spec-kit extension's commands, rules and templates along with its preset's. Each one steers a run, so a wrong edit to any of them misdirects that run the way a wrong skill file does, and every one of them had been merging unreviewed. They now dispatch the prose member. The inert prose sitting beside them stays exempt, and the distinction is recorded where the next entry gets added: not whether a file is Markdown, but whether an agent reads it as instructions or as input data. One candidate was rejected on that same reading, the CLI's rendered template directory, which the bundle build re-creates verbatim from sources the Node member already owns, so a finding raised against the copy could not be repaired there and the exemption is the accurate declaration. Narrowing an exemption rather than deleting it also means a new directory under one of these roots is now reported ownerless instead of inheriting an exemption nobody decided to give it. All of it sits inside the maintainer-only block the release scrub strips, so an adopter clone's roster and exemption list are unchanged (#1294)

- an audit clearance write now refuses when the reviewer's scope moved out from under it. Each Code Audit Team member captures a content digest at scope resolution and carries it into the earned clearance write as `--scope-digest`; if the tree changed in between, `.gaia/scripts/audit-write-clearance.sh` refuses to publish the marker rather than attesting to content the member never actually read. It ships, so an adopter's own audit member gets the same protection on their next `/update-gaia`. A capture is taken once per review and a repeat call returns that first value rather than replacing it, so the comparison cannot be laundered into passing; it is replaced only once that member has published a marker or a refusal keyed to it, which is what marks the round as ended and keeps a refused round from stranding the next one on a stale capture. A round that ends having published nothing at all -- a pass withheld over uncommitted edits, a write refused because the tree moved, an interrupted run -- cannot be told apart from one still going, so its capture outlives it; the next round's refusal releases that capture as it exits, which costs the one forfeited round instead of shutting the gate permanently with no way to reopen it. `--recapture` is the explicit override for a review that legitimately ends on different content. A checkout with no branch to read, which is what GAIA CI pins, supplies the key's branch half through `GAIA_AUDIT_KEY_BRANCH`. **Action required for adopters running GAIA CI with self-heal pushes enabled:** the CI audit now declines to stamp its `GAIA-Audit` success when the run pushed a self-heal commit and no clean marker attests the pushed content, where it previously stamped success regardless. That is the fail-closed direction and it is what makes the refusal above mean anything in CI, but it turns a run that self-healed and lost its marker from green into blocked, and a run whose leftover uncommitted edits the workflow swept to the branch is blocked for the same reason. Re-run the audit on the pushed head to clear it.

- GAIA CI's post-merge watch now interprets its own result in one place instead of at each site that asks for it. The action watches CI twice, once on the merge and again on the revert it opens when that merge reds, and each watch carried its own byte-identical copy of the reading that turns the watcher's answer into a verdict, the guard that keeps an unreadable answer from killing the step before it can report anything included. Only the names the two wrote their results under, and the wording of the annotation each leaves in the job log, had ever differed. That shape had already come within one edit of a silent divergence: the guard was itself a repair applied by hand to both copies, and had it landed on only one, the revert-path watch would have kept the original defect while every check in the repository stayed green, because the shell inside a composite action's steps sits where no `*.sh` glob reaches, so shellcheck never opened it and nothing tested it. The shared reading now lives in a file beside the watcher it wraps, where shellcheck reaches it and a suite holds it to its contract, and each watch keeps only what is genuinely its own. No behaviour changes. Adopters running GAIA CI receive the new file on their next `/update-gaia` (#1704)

- the boundary confining GAIA's self-healing code reviewer now reaches the tests that live inside the surface it repairs. That reviewer is the one audit member allowed to push fixes, `app/**` is its own repair surface, and the refusal set deciding what it may never touch was written as a list of top-level directory prefixes: it refused `test/`, `.playwright/`, and `.storybook/` whole and allowed everything under `app/`. This template keeps its component suites and its stories beside their components, so every vitest suite the merge gate runs and every story Chromatic snapshots sat on the allowed side, and a member could weaken or delete the check that would catch a bad repair in the same self-heal commit that made the repair, with only the file-count cap and a human reading the diff in between. The file recording the boundary already stated its own principle as every test surface rather than just `test/`, and refused `.storybook/` on exactly this reasoning while noting in the same breath that the stories themselves live under `app/`, so the set was narrower than the rule it wrote down. The refusal now names that surface too, and each shape it names is taken from the collector that decides what actually gates a merge rather than from the folder convention, so a suite written outside a `tests/` folder is covered by the same rule as one inside it; a `tests/` directory anywhere under `app/` is refused whole on top of that, since a fixture or a shared helper beside the assertions weakens the suite as surely as the assertions do. Both enforcement points read the one refusal set, so the local hook and the CI push gate widen together, and a member that finds a defect in one of these files now reports it instead of repairing it. It ships, so an adopter's own audit member is bound by it on their next `/update-gaia` (#1699)

- the template's IndexPage test no longer keeps its expectations in prose. Its theme-switch case explained the label by naming both the translation key and the English string that key renders, neither of which appeared anywhere else in the file, so a copy change or a key rename would leave the comment asserting something false with nothing to catch it, while the assertion beneath it admitted any of three labels though the comment had just derived exactly one. The expected label now reads from the same language file the component reads and the assertion pins that one label, so a renamed key moves the test along with the component and an adopter who changes the default theme mode gets a failing test rather than a passing one. The absence block in the same file also stops narrating the edit that removed the brand surface: three of its cases named symbols the page no longer renders, so a reader chasing one found nothing, and its banner claimed a narrower group than the five cases it heads. The cases now pin behaviour and the banner names the group it actually covers (#1703)

- the CLI GAIA ships now builds every git path listing from one shared helper instead of a spelling each author had to remember. Reading paths out of git safely takes a specific incantation, and getting it wrong is silent: under git's default settings a page whose name carries a non-ASCII byte comes back quoted and escaped, a spelling that names no file and no blob, so the caller skips it and reports the answer with exactly the confidence it would report a complete one. That incantation had been hand-copied at four call sites, each with its own paragraph re-deriving why it was needed, and the four paragraphs had already drifted apart in wording. Nothing about the shape made the omission visible, which is how the class reached four instances one at a time, each found by someone reading the code rather than by anything that runs. The helper now owns the flags and the record split, so a caller supplies a verb and its arguments and cannot place the flags wrongly or leave them out; the near relative that reads a diff's line counts is built the same way. The canary fixtures proving the behaviour, which had been copy-pasted across three suites down to the sample filename and its escape sequence, are folded into one module beside it. No behaviour changes, and the repair is prevention by construction rather than detection: it makes the unsafe call site harder to write, and composes with, rather than replaces, a guard that would catch one after it is written (#1681)

- the CLI GAIA ships now bundles a patched `js-yaml`, and both lockfiles were re-resolved to clear the open advisories against `js-yaml`, `fast-uri`, `valibot`, `brace-expansion` and `morgan`. None of them was exploitable here, though not all for the same reason. Four are denial-of-service-by-parsing or host-confusion classes whose vulnerable parsers only ever read first-party input, an eslint toolchain's own config or the adopter's own workspace files. The fifth, `morgan`, is log forging through the `:remote-user` token, which genuinely does read a client-supplied header; it is unreachable here because `@react-router/serve` mounts `morgan("tiny")`, and the `tiny` format string contains no `:remote-user`. What made it worth doing was the shipped artifact rather than the exposure, since `.gaia/cli/gaia` inlines its dependencies and an adopter's own scanner reads that binary as carrying the vulnerable `js-yaml` whatever the real reachability is. The lockfile half was staleness rather than an upstream that had not shipped a fix: every parent's declared range already admitted the patched version and nothing had re-resolved since it published. `js-yaml`, `fast-uri` and `morgan` needed an `overrides` floor to move at all, because `pnpm update` reaches only direct dependencies and `pnpm dedupe` only collapses duplicate resolutions that already exist, so neither lifts a transitive holding a single stale pin; each floor names the advisory it clears and is expected to retire once its parent bumps its own. Adopters receive the floors as a three-way merge against their own `pnpm-workspace.yaml` on their next `/update-gaia` (#1648)

- the React render diagnostic's `bippy` harness moves to 0.7.3. 0.7.0 removed ten of the exports the harness read: five fixed work-tag constants, and five fiber-reading helpers upstream dropped as too policy-heavy to keep. The helpers are now carried in the harness itself, reproducing the reads they replaced, so the attribution `/gaia-react-perf` reports is unchanged. The constants are the part that genuinely improves: they are replaced by a work-tag map resolved for the fiber's own React version, which closes a silent failure, since fixed integers mislabel a component the next time React renumbers its work tags and nothing says so. The port had been written and typechecked a week earlier but could not be committed, because every 0.7.x release was still inside the seven-day release-age window: the dependency would not install, so the harness could not typecheck against it and the pre-commit gate was unreachable by construction. It has now been run green against a live browser as well as the typechecker, which is the verification the parked version never had. The pin and the harness have to move together, so `/update-gaia` carries both; the dead `bippy@0.5.42` release-age exemption retires with them (#1522)

- `@gaia-react/lint` moves to 2.1.0, which activates `sonarjs/parameterized-tests`: three or more sibling tests differing only by their data are now a lint error rather than a style preference. GAIA's own suites were collapsed into `test.each` tables to satisfy it, one row per original test, so no case was dropped and every test name is unchanged. The rule is not auto-fixable, so `eslint --fix` will not clear it for you. **Action required:** run `pnpm lint` after updating and collapse whatever it flags in your own suites into a single parameterized test per flagged run (#1624)

- the root `CLAUDE.md` GAIA ships is 22% shorter with every rule it carried still stated, and the knowledge audit's word budget for that one file rises from 400 to 500. The budget was written for routing and principles, and the file it polices also carries the standing conduct rules that govern every response; those cannot be lifted to a wiki page, because they are load-bearing precisely by auto-loading every session, so a promote would preserve the bytes and switch the rules off. Compressing first and measuring what was left is what set the new number: the routing sections gave up most of the words, largely by dropping a paragraph that restated two rules which already auto-load on the paths they govern, and the floor the remaining content reaches is above 400 and below 500. The budget for a nested `CLAUDE.md` is unchanged at 400, since a nested file is scoped routing and carries none of that conduct. Adopters receive the rewritten `CLAUDE.md` as a three-way merge against their own copy on their next `/update-gaia`, and the raised threshold changes when the statusline's `over budget` nudge fires for them (#1586)

- the audit spawn oracle and the pull-request merge gate now tell an unresolvable diff base apart from a genuinely empty change set. A change set that is empty against a verified remote base dispatches no audit member and no longer holds the merge gate shut, while a base derived from a local branch, a base that does not resolve, and a diff command that failed all stay exactly as fail-closed as before. The relaxation is bound to the pull request the merge command actually names, so a checkout whose own range is empty can never clear the merge of a different, unaudited one (#1538)

- the `vite` version ceiling is lifted and dependencies move to their current releases. Vite was pinned to the 8.0 line because Vite 8.1.x built a Storybook bundle that threw `__commonJS is not defined`, which broke Chromatic story extraction while leaving the ordinary quality gate green. Vite 8.2.x emits the helper correctly, so the hold is removed from `gaia.updateDepsHold` and Storybook builds and runs clean again. Note that `.github/workflows/chromatic.yml` short-circuits on a `chore(deps):` subject, so a `vite` bump arriving through `/update-deps` skips the one gate that catches this class; [[Storybook]] carries the local verification recipe to run instead

- dependencies bumped: `vite` 8.0.16 to 8.2.1, `jsdom` 29 to 30, `storybook` 10.5.4 to 10.5.8, plus in-range moves across the React types, Testing Library, Playwright, Conform, axe-core, and tooling groups. `typescript` remains held at the 6.0 line

- the `priority:high` and `priority:critical` labels fold into `severity:important` and `severity:critical`, and `needs-human-review` becomes `needs-human`, closing two duplicate label vocabularies. **Action required:** if your own workflows or issue templates use `priority:high`, `priority:critical`, or `needs-human-review`, rename them to `severity:important`, `severity:critical`, and `needs-human`. Run `.gaia/cli/gaia labels check` to find every remaining reference (#1436)

- a tech-debt issue's handler class, whether its fix is a prompt, a plan, or a design SPEC, is now a `handler:prompt` / `handler:plan` / `handler:spec` label instead of a `Handler:` line buried in the issue body. It was the last of the four classification axes carried as prose while severity, surface, and difficulty were all labels, and prose cost it three things a label has for free: you can now filter the backlog with `gh issue list --label handler:spec`, a body someone reformatted by hand no longer parses as no class at all, and re-grading is a one-command relabel whose before-and-after GitHub records in the issue timeline. That last one matters most, because the class is advisory by design: `/gaia-debt` re-derives it from the cited code at drain time and can move an issue in either direction, so the field most likely to be changed was the one that was hardest to change. The label is validated when present and never demanded, so a hand-filed issue that carries no class stays legal and drains exactly as it does today. New filings carry the label and no body line, and there is no backfill for you to run: the body-line convention this replaces only ever existed inside GAIA's own repository, so nothing in your backlog carries a stored class to convert (#1428)

- `/gaia-debt` now verifies that an issue still describes your code before it cuts a branch to fix it. An issue asserts a file path, a set of line citations, and sometimes a count its suggested fix is built around, and nothing re-checked any of those between the day it was filed and the day it is drained, so an issue whose subject was renamed, moved, or already fixed still read as actionable and would be implemented faithfully as a change the code does not need. Every subcommand now flags candidates whose cited file no longer exists, advisory only, since a renamed file does not by itself make a finding untrue. The full verification runs once, against the one issue about to be fixed, and it blocks: on a mismatch the fix is released back to the backlog and you are told what your tree says instead, rather than the issue being quietly rewritten or the fix proceeding on a re-derived guess, because choosing between correcting the issue and correcting the code is yours to make

- `/gaia-debt` now reads an issue's comments, for the issue it is about to fix. Posting a correction as a comment and leaving the body alone is the natural way to record that a suggested fix turned out to be wrong, and a body-only drain would rebuild work a comment already recorded as reverted, which is indistinguishable from ordinary progress once it is in the diff. A comment correcting part of the body now supersedes that part, newest first, and the substitution is stated before any code is written; a comment reporting the fix as already done, reverted, or unsafe stops the drain instead. The backlog listing stays body-only on purpose, so the cost is one extra read per issue actually being fixed rather than one per issue in your backlog

- a cross-remit out-of-scope finding waived at merge time, recorded in the pull request body rather than filed, now has to clear two disqualifiers on top of matching a path. A finding is no longer waivable when the change under review is what created the inconsistency it names, or when the change leaves a pointer in shipped content promising that a separate change will handle it; both kinds are filed as tech-debt issues instead. The path condition used to be sufficient on its own, which let a change wave away a defect it had just introduced in a file it happened to be touching.

- a rejected GAIA-Audit status POST no longer fails your audit job, on any of the four paths that can stamp one. Three of them let the rejection red the whole `code-review-audit` run, which told you your review had failed when it had passed and a transient GitHub API error had merely stopped a commit status from landing. The red also protected nothing: with no status posted the required check is absent and the merge button stays shut regardless, so it was noise on top of an already-closed gate. On the two skip paths it was worse than noise, because a failed step suppressed the very pull-request comment written to explain a shut gate, leaving the case most in need of an explanation with none. All four paths now log the rejection and publish it, and the comment names it, so you are told to re-run the workflow rather than sent to repair a `.gaia/VERSION` file that is fine, which is what the existing no-stamp message would have claimed. The comment covering the two audit-ran paths gained the rest of that reporting with it: a rejected POST is only one of five ways those paths can finish having stamped nothing, and the other four (an absent or empty `.gaia/VERSION`, a frontend digest that would not recompute, an audit not proven clean, a co-dispatched auditor CI cannot run) previously all read as "complete: no fixes needed" beside a shut gate. Advisory runs are unaffected, since a mode that posts no status by design earns no warning about not having posted one. The four disagreed because the answer was carried as a per-path argument rather than settled once; that argument is gone, so the answer cannot drift back one writer at a time. Nothing to do on your side: the argument was added and removed between releases, so no rendered workflow you received ever passed it (#1296)

- the pre-merge workflow no longer tells you a small correction costs a full audit round, and no longer sends you to defer one on that basis. It priced every re-dispatch at the 60-110k full-scope figure, but a member re-reads its whole owned surface only when it has no earned clearance to anchor on; otherwise it reviews just the delta since that clearance, which is what a comment or prose repair produces. Because the merge workflow is read before every merge, that one wrong number was quietly suppressing small correct edits across every pull request: each was individually judged not worth a cost the machinery was not actually charging. The page now prices a re-dispatch by the scope it resolves, names the specific conditions that do reset a review to full scope, and reverses the guidance for the cheap case, applying a one-line correction is the default for a member that still holds its clearance, and accepting-and-noting is reserved for the reset cases and for findings big enough to deserve their own change. It also prices the deferral side, previously treated as free, and adds a sweep for comment and prose repairs before a round's last dispatch so they ride a re-review already being paid for. This is the guidance-side counterpart to the reset-tier narrowing above. Maintainer-facing process guidance on a shipped wiki page, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1398)

- editing a coding-convention rule under `.claude/rules/` no longer makes every dispatched audit member re-read its entire owned surface. The whole directory was treated as one block governing the gate itself, so changing a word in a convention about how code should be written threw away each member's incremental review position and re-scoped the roster to a full pass. That made a rule edit the most expensive change in the repository, and the practical effect was that rule edits got deferred rather than made. Only the two rules that decide what the gate does with a review, the quality gate and the merge workflow, keep that reach; the rest are conventions. Nothing merges with less scrutiny: a convention change still requires a fresh review of the code under it, and what goes away is only the re-reading of work the change cannot have affected (#1397)

- the optional CI workflows GAIA renders for you now share their crons instead of each holding its own. GitHub bills every job a whole minute, so each tool that woke on its own schedule paid that minimum just to decide it had nothing to do, and on the private repositories most adopters run this on those minutes are billed. A new `gaia-ci.yml` is now the only scheduled workflow: it carries every distinct tool cron, and when one fires it asks each tool due on that cron whether it has work and runs only the ones that answer yes.
