It also skips a tool whose previous GAIA CI pull request is still open, which previously cost a full-history checkout and a billed minute to discover. Each tool workflow keeps its manual "Run workflow" button and behaves exactly as before, whether the scheduler starts it or you do. Two tools on the same schedule now cost one tick between them rather than one each: on the default schedules a quiet month goes from roughly 75 billed minutes to roughly 45, and if you had put all four tools on a daily cron, from roughly 120 to roughly 30. Tools on different schedules still cost a tick each on a day when both their crons fire, because GitHub starts one run per matching cron entry. **Action required:** run `/setup-gaia` after updating if you already have these workflows installed, it detects the drift and offers to re-render them; until then your installed copies keep their own crons (#1380)

- a scheduled `gaia-ci-*` workflow now decides whether it has work before it installs anything. All four ran a full checkout, a pnpm setup, a Node setup, and a frozen-lockfile install, and only then consulted the skip decision, so a tick that was going to do nothing still paid the entire setup to find that out. That is invisible on a public repository, where Actions minutes are free, and it is not free on the private repositories most adopters run this on, where minutes are billed. The decision now runs straight after checkout, against the `gh` CLI and the committed GAIA binary, neither of which needs an install, and pnpm, Node, and the install are gated behind it. Every skipped tick does less work and finishes sooner; because a job is billed in whole minutes, that converts to a billed minute recovered only where the old setup pushed the job past sixty seconds, which is plausible on the 2-core runners private repositories get and is unmeasured. The floor of one billed minute per tick is set by the rounding and by how many crons there are, so this change cannot go below it. A tick that does run behaves exactly as before. The `gaia-ci-*` workflows are re-rendered by `/setup-gaia` rather than refreshed by `/update-gaia`, so this reaches an adopter's CI on their next `/setup-gaia` (#1312)

- a Code Audit Team member re-dispatched in a later review round now reviews only the delta since its own clearance last covered it, instead of re-reviewing the whole pull request whenever a sibling's repair rotates every member's digest by touching shared gate machinery. The review base still resets to a full-PR review whenever the rules deciding what a member owns, how a clearance is computed, or which members run at all have themselves changed, or whenever the member's own instructions changed.

- GAIA's own bats suites are now split across shards by file size rather than by file count, which takes a hand run of the whole set from about 227 seconds to about 202. Counting files balanced the wrong quantity: per-file setup dominates per-test work here, so one 22-second suite holds 185 tests while another holds one, and shards that came out even in files were lopsided in minutes. Size is read from the directory listing at assignment time, so a newly added suite is weighted the moment it lands; recording measured runtimes instead would be a better proxy and would reintroduce exactly the silently-stale table this sharder was designed to avoid. One shard is added so the scripts group stops binding the whole partition, and the sharder's guard suite gains a balance check, since its existing partition checks care only which shard a file lands in and are blind to how heavy that shard becomes. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1362)

- the pre-merge code audit no longer pays for two kinds of work it can prove nothing reads. Its three deterministic oracles, react-doctor, knip, and the dependency-CVE advisory, scan the whole repository or the whole dependency tree regardless of what changed, and ran on every dispatch, including one against a pull request that changed nothing at all. They now skip in exactly that case, the only one where a whole-repository oracle provably has nothing new to report. The gate deliberately reads the unfiltered set of changed files rather than the TypeScript-filtered scope the specialist subagents gate on, so a lockfile or configuration change carrying no TypeScript still runs all three, and a base the audit cannot resolve runs them too. Separately, the per-phase progress breadcrumbs the agent writes are now written only in CI. Their sole reader is a workflow print step, so a local audit run was making six tool round-trips to produce a file nothing opened, and now writes nothing at all. Note that this changes an audit agent definition, which invalidates any clearance already earned on an open pull request; re-run its audit members before merging (#1359, #1360)

- the hand-run command that runs GAIA's own bats suites now finishes in roughly a third of the time, about 232 seconds against about 638. It grouped the suites into six directory-sized batches, which left the whole wall clock pinned to the largest of them running as a single process, while the pre-merge check had already moved to a nine-way split proven on every pull request. The hand run now consumes that same split rather than carrying a second description of it, so a local run and CI execute an identical set of test files and cannot drift apart; the runner is also now fail-closed, refusing to report success over a partial set rather than greening on less work than it claims. The runner and its guard suites are maintainer-only and release-excluded, but the corrected contributor documentation ships, so an adopter clone receives the updated instructions on its next `/update-gaia` (#1366)

- the pre-merge check that runs GAIA's own bats suites no longer downloads its test harness. Every leg of the matrix fetched the same pinned archive from GitHub within seconds of its siblings, and the host answered part of that burst with rejections: unretried, it failed four legs and turned a required check red on a change that had nothing wrong with it, and with backoff absorbing it instead, the worst leg spent about two minutes waiting on the critical path. That archive is now a tracked file in the repository, still verified by digest before anything is extracted, so the check no longer depends on a third-party host being reachable at the moment you open a pull request. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1346)

- the pre-merge check that runs GAIA's own bats suites now fans out across a matrix of parallel runners instead of a single job, so pull-request feedback arrives materially sooner: a clean run finishes about 2.8x faster than before. The bats test harness itself is now pinned to a specific release and verified by digest before it installs, closing the last gate tool that ran unpinned and could return different verdicts depending on the machine. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1343)

- a GitHub issue number inside a code comment or a bats test name now has a stated test behind it: paired with a description of the failure mode it names, it stays; unpaired, it goes. On every non-Markdown file GAIA ships, such a reference is now written as `gaia-react/gaia#NNN` so it resolves to GAIA's own repository rather than to the reader's. This reaches the audit workflow template too: an adopter running `install-audit-workflow` previously received a workflow whose comments pointed at issue numbers in their own repository, and now receives one whose comments point at GAIA's (#1324)

- the rule above is now enforced rather than asserted. Its audit command existed and was correct, but nothing invoked it: no job, hook, or test suite ran it, so it fired only when a human happened to read the rule and paste it, and a bare issue number written into a shipped file would have merged green and reached adopters pointing at their own tracker. A gate now runs on every pull request. It also narrows one thing the pasted command got wrong, which mattered only once the check ran unattended: a three- or four-digit CSS hex colour such as `#333` is indistinguishable from an issue number, and shipped stylesheets would otherwise have failed the build on a colour. The audit section of the comment rule is now maintainer-only and no longer reaches adopter clones, because the obligation it enforces is GAIA's own: on your clone, a number you write in a file you edited means your tracker, and a check demanding GAIA's prefix there would be wrong (#1335)

- both TDD gates' content signal (the RED-observation ledger and the worthiness ledger) now hashes each test's comment-free content, so rewording a comment inside a test leaves the signal unchanged and a recorded verdict in either ledger survives it; any change to what the test executes still rotates the signal and still invalidates the recorded verdict. Both ledgers are gitignored, per-tree state, and no migration ships: a stale worthiness line is re-earned by simply re-running the evaluator, a stale RED line is not self-healing since the gate demands a freshly observed failing run at the current content and the affected test now passes, and a checkout that moves across this change and back pays that re-judgement once per direction of skew (bounded at two, since both ledgers key on the resolved tree root rather than the branch), with reverting corrupting nothing since there is no migration to undo. Measured on this repo: 12 of 138 tracked test records across the 32-file corpus carried a comment inside the hashed span and rotate under the change, invalidating 2 live worthiness-ledger lines and 0 live RED-ledger lines on this checkout (its one RED-ledger line was already dead by `fullName`, unrelated). **Action required:** none automatically; if the worthiness gate denies on a line this change invalidated, re-run the worthiness evaluator or invoke the ledger writer directly: `node .gaia/scripts/audit-ledger/append-worthiness.mjs <file> <fullName> <verdict> [artifact]`. (#1332)

- the CLI workspace now type-checks every indexed read. `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` was off and is not implied by `strict`, so an array or record lookup was typed as though the key were always present, and most of the CLI quietly depended on knowing that. The convention had been written down only as a comment repeated at twenty-six sites, which a comment sweep correctly removed as a copy-pasted explainer, leaving the setting the code relies on recorded in no file at all. The compiler now carries it, which is the one form a later sweep cannot delete. Turning it on surfaced no live defect: every check added guards a case the surrounding control flow already prevented, and the argument parsers were compared command by command against their previous output before landing. Maintainer-only, and adopters receive the built binary rather than this source, so no adopter clone is affected (#1323)

- the optional CI workflows GAIA renders for you no longer reference their third-party actions by a moving tag. The shared checkout-and-setup steps behind `gaia-ci-wiki`, `gaia-ci-update-deps`, `gaia-ci-pnpm-audit`, and `gaia-ci-stale-branches` named `actions/checkout@v5`, `pnpm/action-setup@v6`, and `actions/setup-node@v5`, and a major tag resolves to whatever it points at the moment your CI runs, so a force-moved upstream tag would execute in your job holding that job's token. They now pin to full commit SHAs, which is the convention the audit workflow's own template already stated and followed, so one template directory stops handing out two different supply-chain postures. Pinning alone would have traded a moving ref for a frozen one nobody advances, since these are template files Dependabot cannot see, so the pins are tied to the ones GAIA's own workflows run and a test holds them equal: the weekly bump lands there first and fails that test until the same pin is mirrored across, which is how it keeps reaching you. The pins also catch up two majors on checkout and setup-node, the same versions GAIA has been running on its own CI. **Action required:** if you already have these workflows installed, run `/setup-gaia` after updating, it detects the drift and offers to re-render them with the new pins; until then your installed copies keep the tags they were rendered with (#1301)

- the third-party actions GAIA's CI workflows pin move up: `actions/checkout` to v7.0.1, `actions/setup-node` to v7.0.0, `pnpm/action-setup` to v6.0.10, `dorny/paths-filter` to v4.0.3, and `chromaui/action` to v18.1.0. Two of these cross a major boundary twice over, and both breaking changes were checked against how GAIA actually calls them rather than waved through on the version number: checkout v7 refuses to check out fork pull request code, but only under `pull_request_target` and `workflow_run`, neither of which any GAIA workflow uses; and setup-node v6 narrowed its *automatic* caching to npm, which reaches nothing here because every call site names `cache: 'pnpm'` explicitly. Chromatic v17 drops Node 20, below the floor GAIA already requires. The two workflows adopters receive arrive as a merge on the next update. **Action required:** none (#1300)

- the test workflow no longer pays for a container image it may never use. It read the package version through a Marketplace action packaged as a Docker container, and a container action's image is built when the job starts rather than when its step is reached, so a run that filtered out every other step still spent 15 seconds of a 24-second run building an image for a step that then did nothing at all. Reading the version inline costs nothing and produces the same value under the same name, so nothing downstream changes. This is one of the two workflows adopters receive, so it arrives as a merge on the next update, and a clone that has customised that step will see the conflict there. **Action required:** none

- GAIA's own CI now bounds every job's runtime, starts nine more workflows from a default-deny token, which is every workflow in the directory, and checks that the committed CLI binaries still match their source in one place instead of two. Four jobs had no runtime cap at all, so a hang held a runner for the six-hour default; the worst of them was the release publish job, which carries the only write-scoped token in the directory. Six of the nine granted permissions per job but named nothing at the top, which left any job added to them later inheriting whatever the repository default happens to be, rather than nothing; the release workflow was the sharpest case, since the token a later job would have inherited there is the one that can write to the repository, and it now grants that scope to the publish job alone. The remaining three named a read scope at the top and had no job-level block, so what their single job can do is unchanged, and each now grants that scope on the job itself; what this removes is the grant a second job added to any of them would have inherited. The binary-freshness check was two verbatim copies of one step kept in step by a comment asking a human to remember, and it guards a required check, so what one copy missed merged; both callers now run the same script. Bumps for the pinned action SHAs are now proposed weekly instead of being noticed by hand, which also gave the audit roster a file it did not cover: a config that decides which action versions land in a repository dispatched no auditor and needed no clearance, so a change to it would have merged with nobody responsible for it. The shell member takes it, alongside the rest of the maintainer-only governance config it already owns. All of it is maintainer-only and release-excluded apart from the default-deny grant on the shipped test workflow, so an adopter clone is otherwise unaffected. **Action required:** none

- the CI setup steps now come from one shared definition instead of being spelled out at every call site, Chromatic reuses the Storybook build the preview-environment guard already produces instead of building a second one, and the rule deciding whether a dependency-bump pull request skips a check exists once rather than in four places. Two of the workflows this touches are ones adopters receive, so both changes arrive as a merge on the next update and bring two new files with them; a clone that has customised its setup or Chromatic steps will see the conflict there. Separately, GAIA's own bats suites now run concurrently rather than one after another, which is maintainer-only. Moving the third-party action pins into the shared definition would have taken them out from under the weekly bump that keeps them current, so the bump automation and the pin-parity test both follow them there rather than leaving a pin that silently stops moving. **Action required:** none

- the rule that decides whether the audit's merge-gate status may be posted at all now exists once instead of five times. The workflow finishes down five different paths, and each one carried its own copy of the same safety rule: work out what was actually reviewed, refuse to post anything if that cannot be established, and never overwrite a clearance already recorded by a local run. Five copies of the most safety-critical rule in the file meant a correction landed on one and quietly left the others behind, and because each copy guards a path a given pull request may never take, the difference could survive indefinitely and show up only as the same status meaning different things depending on how the run finished. All five paths now call one shipped script, which is covered by tests that drive each path exactly as the workflow runs it. No behaviour changes on any path, deliberately including one place where the five paths already disagreed: three treat a rejected status post as a failure and one does not, and that difference is now carried explicitly rather than silently resolved by whichever copy the consolidation happened to keep. **Action required:** none, though an adopter who has hand-edited this workflow will see a large merge on their next update, since roughly a third of the file is replaced (#1286)

- the audit workflow that backs the merge gate is now much harder to break by accident, and the helper that posts its status comment is now covered by the same checks every script beside it gets. The workflow restated its conditions for having reached the audit phase in full on each step that depends on them, one of those clauses in twenty-seven separate places, so tightening the conditions meant making the same edit at every site and missing one left that step running on a path it was meant to skip. Those steps are the ones that write the commit status the merge gate reads, so a missed site could let the gate clear a change nobody had audited, and no single pull request could surface it, because each condition guards a path a given run does not take. The conditions are now worked out once and read from one place, so there is one site to edit rather than twenty-seven, and a new test suite evaluates every step's condition against each of the eight ways the job can finish and asserts exactly which steps run, including cases that break one condition at a time to prove each still carries its weight. Separately, the helper that posts and updates the audit's status comment now ships as a committed script instead of being written out to a temporary file while the job runs, which brings it under the shell linter and gives it its own tests; it was reachable by neither before, and that mattered because every error path in it is deliberately silent, so a break would have surfaced only as a status comment that quietly stopped updating while the job stayed green. No behaviour changes on any path. **Action required:** none (#1284, #1285)
- the drift guard holding GAIA's two workspaces to one lint rule set now covers every ESLint plugin and shared config they both resolve, rather than the single package it named. The guard existed because `.gaia/cli` is its own pnpm workspace and inherits nothing from the repository root, so a plugin arriving through a floating range is frozen independently by each lockfile and the two can sit on different versions with every check green: one workspace then lints under rules the other does not have. That mechanism is a property of how a package arrives, not of which package it is, and thirty plugins and configs arrive that way, of which the guard watched one. The tools each workspace pins for itself, `eslint` and `prettier` among them, are a separate class that this does not cover: they are stated in both manifests rather than resolved through a range, so they are visible to a reader in a way a transitive resolution is not. Which packages earn parity is now decided by the npm naming convention for a rule provider rather than by a list, so one arriving later is covered without an edit, and a package deliberately left out has to say why in an exemption the guard reds on when it stops applying. Two packages had already drifted: `eslint-plugin-import-x` is converged, since both workspaces run its rules, and `@next/eslint-plugin-next` is exempt, since neither workspace loads it at all. The guard is maintainer-only and release-excluded, but the convergence moves the shipped lockfile, so a project scaffolded from GAIA now resolves `eslint-plugin-import-x` 4.17.1 in place of 4.16.2, along with four transitive bumps the re-resolve carried. **Action required:** none (#1205)
- the Chromatic workflow no longer hands the Storybook build three further environment values it cannot use. Two of them were kept on the strength of a comment saying they had to satisfy a required environment schema that the app parses when its server module loads, and that module is never loaded by a Storybook build: nothing in the story graph reaches it, so neither value could ever have satisfied the parse the comment describes. The third had no reader anywhere in the repository. The workflow ships verbatim, so every adopter carried both the dead configuration and a comment that would have told the next person to read it to keep the entries, or to add a fourth for the next key the schema requires. The guard that asserts the published preview inlines no environment values also widens to cover `SESSION_SECRET`, which it had been leaving out: it is one of the two keys the client schema deliberately withholds from the browser, so a substitution map that inlined it would publish the worst class of value the app has, and that was the one case the guard could not see. **Action required:** none for most projects, since nothing read the removed values. But if a Storybook build of yours does inline `SESSION_SECRET` into the published preview, that guard step now fails where it previously passed; pass the value to a story as an arg instead (#1263)
- `gaia init` now refuses to run anywhere except a project scaffolded from GAIA. Every one of its steps rewrites the directory it is invoked from, and every check it made asked only whether its own rewrite was possible, never whether this was the right tree, so pointing it at a parent project, a sibling checkout, or GAIA's own source rewrote that tree's package name and title in place, at exit 0, with no confirmation and no dry run. It now refuses unless the directory carries a GAIA manifest, refuses again if the directory is the GAIA template source rather than a project made from it, and names the path it resolved either way, so a wrong-directory invocation tells you which tree it read instead of silently rewriting it. The refusal covers all eight init steps rather than only the one where this was noticed, and a `--help` request still answers from anywhere. **Action required:** none, unless you drive `gaia init` from somewhere other than your project root, in which case re-run it from the root (#1242)
- the guard on `eslint.config.*` now asks you instead of refusing outright. It denied every edit to the file on the filename alone, which caught the reflex it exists to catch, silencing a lint error in config rather than fixing it in the source file, and also caught the one edit GAIA's own **Action required** tells you to make: an agent asked to add the `...lint.reactRouter` preset spread was refused and had to hand the migration back to you. Nothing about the edit separates those two cases, because they are the same shape, since ESLint applies later config objects over earlier ones and the preset groups overlap rather than partitioning the rule space, so adding a spread is both the sanctioned migration and a way to turn rules off. The separating property is what you intended, which no hook can read, so the hook now puts the call to you: every edit to `eslint.config.*` prompts for confirmation, with a reason that names both cases, points the common one back at the source file, and says the answer is yours. **Action required:** none, but note this is a genuine loosening. What was an absolute refusal is now a prompt you can approve, so what stands between a lint rule and being silenced here is your answer rather than the hook's (#1220)
- the Chromatic workflow no longer hands the Storybook build four environment values it cannot use, and it now proves the published preview carries none. Those values reached the preview only through the substitution map that went away when the preview stopped inlining environment values, so what was left read as a claim the build no longer makes, and the next person to open that block would infer a mechanism that is gone. Deleting them is the smaller half. The larger half is that nothing enforced it: the test that guards this pins the Vitest worker, while the Storybook build is a separate pipeline that test never touches, so a substitution added back to either Storybook config, to a third config, or to a plugin would bake values into a publicly published artifact with nothing failing anywhere. The workflow now builds the preview with sentinel values in the environment and fails if one survives into the output, before anything is uploaded. It costs one extra Storybook build, and only on pull requests that touch a path able to affect a preview. **Action required:** none for most projects, but if a story or component of yours depends on an environment value reaching the built preview, that build now fails; pass the value in as a story arg instead (#1211)
- the session-start janitor no longer repeats its most expensive check on a worktree it has already declined to reclaim. Reclaiming an orphaned worktree requires proving the branch's work actually landed upstream, and a worktree whose branch was abandoned rather than merged can never pass that proof; it nonetheless paid the full scan again at every session start, for as long as it sat there, and each one you keep around costs its own. The declined answer is now remembered against the two commits that could change it, the branch's tip and your base branch's remote tip, and the scan runs again the moment either moves. Only a declined answer is remembered, so the worst a stale one can do is keep a worktree rather than reclaim it, and landing the branch's work upstream moves your base tip and brings the scan back on its own (#1257)
- a pre-merge Code Audit Team member now refuses to review a file that has uncommitted changes, instead of reviewing your working copy and clearing the merge anyway. A member's clearance covers the content at `HEAD`, but it read files off disk, so auditing mid-edit could earn a clean gate for bytes nobody looked at. Each member now checks the files it is about to review and, when any of them is dirty, writes no clearance, records the paths in its findings report so you can see exactly what stopped it, and asks to be re-run once you commit or revert. The check looks only at the files that member reviews, so a change elsewhere in the tree does not stop it. In the normal flow, where the audit runs against a pushed commit, nothing changes. **Action required:** none, but this edits the audit agent definitions, so any clearance already earned on an open pull request is invalidated; re-run its audit members before merging (#1105)
- the Storybook preview no longer inlines environment values, and the test suite no longer reads your `.env`. These are one decision rather than two: the preview's env shim never survived bundling, so a published Chromatic snapshot already ran without those values while `storybook dev` had them, and a snapshot that passes against the wrong state is worse than one that fails. The same shim was the only thing that ran in the Vitest worker, where it replaced the entire environment with a five-key object, so every test file ran without `PATH` or anything else the shim did not name. Rather than push deployment values into a public artifact to close the gap, the preview now inlines nothing and `storybook dev` matches what gets published. Separately, the Vitest config was merging every value from your `.env` into the test process and forwarding it to every worker, putting `SESSION_SECRET` within reach of any test file or transitive dependency loaded there; tests now see the same environment locally that they already saw in CI, where `.env` is absent. **Action required:** none for most projects, but if a story of yours reads `window.process.env`, pass the value in as a story arg instead, and if a test of yours depends on a value from your own `.env`, set it in your shell or give it a default in `test/setup.ts` (#1210)
- the response-style guidance in `CLAUDE.md` is reorganized and gains an instruction about narrating work: say in one sentence what you are about to do before the first tool call, give updates only on a real finding or a change of direction, and lead with the outcome when finishing. The brevity guidance now sits in the terseness paragraph rather than in one of its own, and the terseness and partner-not-cheerleader guidance is unchanged in substance, only regrouped. The response-style and before-responding prose is also unwrapped to one line per paragraph, matching the rest of the file. `CLAUDE.md` is a shared file, so `/update-gaia` three-way merges it into your copy; if you have edited these sections yourself, expect those hunks to need a decision.
- GAIA's bats assertion rule now covers the trap its own advice creates. It tells you to write an absence check as a positive match on the bad case followed by `&& return 1`, which is right everywhere except as a test's last statement: there, a correctly absent needle leaves the failed match's own non-zero status as the test's result, so the test reports failure in exactly the case it exists to pass. The rule now says to end such a test with an explicit `true`, so a suite written to it cannot green on a broken assertion or red on a correct one (#1140)
- the pre-merge audit no longer opens durable tech-debt for a non-security finding that sits outside the changed lines but inside a file the pull request already touches. The waive that already existed for this only covered a fixed list of gate-machinery files, so fixing one line in an untouched file opened it to review, and that file's other pre-existing issues became filable debt: draining the backlog was filing roughly as many issues as it closed. The waive now also covers any file the pull request is already changing, and it now covers a finding raised by any Code Audit Team member, not only the default one, since four of the five members file nothing themselves and hand every out-of-scope finding to whoever is driving the merge. Genuine debt in a touched file no longer becomes durable backlog; it is recorded in the pull request body instead, visible at review time rather than afterward. A finding on a genuine gate-machinery file is still recorded whether or not the pull request touches it. **Action required:** none, but note that this changes the audit agent definitions and the shared gate machinery, which invalidates any clearance already earned on an open pull request; re-run its audit members before merging (#1137)
- the pre-merge audit's default member now reviews the same content its clearance covers. A member's clearance certifies the files at the branch's latest commit, but the default member worked out which files to review by comparing the review base against your working copy rather than against that commit, while the four specialized members compared against the commit. On a clean checkout the two agree, so this was invisible in normal use; with uncommitted edits present they diverge in three ways at once, and the most serious is silent: a change committed to the branch and then reverted in your working copy dropped out of the review entirely while the clearance still vouched for the committed version. Uncommitted work went the other way, reviewed but covered by nothing, and when the review base fell back to the default branch and that branch had moved on, files the branch never touched were pulled in as well. All five members now resolve one range, and two checks hold them there: a static one that reads the definitions, and a behavioural one that executes their real scope-resolution snippets and compares the file lists they produce. **Action required:** none, but note that this changes the audit agent definitions, which invalidates any clearance already earned on an open pull request; re-run its audit members before merging (#1099)
- the secret-scanning write guard now bounds how much it will judge in one write: at most 200 suspicious assignments, and at most 65536 characters of them, refusing anything past either. Each such line costs several processes to judge and a single line can carry thousands of judgements on its own, so an unbounded scan could run long enough to reach the time limit the editor gives a guard, and a guard that reaches its limit is cancelled rather than refused: it returns no verdict at all, and the write goes ahead unexamined. Both limits sit far above anything you would plausibly write, and a large file carrying a few such lines is unaffected, because what they measure is the material the guard judges rather than the size of the file. What they buy is a known worst case, around 90 seconds on a developer laptop for a deliberately pathological write and proportionally more on a slower machine, in place of an open-ended one (#1075)
- the secret-scanning write guard no longer refuses a line that fetches its value with a guarded command substitution and then carries a trailing comment or a second statement. Writing `export GH_TOKEN=$(gh auth token 2>/dev/null || true) # for gh cli` was denied, and the message told you to use an environment variable on a line whose whole purpose is fetching one. The same line without the comment was allowed, so the comment alone decided the verdict. The guard was reading the `||` inside the substitution as the start of the trailing part and cutting the value there, leaving a fragment it could not recognize. It now works out where to cut while the substitution's contents are hidden from it, then judges the value you actually wrote, so this admits nothing beyond the lines it was wrongly refusing. One shape stays refused: a second statement introduced by a `;` with no space before it, as in `GH_TOKEN=$(gh auth token || true); export GH_TOKEN`. A `;` is ordinary content in a `.env` value and a statement separator in a shell line, and this guard reads both kinds of file, so no reading of a bare `;` is right for both. A single value longer than a few thousand characters is also judged the old way, because the work of setting the substitution aside grows faster than the line does and a guard that stalls is worse than one that refuses (#1073)
- you can edit `.env.example` again. The secret-scanning write guard judged that file's assignments against its placeholder list, which every honest example value fails (`local`, `development`, `3001`, a localhost URL), so adding or changing one was refused with a message telling you to use a gitignored `.env` instead, which is backwards for the one file that exists to hold placeholders. GAIA's other two env guards already special-case `.env.example` by name, and this one now agrees with them. It drops the placeholder list for that file only, and puts the guard's shape test in its place rather than nothing: a value that reads like a real key, an unbroken run of 13 or more letters-and-digits, is still refused there, as are an AWS key id, a GitHub token, and a private-key header. So the file's real content writes freely while a pasted live key is still caught. The match is that exact filename, so `.env`, `.env.local`, and anything merely starting with `.env.example` are scanned exactly as before. Two accepted costs, running in opposite directions. An ordinary value that happens to carry a long unbroken letters-and-digits run, a hostname like `myapp123456789.example.com`, is refused in that file. And because the test looks for an unbroken run, a key written in segments, a UUID-style id or a base64 secret broken by `/` or `+`, reads as placeholder-shaped and is not caught there, though it is still refused in every other file (#1070)
- GAIA no longer creates or removes git worktrees; Claude Code does it natively, and GAIA's own worktree scripts and their two hook registrations are gone. You get the runtime's own handling in their place: stricter worktree-name validation, a refusal to follow a redirect symlink, and its own locking, under the same `.claude/worktrees/` layout as before. **The branch a worktree runs on is now named `worktree-<name>`, with every `/` in `<name>` written as `+`**, so a worktree requested as `debt/123-slug` shows up in `git branch` and on its pull request as `worktree-debt+123-slug`. Setting up a worktree's shared state and its generated route types is still GAIA's job, and now happens when you enter a worktree rather than when one is created, so a worktree made with a plain `git worktree add`, or one whose links you broke by hand, repairs itself the next time a session works in it
- `/update-gaia` now understands that some shipped files carry a machine-generated block, so regenerating one no longer costs you a conflict on every release. GAIA ships a few files whose content is partly written by a command rather than by hand, and until now the update compared them whole-file: the moment you ran the command GAIA itself tells you to run, your copy differed from the release copy and every subsequent update handed you a conflict patch for a difference you did not author. The update now compares those files with the generated block set aside, and regenerates it against your own configuration after the merge. Because the merge runs from your pre-update copy of the update instructions, this protection applies to your **next** update, not the one that installs it. **Action required:** after updating, run `bash .gaia/scripts/write-audit-remits.sh` once to bring the generated blocks in line with your own roster. That write changes files the pre-merge audit gate has already inspected, so any clearance already earned on an open pull request is invalidated and its audit members need to be re-run. Set `GAIA_UPDATE_NO_REGIONS=1` for a single run to turn the whole behavior off (#979)
- the pre-commit hook now runs the Quality Gate floor for a commit that only deletes files under `test/`, `.storybook/`, or `.playwright/`. Those three checks ignored deletions while the `app/` one did not, so removing a shared helper, fixture, or spec skipped typecheck, lint, and tests, and the broken types of every file that imported it surfaced only in CI. The hook is also covered by the shell linter now: it is extensionless POSIX shell, so the deterministic gate never reached it and a reviewer running shellcheck by hand got a dialect error instead of a review (#1007, #1016)
- the Storybook preview now exposes exactly the environment keys the app marks as browser-safe, and can no longer drift from them unnoticed. `SITE_URL` was still being inlined into the preview and copied onto the browser's `window.process.env` even though `clientSchema` withholds it, because the key set lived in hand-maintained lists that nothing forced to agree. Both lists are now checked against that schema at compile time, so adding, renaming, or removing a client env var fails `pnpm typecheck` until every place that inlines it agrees, instead of reaching a browser unnoticed or reading `undefined` at runtime. The Storybook Vite config also stops merging every `.env` value into the build process's environment, where any plugin, addon, or transitive dependency in that process could read it (#1018)
- Storybook no longer inlines `SESSION_SECRET` into its preview bundle. No browser code legitimately reads the key, so both the build-time substitution that baked its value in and the browser shim that consumed it are gone. A developer with a real value in a local `.env` could otherwise publish it inside a JS chunk on a public snapshot host by running `pnpm build-storybook && pnpm chromatic`, so this closes at the source what #996 closed at the CI boundary (#1000)
- the Chromatic workflow now defaults to no `GITHUB_TOKEN` scope at all and grants each job only what it needs, instead of leaving both jobs on the repository default, and it no longer hands a token to the third-party Chromatic action. At the pinned action version that token was never read: Chromatic authenticates with its own project token and reports statuses through its GitHub App, so the snapshot job needs nothing beyond `contents: read`. The workflow ships verbatim, so every adopter inherits the tighter default. The maintainer-only CLI test workflow gets the same treatment, so no workflow in the repo now leaves a job on the ambient default (#1001, #1012)
- the Chromatic workflow no longer passes the repository's real `SESSION_SECRET` into the Storybook build. Storybook never signs a cookie, so the variable only ever had to satisfy a required environment schema, and it now receives a literal placeholder instead. Nothing reached the published static build before either, but that depended on a bundler optimization rather than on the secret never being there (#996)
- the lint preset's React Router rules are now a separate, optional group, and `eslint.config.mjs` opts into it explicitly. One rule was relaxed for every file under a `routes/` folder, a path convention React Router shares with other file-based routers, so a project that swapped routers silently lost that rule across its whole route tree with nothing in the config to say why. `@gaia-react/lint` 2.0.0 moves the relaxation into a `reactRouter` block you spread yourself, which makes what your router needs visible at the top of the config rather than implied. Nothing changes for GAIA itself: no route file in the template depends on the relaxed rule. **Action required:** confirm your `eslint.config.mjs` spreads `...lint.reactRouter` after `...lint.react`, then run `pnpm lint`. If you had customized that file and resolved its conflict patch without taking the new line, any route module that destructures nothing (`({}: Route.ComponentProps)`) starts failing `no-empty-pattern` (#1049)
- ESLint now checks `.playwright/` and `.storybook/`. The lint preset ignored both by default, which silently disabled the very rules it configured for them, so every e2e spec, Playwright helper, and Storybook config file went unchecked while `pnpm lint` reported a clean tree. `@gaia-react/lint` 1.11.0 stops ignoring them, and the 78 findings that surfaced in GAIA's own tree are fixed here. **Action required:** run `pnpm lint` after updating. Most of what surfaces in your own e2e and Storybook code auto-fixes; what remains is real (#980)

- a pre-merge audit member that withholds its clearance now leaves behind everything you need to clear it. A withheld clearance is absolute and can only be retired by the member that wrote it, either by repairing what it found or by an explicit, reasoned supersede, so a refusal you cannot read is a merge nobody can unblock: you can guess at an edit, which just earns a fresh refusal, or supersede with a reason you are not in a position to state. Three things now make that impossible. Each finding a member records carries its file, line, failure mode, the evidence that established it, and the recommended repair, instead of only a class, a severity, and a directory tag. A refusal writes the carry-forward ledger the fix loop reads, so the next round is briefed on exactly what is still open and since when. And the no-op detector treats a refusal as what it is, a member that did the work and said no, rather than as a member that failed to run, which previously spent the protocol's single retry re-running an auditor that was never broken and reported the wrong diagnosis. **Action required:** none, but note that this changes the audit agent definitions, which invalidates any clearance already earned on an open pull request; re-run its audit members before merging (#1056)
- inside a linked worktree, the statusline now shows only the blocking setup prompt when one is owed, and stays quiet otherwise. The chores it used to list, and the counts they carry, are facts about the main checkout, not about the tree you happen to be in, and a tree that cannot act on them is not the place to show them. Fourteen commands and skills are main-checkout-only, and the ones that were not yet saying so now refuse out loud from a worktree, naming the main checkout and the command to get there, instead of running against the wrong tree; `/setup-gaia` is the one command that both nudges from a worktree and refuses there, so the nudge tells you it is owed and the refusal tells you where to run it. The pre-merge audit machinery now decides which checkout it is operating on from the checkout under review rather than from wherever the session happens to sit, so a pull request audited from a worktree is no longer measured against a different branch's content. **Action required:** none, but note that this changes the audit agent definitions, which invalidates any clearance already earned on an open pull request; re-run its audit members before merging (#1055)
- `/update-gaia`'s instructions now state their command-resolution rule once, completely, where the merge first applies it. That rule decides which copy of a program an update runs, the staged release's or your own working tree's, and it had been spread across four places with no single one stating it in full, so what the update understood depended on where it entered its own instructions: one site gave the rule without its exception, another the exception without the rule it excepts, and the constraint that a release-resolved command must never be printed as a follow-up for you to run by hand sat hundreds of lines from the rule it qualifies. Getting that wrong means running the release's copy of a program that has to run from yours, or printing you a command that points into an update cache a later run prunes, so it is not a path you can keep. Two narrower duplications in the same file go with it: a rule stated twice in character-identical words, so changing it meant finding both, and a rationale re-argued at three sites. The update does exactly what it did before; what changed is where its instructions say so (#1038, #1039, #1040)
- GAIA's cost telemetry no longer under-reports a run on a model its rate table does not know. The table carried no entry for Opus 5 or Mythos 5, and an unpriced model contributes nothing to a total rather than failing, so a session on either one recorded a cost missing its largest component, frequently reading as exactly $0.00, with nothing in the output to say the figure was incomplete. Both models are priced now, and a tally that still cannot price some model names it and marks its total a lower bound, on the stored record as well as on screen, so a partial figure is never presented as a confident one; that signal keys on the unpriced model rather than on a zero total, so a genuinely free run is not mislabeled. **Action required:** none unless you have run GAIA on Opus 5 or Mythos 5, in which case run `bash .gaia/scripts/cost-reprice.sh` once to re-price the rows already written under the old table. It backs the ledger up first, rewrites only rows whose price actually moves, prices each row against the rates in effect when that row was written rather than today's, and takes `--dry-run` if you want to see the effect before applying it (#1088)
- the findings report posted when you merge no longer drops four of the five pre-merge audit members. Each member files its findings under a key derived from the commit its review starts at, and the four specialized members worked that commit out differently from everyone else: once a pull request had passed one clean audit round, they kept measuring from the branch point while every other writer and every reader moved on to the commit that was cleared. The report reads one key, so it found the default member's findings, silently missed all four specialists', and posted a consolidated result short four members with nothing to say so. All five now agree on that commit, which fixes the report and, as a side effect, stops the four re-reading the whole pull request on every fix round instead of just what changed since it was last cleared. They still measure one thing from the branch point, whether the change touches their area at all, because that question is settled over the whole pull request; answering it from a narrower window could leave a member with nothing to review, no clearance to give, and a merge nobody could unblock. **Action required:** none, but note that this changes the audit agent definitions, which invalidates any clearance already earned on an open pull request; re-run its audit members before merging (#1098)
- `/update-deps` no longer bundles `typescript` with `@types/node`. A compiler bump and an ambient Node type-definition bump carry independent risk and independent migration guides, so pairing them meant one could stall or drag the other; both now move on their own lines. `typescript` is also held at the 6.0 line via `gaia.updateDepsHold`, so patches still land but 6.1 and 7.0 are not offered. TypeScript 7 stabilizes the programmatic compiler API the type-aware lint rules depend on at 7.1, not 7.0; the hold lifts when 7.1 ships and the lint toolchain supports it. (#974)
- `nanoid` moves to 6.0.0, which narrows its supported Node range to `^22 || ^24 || >=26`. GAIA's own floor stays `>=22.22.0`, so an app running a non-LTS Node 23 or 25 now sees an engine mismatch on install where it previously did not; move to an LTS release (22 or 24) to clear it. Apps on the pinned `.node-version` are unaffected. `chromatic` also moves to 18.0.1, which needs no code or CI change. (#973)
- The worktree-path guard now identifies the calling agent's working directory from the hook payload instead of inferring it from the hook process, so it keeps correctly denying the main-checkout footgun even if a future harness version ever shares process working directory across concurrent agents. No behavior change on current harness versions, where the two agree. (#938)
- The maintainer-only Comprehensive Audit phase now judges convention findings by consequence rather than provenance. Its feature-coherence lens must state what an asymmetry protects and what removing it would cost before filing, and its refuters may no longer treat "no recorded rationale" as evidence that none exists on a permission, guard, or enforcement surface, a standard that had made the audit reliably argue for the more permissive side of any undocumented boundary. Refuters also gain an `intent-dependent` verdict for a finding that is factually accurate but whose disposition turns on product intent the repo records nowhere; those surface as questions to the maintainer instead of becoming filed issues carrying confident wrong fixes. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#914)
- The shared `@gaia-react/lint` config (bumped to 1.10.0) now flags the bare `@conform-to/zod` import as a lint error: that specifier targets Zod v3 and throws at runtime, a failure typecheck, lint, and build could not otherwise catch. Import from `@conform-to/zod/v4` instead; the `/v4` subpath stays allowed, and an app carrying a bare import sees the new error on its next update. (#880)
- Bare `/speckit-clarify` now redirects to `/gaia-spec` instead of running spec-kit core's clarify loop, which wrote an off-shape artifact and enforced a 5-question cap GAIA no longer uses. Adopters who want the core clarify primitive directly lose that path (#840)
- The pre-merge Code Audit Team now handles an out-of-scope finding whose file the same PR is already editing more usefully. A non-security, prompt-shaped finding in a changed TypeScript file inside the self-heal repair boundary is repaired in-branch through the audit's existing self-heal path instead of being filed as a `tech-debt` issue the author has to triage later, and every finding still filed carries the best-effort root-cause class the audit assigns to in-ledger findings (the `holistic/unclassified` fallback is reserved for a genuine no-map) so the `/gaia-debt` clustering rule can start batching the backlog. The out-of-scope dedup now matches an already-open issue on file path and line alone, ignoring the class, so re-running the audit after a finding is reclassified never double-files. Note that a reclassified out-of-scope finding of Important severity becomes countable in the `/gaia-harden` recurrence tally. (#814)
- Each Code Audit Team member's pre-merge clearance is now keyed to a content digest over exactly the files that member owns plus the shared gate machinery, not to a digest of the whole repository tree, so an approval survives any change that could not have affected what that member reviewed. An out-of-glob edit (a CHANGELOG line, a wiki page, a merge commit from main) rotates no member's key, so every existing clearance keeps validating and nothing is re-dispatched; a change to a file a member owns rotates only that member's key, re-auditing exactly the member whose content moved; and a change to the gate machinery, anything that can alter what a member reviews, who reviews it, or whether a clearance is believed, rotates every member's key by construction. Content addressing also makes the members order-independent: they can run in parallel, and the `GAIA-Audit` trailer stamp, an empty commit that leaves every blob byte-identical, no longer voids the clearances that preceded it. The narrower key does not loosen the gate, which stays fail-closed: a refusal for the current digest is checked before the earned clearance and is absolute, a valid frontend clearance whose out-of-scope disposition sidecar is missing denies the merge, and every clearance check returns false when `jq` is absent rather than degrading to a bare-existence match. (#800)
- A Code Audit Team member that already cleared a tree is no longer re-spawned by a later commit that changes nothing that member owns, so the fix-the-findings loop and a ride-along wiki or CHANGELOG commit stop paying for a full re-audit of otherwise-unchanged code. Clearances are now written by one shared script with a versioned body that the merge gate reads instead of trusting a bare filename, so any clearance marker already sitting in a developer's pool from before this release is re-earned once. The clearance pool is retained for 72 hours past reachability, tunable via `GAIA_AUDIT_MARKER_RETENTION_HOURS`.
- The interactive Socratic ceiling in `/gaia-spec` rises from 5 to 10 substantive questions, and GAIA owns the number outright rather than inheriting spec-kit's; auto mode holds at 5. The clarify loop is decoupled from `/speckit-clarify` entirely, so no spec-kit-authored question budget reaches the driving agent, and it gains a real coverage scan (Clear / Partial / Missing over the topic bank) whose "no topic is Partial or Missing" is the stop condition, so the higher ceiling bounds the loop rather than baiting it: a simple feature still settles in the two to four questions it does today, and raising the ceiling does not raise the floor. The coaching signal is made safe for the new regime, since the intent-clarity-gap pattern no longer pools auto-mode runs with human-driven ones and now reads a session's question count against the ceiling that was in force, so raising the ceiling cannot by itself strengthen a pattern whose own advice is to lengthen the question phase; telemetry already on disk is read at the ceiling it ran under, so no adaptation changes state as a side effect. A bare `/speckit-clarify` invocation is unchanged, and the spec self-review still runs. (#694)
- `.claude/rules/code-search.md` is now the single source for the Serena-vs-Grep symbol-tool routing, including the `rename_symbol` guidance that previously lived only in the always-loaded override; `serena-cc-override.md` shrinks to a short session-start pointer that keeps only its Opus-default nudge. This removes the duplicated routing guidance that had drifted between the two rule files. (#671)
- `/gaia-fitness` and `/gaia-harden` now finish the job instead of leaving changes uncommitted for you to publish by hand. After healing and showing the report card, `/gaia-fitness` gates once on a single publish confirmation, then commits, opens a PR, and merges it on a main-branch run (commit-and-push on any other branch), the same out-of-scope-bypass flow `/gaia-audit` uses. `/gaia-harden` opens the branch and PR for you once you have approved at least one change, and offers to merge it only when you approved every candidate in the run; a selective run (any decline or defer) leaves the PR open for review. (#670)
- `/gaia-debt` now claims each drain unit with the shared `in-progress` GitHub label the moment it picks a ticket, so two concurrent drains on the same checkout stop offering each other the same work: a claimed issue drops out of the open-debt count and a peer session's `Run /gaia-debt (N issues)` nudge, and is excluded from that session's own candidate and batch offers. A drain reconciles stale claims left by an ungraceful session death before it reads the backlog (a claim stays live while a `debt/…` branch names the issue, an open PR closes it, or it was touched within a ~30-minute grace) and releases the label on a controlled stop or a security divert; `list` annotates a claimed issue `[in progress]` and `why` reports its claim status. The batch branch name changes to `debt/<members-joined-by-dash>-batch`, which names every member so the reconcile's branch check protects the whole batch. (#638)
- `/gaia-debt` now drains one drain unit per invocation rather than strictly one issue: it deterministically detects related `tech-debt` issues (same file, or same finding class within one directory) and recommends fixing a related batch together as one branch, one PR, and one audit marker that closes every issue in the unit on merge, while one-at-a-time stays an explicit, always-available choice and security-class issues are kept out of public batches. Each drain also prompts feature-branch-in-place vs git worktree the same way the plan orchestrator does, once per drain. (#635)
- `/gaia-spec`, `/gaia-plan`, and the plan-execution closeout now report cost as one pinned, terse line (`Cost: ~<total> tokens, $<dollars>, <elapsed>`, with a per-stage `(spec $… + plan $…)` breakdown when multiple stages exist) instead of a free-form agent summary that drifted run to run and, at spec/plan time, omitted the dollar figure entirely. Instruction-only: the token buckets, pricing, and ledger are unchanged, and unpriced input still degrades to `cost unavailable` rather than a fabricated number. (#634)
- unifies the spec and plan ledger status vocabularies into one set (specs `draft | ready | merged | abandoned`; plans `ready | merged | abandoned`), retiring `specified`, `allocated`, `completed`, `archived`, and `in-progress` as ledger statuses. **Action required:** none: a one-time migration rewrites every existing `specs/ledger.json` and `plans/ledger.json` row onto the unified vocabulary automatically, the next time the SessionStart janitor runs, and is idempotent, so the only visible effect is that local ledger status values change. (#609)
- a merged SPEC folder's `SPEC.md`, `AUDIT.md`, and `cost.json` are kept at merge, gated on the folder's cost being fully represented in the durable `.gaia/local/telemetry/cost.jsonl` ledger, and age-reaped by the SessionStart janitor only once past a retention window (`GAIA_SPEC_RETENTION_DAYS`, default 30); spec-less `PLAN-NNN` folders and colocated plan subfolders still delete at merge. The fail-closed pre-delete cost gate reads the `cost.json` sidecar (an unparseable sidecar still blocks a delete, the SPEC-026 invariant intact). The shared ledger write behind this is concurrency-safe under parallel worktrees (locked, with timeout-safe degradation so no row is ever lost), and a separate, human-gated one-time migration is available to clear the pre-existing `archived/` backlog under the same cost-representation guarantee. The plan orchestrator reconciles its id-ledger the moment its PR merges (new `plan-reconcile.sh` for the plan arm, `spec-reconcile.sh` for the spec arm). (SPEC-029) (#606)
- the bats-assertion-hygiene rule now also warns against a `!`-negated absence assertion used as a non-final line in a bats `@test`: POSIX `set -e` exempts a `!`-inverted command, so on every bash version the assertion silently greens even when its bad case is true. It directs the positive-match-plus-`return 1` form instead (#605)
- the audit dispatch narration in `/gaia-spec`, `/gaia-audit`, and the maintainer-only `/health-audit` challenger now names each lens in full with its id code in parentheses (e.g. `factual grounding (FG)`) instead of bare acronyms, so the workflow output says which lenses it is running in terms an adopter can read. (#599)
- `/gaia-audit` now runs the full flow `/update-deps` and `/gaia-debt` use: the up-front Apply / Discuss / Decline gate stays the single decision, and on Apply the run files any out-of-scope problem it can't fix (wiki-internal redundancy, a page-vs-page conflict, a doc that needs a rewrite) as a deduped, severity-labeled `tech-debt` issue, then commits, opens a PR, and merges it on a main-branch run (a non-main or CI run commits and pushes only; an empty diff no-ops). Findings that used to vanish into an unread Summary line now get a durable home. (#597)
- the `Run /gaia-audit` statusline nudge now names the concrete per-machine drift instead of the generic `machine drift`: it shows `N new memories` when machine-local memory has grown past the audit threshold, or `N days since review` when the last applied audit has aged out. The label tells you what accumulated and how much before you run the audit. (#589)
- amends the unreleased cost-contract data GAIA's dashboard reads: every new `cost.jsonl` record now carries `session_cwd`, ledger titles (specs `intent`, plans `subject`) use a shared first-sentence/word-safe-bounded rule instead of truncating mid-word, and plan rows gain a `status`/`completed_at` lifecycle through a new guarded chokepoint. This repo's archived `cost.md` history is backfilled into `cost.jsonl` and existing broken ledger titles are repaired. The `token-rates.json` read contract is now documented on the `Token Cost Readout` wiki page. All additive, no `schema_version` bump; pre-release, so no adopter-migration burden. (#578)
- `/gaia-debt` drain now runs the happy path start to finish like `/update-deps`: once the candidate is chosen it implements, gates, commits, opens the PR, and drives straight to merge with no separate merge-time confirmation. It still pauses only for the up-front candidate pick (two or more issues) or a genuine blocker (a security-class diversion, a rejected push, a gate that won't go green); the deterministic severity-then-age ordering, one-issue-per-invocation limit, drain-time security screen, and marker handshake are unchanged (#577)
- `/gaia-plan` and `/gaia-spec` recognize Fable as a top-tier planning model alongside Opus. On Opus or Fable the workflow proceeds without prompting; on Sonnet or Haiku it offers Opus (recommended), Fable, or the current model. `/gaia-plan` pins the spawned planner accordingly; `/gaia-spec` gains a new pre-flight gate that, because its synthesis runs on the interactive main thread and cannot pin a subagent, stops with a `/model`-and-re-run instruction instead. (#573)
- GAIA's token-cost artifacts consolidate onto a durable, versioned, self-describing `cost.jsonl` ledger for an external cost dashboard, documented on a new `Cost Data Contract` wiki page with `token-tally.sh` as its single source of truth. Also adds a terminal `abandoned` spec status with a guarded empty-draft sweep, so a never-authored draft stops resurfacing for resume. Pre-release, so no adopter-migration burden. (#569)
- the machine-local cache consolidates onto a single `.gaia/local/cache/` tree: the standalone `.gaia/cache/` is eliminated and its shared artifacts (update-check, the coaching flag, serena-guard, and `/update-gaia` tarballs) move to a worktree-symlinked `.gaia/local/cache/shared/`. Orphaned SPEC-workflow and react-perf artifacts (`gate1-*.json`, `renders.json`) are now reaped, on SPEC merge/close and by a 14-day SessionStart age-sweep backstop, and the `/update-gaia` tarball prune no longer deletes `serena-guard/` on every run. **Action required:** after updating, delete the now-dead cache directory once per clone: `rm -rf .gaia/cache` (its contents are regenerable runtime state). (#567)
- `/gaia-plan`'s pre-flight isolation prompt now offers the git-worktree option without the experimental caveat, since the worktree lifecycle is dependable as of #557. When a plan starts from a non-main branch, a worktree cut from main is now the sole isolation mode, so the concurrent-orchestrator scan and its three-option "Concurrent plan" prompt are folded away. (#566)
- `/gaia-debt` drain now presents the candidate choice through a structured `AskUserQuestion` prompt rather than a free-text override: it offers the top three backlog candidates (recommended one first) plus the built-in Other entry for a lower-ranked issue number, and skips the prompt entirely when only one `tech-debt` issue is open. The deterministic severity-then-age ordering, one-issue-per-invocation limit, drain-time security screen, and merge handshake are unchanged (#564)
- SPEC-number allocation moves from the tracked `.gaia/specs.json` ledger to immutable `spec/NNN` git tags on the remote (SPEC-021): the next number is max+1 over the union of the remote's `spec/*` tags and local signals, and a non-force tag push is the cross-machine collision lock, so a shared team no longer depends on everyone committing, pushing, and pulling a working-tree file in time. The ledger survives only as a local, gitignored per-machine cache at `.gaia/local/specs/ledger.json`. A clone without a populated `.gaia/specs.json` (i.e. a fresh scaffold) needs to do nothing: the allocator creates the local ledger on the first `/gaia-spec`, and the first online allocation seeds the remote tag namespace. **Action required:** none — `/update-gaia` performs the one-time ledger cutover for you automatically on your next update.
- a plan derived from a SPEC is colocated inside its SPEC folder at `.gaia/local/specs/<SPEC-ID>/plan/` (`plan-2`, `plan-3` for a revision) instead of living under `.gaia/local/plans/`, so plan-to-SPEC discovery is structural and the plan's merge-time disposition now follows its SPEC's. Applies to plans generated or merged after this lands; existing on-disk plan folders are not migrated (#544)
- adopt `@gaia-react/lint` 1.9.0 (from 1.8.0), which adds the `no-zod-enum` guardrail: `z.enum(...)` is now an ESLint error, use `z.literal([...])` for string unions (sorted alphanumerically). This makes the Zod convention a deterministic lint failure instead of a discretionary review note. **Action required:** convert any existing `z.enum([...])` schemas in your app to `z.literal([...])` to keep `pnpm lint` green (#540)
- plan-execution token and wall-clock accounting now records execute cost from the orchestrator's own git commits and pushes through a `PreToolUse` hook instead of a manual, instruction-driven tally, so no phase is double-counted; it aggregates the whole execution across every session and dedups re-invocation rows on read. A new merge-time `PostToolUse` hook renders a full-cycle spec / plan / execute / total cost breakdown at `gh pr merge` (the spec line omitted for a spec-less plan), and a new `.gaia/scripts/token-rollup.sh` reader surfaces the same roll-up on demand (#539)
- `/gaia-plan` now pins each plan-execution task sub-agent to Sonnet by default instead of letting it inherit the orchestrator session's model (typically Opus). The feature's complexity is resolved upstream in `/gaia-spec` and `/gaia-plan` and their audits, so execution runs faster and cheaper on the lighter model; the planner can still escalate a specific deep-synthesis phase to Opus, naming which and why in `ORCHESTRATOR.md` (#538)
- `/update-deps` completes the flow on a `main`/`master` run: it now writes the update commit with a load-bearing `chore(deps)` subject (previously Phase 8 referenced "the update commit" but never wrote one), opens the PR, and merges it once the required checks are green (`--auto` under branch protection), then verifies the terminal `MERGED` state and cleans up the local checkout. A run on any other branch (or in CI) is unchanged: it pushes and leaves the PR to the branch owner. The `chore(deps)` subject clears the merge gate's dep-bump bypass, so the PR stays turnkey-mergeable without a code-review-audit marker (#534)
- GAIA's Serena code-search routing guidance is now language-agnostic: the advisory `code-search` rule nudges toward Serena's LSP-backed symbol tools for symbol queries in any language Serena indexes for the project (not only TypeScript, and no longer scoped to `app/`/`test/`), so an adopter who configures another language server gets the same routing. The enforcement guard stays deliberately TypeScript-conservative and tsconfig-gated, since a wrong hard-block on a non-TS search is worse than a miss while the rule only nudges (#533)
- `/gaia-debt` drives its fix PR through the standard PR Merge Workflow to completion instead of stopping after `gh pr create` for a human to merge by hand: it confirms intent (open-only or merge, defaulting to merge), then resolves the marker handshake, the maintainer-only CHANGELOG gate, the merge (`--auto` under branch protection, never `--admin`), and the post-merge verify-and-cleanup itself. The `code-review-audit` marker gate is unchanged, the skill drives the merge and never bypasses, fakes, or pre-empts it (#523)
- `/gaia-spec`'s closing handoff now prints a single copy-pasteable block: the `/gaia-plan` command and its SPEC argument together, prefaced by a `/clear`-and-paste instruction, instead of a bare argument the user had to prefix with the command by hand (#519)
- catch the README up with shipped changes: correct the tech stack to React Router 8 and the Node engine floor to `>=22.22.0` (both moved with the React Router 8 major), and document `/gaia-debt` in the commands table and the tech-debt section (#517)
- `/setup-gaia` gates the Claude GitHub App install before its CI verification run: because every GAIA CI workflow authenticates as that app, the command now walks you through installing it (skipping the prompt when it detects the app already present on your org) and waits for confirmation, instead of dispatching a run that fails with a 401 and only then telling you what was missing. You can also skip verification and finish setup, then trigger a run from the Actions tab once the app is installed (#516)
- `/setup-gaia` finalizes greenfield CI setup by committing GAIA's own CI-install workflows directly to the default branch (no PR and no audit, since that commit has nothing to review), so first setup no longer waits on or wedges behind an audit run; the direct push accounts for repository rulesets and recovers cleanly when one blocks it, and the block-main guard stands down only for that single commit via a self-healing, gitignored sentinel. Its audit-mode onboarding now opens with a solo-or-team choice: solo engineers run audits locally by default (CI kept as a failsafe), and teams get a single Local-recommended-or-CI choice (#515)
- `/setup-gaia` now asks where a new GitHub repo should live, your personal account or one of your organizations, instead of silently creating it under your personal namespace; organizations are offered ranked by your own recent commit activity, and the prompt is skipped entirely when you belong to none. `/gaia-init`'s closing message drops the stale "create your GitHub repo, then run /setup-gaia" step now that repo creation lives inside `/setup-gaia` (#512)
- onboarding is now a single `/setup-gaia` command, replacing `/setup-gaia-ci` and `/setup-cloned-gaia-project`. It detects your situation (fresh clone, first adopter, partial run, or fully provisioned), runs only the phases still owed, provisions the GitHub repository (private by default), and is safe for any teammate to re-run after cloning. CI-token setup is out-of-band and never asks you to paste a secret into the chat: it reuses a `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` or `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` the repo or org already exposes when present (by name only, never by value), otherwise you set the secret yourself and the command only verifies it exists. **Action required:** run `/setup-gaia` instead of the two retired commands; `/update-gaia` surfaces the removal of the old command files with a per-file confirm prompt on your next update (#478, #480, #509)
- `/gaia-init` setup-flow polish. It auto-detects the user's GitHub handle for `.github/CODEOWNERS` via `gh api user` instead of always writing the `REPLACE-WITH-YOUR-GITHUB-HANDLE` placeholder (on failure it keeps the placeholder and the required-follow-up warning). The i18n and CI prompts are polished, and `/gaia-init` and `/setup-cloned-gaia-project` strip React Doctor's bundled extras after install (standalone Actions workflow, commit hook, `doctor` package script, pinned devDependency), since GAIA already triggers react-doctor via the skill and the `code-review-audit` agent at `@latest` (#454, #507)
- `/gaia-fitness` (and `/health-audit`'s shared fitness bucket) now dispatch each judgment-auditor with an explicit coverage directive in its prompt (surface every candidate, including uncertain or low-severity ones; the Orchestrator's adjudication stage is the filter), so recall no longer rides on the auditor's own bar. Documented in `wiki/decisions/Claude Integration Fitness.md` (#486)
- adopt `@gaia-react/lint` 1.8.0 (from 1.5.1), reconciling every lint delta across the repo. The headline behavior change: `no-null-render` now autofixes `return null` to `return undefined` inside render functions only (loaders, actions, and utils untouched; structural `null` left intact), and a report-only selector flags the `.length && <JSX/>` numeric-0 leak. The bundled toolchain (`typescript-eslint`, `eslint-plugin-sonarjs`, unicorn) moves in step and the surfaced findings are resolved (#433, #441, #461)
- the `prepare` lifecycle no longer provisions Playwright browsers: `pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps` moves to a dedicated `pnpm install:browsers` script, so `pnpm install --frozen-lockfile` no longer triggers an `apt-get` that can fail behind a proxy or on a hardened runner. **Action required:** run `pnpm install:browsers` once to provision Playwright browsers locally if you run the e2e suite (`pnpm install` no longer does it for you) (#456)
- the fresh-scaffold visual baseline is now neutral and brand-free (SPEC-008): the `--color-claude-*` terracotta scale is replaced by a zero-chroma `--color-primary-*` scale so re-skinning is a few-value change, Google Fonts are dropped for system stacks, and `Layout` is simplified to a single `<main>` wrapper. A path-scoped `design-baseline.md` guard and a `Design System.md` stub keep Claude from reading the neutral baseline as a chosen design system. **Adopters who customized owned files** (`Header`, `Footer`, `GaiaLogo`, or the bundle) will see a migration impact in `/update-gaia`; those files are removed upstream (#455)
- routine dependency refresh across several waves: `@types/node` to 26.0.0 (types-only, runtime stays on Node 22), plus the `storybook` group, `@playwright/test`, `tailwindcss`, `vitest`, `@faker-js/faker`, `chromatic`, `happy-dom`, `nanoid`, and further minor/patch bumps. Transitive `undici`, the nested `vite` 7.x copy, and the `qs` security-floor override reached their patched floors or became redundant through the bumps, so those interim overrides were pruned and `pnpm audit` stays clean (#387, #413, #415, #452)
- upgrade React Router 7.18.0 → 8.0.1 across the family (`react-router`, `@react-router/*`), a low-risk "boring major": GAIA already adopted all five v8 future flags on v7 and imports everything from `react-router`, so router runtime behavior is unchanged at cutover and the now-invalid `future` block in `react-router.config.ts` is deleted. It also bumps `remix-utils` to 10.0.0 and `remix-i18next` to 8.0.0 (both peer `react-router` ^8 natively), and the nested `vite` 7.x toolchain copy disappears because `@react-router/dev@8` widens its vite peer to ^7||^8, so the dev-only audit advisory has nothing left to flag (closes #398). **Action required:** raise your local Node to >=22.22.0 (the v8 engine floor; `.nvmrc`/`.node-version` pin 22.23.1 on the 22.x LTS line) (#444, #450)
- catch the README up for the next release: add `/gaia-react-perf` and `/gaia-harden` to the commands table, add `a11y-fixes` to the bundled-skills lists, and refresh the lint-rule count (#449)
- replace the handoff archive lifecycle with delete-on-done. `/gaia-handoff` enforces a single-handoff invariant (it deletes any existing handoff before writing, carrying forward anything unfinished), and `/gaia-pickup` resolves it in place, self-deleting on the happy path and leaving outstanding work untouched so an interruption stays recoverable, instead of moving consumed handoffs into an `archive/` directory. Deletion is the only terminal state; nothing is archived. **Action required:** remove any orphaned archive directory left by the prior workflow with `rm -rf .gaia/local/handoff/archive` (gitignored local scratch, safe to delete) (#448)
- split the spec-session lifecycle from the SPEC artifact's status: the spec ledger tracks the authoring session while `SPEC.md` frontmatter keeps its own (`in-progress | reopened | closed`). `/gaia-spec` resume only offers a still-`draft` session, so a finalized SPEC is never re-surfaced as resumable, and a fail-open reconcile pass derives the `merged` transition from a merged PR on git ground truth (#423)
- route GAIA workflow-misfire reports (hooks, skills, commands, quality gate, scaffold failures) through `/gaia-forensics` before filing by hand, and document an issue-claiming policy in `CONTRIBUTING.md`: only issues labeled `help wanted` are open for outside PRs (#404)
- the add-locale runbook no longer adds new-locale files to `.gaia/manifest.json` (those files are adopter-owned), and the remove-i18n and `/update-gaia` manifest steps now write through an exemption marker so they still complete under the new guard. The guard registers in `.claude/settings.json`, a shared, non-field-aware file, so it activates once that file merges cleanly; a drifted adopter must resolve the settings conflict first
