- the merge-gate auditor used to ask for comments on complex logic, which rewards volume over usefulness; it now applies a comment-worthiness standard instead, a test of subject rather than length: a comment must be about something the file does not already contain. It flags a comment that talks about the file itself, names something no longer present, or restates the line below it, and holds a comment that names something to a much stricter bar than one that explains something, since a pointer rots and an explanation does not. The same standard is now a rule Claude loads while it writes code rather than only a bar the auditor applies at merge time, it covers shell and test files as well as TypeScript, and it says what to do when you edit an existing comment (never truncate a pointer down to an id nothing resolves; delete the line instead). GAIA's own tree was brought up to the standard as part of this change, so your next update may surface comment-only merges on files you've customized

### Removed

- the `debt:pre-provenance` label, a one-time cohort marker that recorded which tech-debt issues predated origin-provenance tracking. The distinction it drew had no consumer and no ongoing value, while the label itself kept costing something: it was the registry's only deprecated entry, so `gaia labels sync` printed a standing advisory about it on every run, forever, and a standing advisory that must never be acted on teaches you to skim the output where the actionable warnings also appear. It also sat on a delete path with no carrier guard, one plausible flag away from being removed by accident rather than by decision. The cohort itself is preserved as prose on the removal pull request, so the fact survives without a label holding it. The filing gate that policed the marker loses that arm and, with it, an extra network round-trip on each of its two advisory modes; the registry's retirement vocabulary is kept as-is for the next name that has to be withdrawn (#1531)
- the orphaned `setup-ci set-secret` CLI verb, superseded by #478's out-of-band token provisioning: dead-but-advertised surface and a latent paste-reintroduction vector (invoking it required an agent to hold the CI bot token on stdin). Deleted outright; maintainer CLI surface, so release-excluded (#479)
- `app/components/Header/`, `app/components/Footer/`, `app/components/GaiaLogo/`, and `app/assets/images/gaia-logo.svg` from the template: these GAIA-branded surfaces are removed from the scaffold rather than shipped and stripped at init, with the neutral index + `Layout` in their place. Adopters who customized any of these owned files will be offered a migration note by `/update-gaia`; the maintainer logo the README references was relocated to `docs/assets/gaia-logo.svg`, outside `app/` so it never enters the app build or the distributable (#455, #458)
- the vestigial `react-router-dom` dependency, a v6/v7 re-export shim that React Router 8 drops entirely; GAIA runs framework mode and imports everything from `react-router` (`HydratedRouter` comes from `react-router/dom`), so it was already dead weight. `/update-gaia` leaves an existing `react-router-dom` in your `package.json` by design (adopter-owned dependencies are never auto-removed); `pnpm knip` also flags it as unused (#419)
  - **Action required:** run `pnpm remove react-router-dom` to drop it
- the `/gaia-spec` GitHub-issue mirror and the inline `/gaia-plan` chain (SPEC-012). The mirror is gone end to end (the opt-in question, auto-mode force-on, resume re-ask, mirror step, PR-body-closure, frontmatter stamping, and the `gh-mirror.sh` script). The chain is replaced by a handoff: after the canonical save `/gaia-spec` prints a copy-pasteable `/gaia-plan` handoff prompt and stops, so the human runs `/gaia-plan` in a fresh session with a clean context budget (#484)
- GAIA's adaptive mentorship layer and the structured event pipeline that fed it (`gaia mentorship enable|disable|status|purge`), an opt-in, local-only feature that adapted Claude's responses to observed dev-workflow patterns over time. It shipped off by default, nothing in the shipped surface read the stream it wrote or transmitted it anywhere, and it charged every new adopter a blocking privacy decision during `/setup-gaia`'s first run before they had any signal the feature was worth the ask. The four retired commands are `gaia mentorship enable`, `gaia mentorship disable`, `gaia mentorship status`, and `gaia mentorship purge`: drop any script that calls one. **Action required:** none — a one-time SessionStart janitor sweep destroys the mentorship residue on your next session start, including the cloud event stream, which accumulated regardless of whether mentorship was ever opted into. A locally drifted setup document that still passes `--mentorship` to `gaia ping` will fail that command's flag parse.

### Fixed

- the two wiki pages describing GAIA's cross-workspace lint guards no longer tell adopters that the `eslint` and `prettier` versions each workspace declares are a class the parity guard leaves uncovered. They are covered now: the guard asserts both workspaces declare the same version of each tool, alongside the shared lint-config pin it already held, and the two versions that had drifted apart are converged. What decided the shape of that guard was a measurement rather than an assumption, because the question it turns on is whether two versions of a formatter can disagree at all. Two Prettier releases one patch series apart produced byte-identical output across the entire tracked TypeScript corpus, and disagreed on exactly one Markdown construct, where the older one truncated a link anchor at its first colon and silently discarded the rest of the heading. So the guard asserts the declared versions match and deliberately stops short of asserting the two behave alike, since the stronger claim is the one just shown to be false. The pages also now state the guard's own limit, that it needs a version on both sides to compare and so cannot reach a package only one workspace declares. Those pages ship, so the correction reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia`; the guard itself and the workspace it guards are maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs either (#1268)

- every hook GAIA registers now resolves from the repository root instead of from the shell's working directory. The registrations were relative, and a `cd` in a Bash call persists for the rest of the session, so a single one left every hook script unfindable; a script that cannot be found exits 127, which is not the status that blocks, so the whole guard layer went silent while every tool call carried on. Under `bypassPermissions` those guards are the only layer there is, which makes this a last line of defence failing open rather than a redundancy degrading. A check ships alongside it that holds every registration to a form resolving independently of the working directory, so an adopter who adds a hook of their own can confirm it is not registered by a bare relative path: run `bash .gaia/scripts/check-hook-command-rooting.sh .` after editing your registrations. It is a hand run rather than an automatic one, because every runner that would invoke it is maintainer-only. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1740)
- `pnpm format` no longer rewrites files outside the working tree it was run in. The script pointed Prettier at `.prettierignore` explicitly, and that flag replaces Prettier's default ignore set rather than adding to it, so `.gitignore` stopped being consulted at all while the glob stayed anchored at the repository root. Everything `.gitignore` covers was in scope for a `--write`: build output and coverage, the `.gaia/local` working state a provisioned worktree reaches through a symlink, and, worst of all, every live git worktree under `.claude/worktrees/`. On a checkout with two worktrees open that was 764 files, 274 of them inside another session's isolated, in-flight tree. The write lands with no error and no indication which tree it touched, which is exactly the separation the worktree model exists to hold, and it walked around the guards that hold it because formatting never goes through a tool call they see. The script now passes no ignore path at all, which restores Prettier's own default of reading `.gitignore` and `.prettierignore` together; no tracked file in this repository loses coverage, checked rather than assumed: Prettier applies `.gitignore`'s patterns and has no notion of the index, so a file force-added while matching one would quietly stop being formatted, and this tree carries none. Pre-commit was never affected, as `lint-staged` scopes Prettier to staged files, and `pnpm lint` escaped only by accident, because its ESLint config feeds `.gitignore` through `includeIgnoreFile`. A guard now holds the script to an ignore set that still consults `.gitignore`, and holds `.gitignore` to still covering the worktree root the first half leans on. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1722)

- the Storybook and vitest router stub GAIA ships now registers every path the app submits a fetcher to. It registered `/actions/set-theme`, a path no route file, form action, or fetcher matcher in the project resolves, under a comment stating the entry existed to keep a theme submission from erroring. Any story or test that clicked the theme-switch button therefore got a 404 and the router's error boundary in place of the component, after reading a line promising that could not happen, and nothing caught it because no test clicked the button. The stub now registers `/resources/theme-switch`, and a test clicks the button through a stub-decorated story so the stub and the component cannot silently diverge again. The language select's `/actions/set-language` was missing from the stub for the same reason and is registered alongside it; that one is latent on a single-locale project, where the select renders nothing, and becomes reachable as soon as a second locale is added. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1708)

- GAIA CI's post-merge watch now tells an unreadable CI query apart from a genuine timeout. Every way the query it polls GitHub with can fail, an expired token, a rate limit, a network fault, a repository the token cannot see, was reported as the watch running out of time, which sends an operator to raise a deadline that cannot fix any of them and leaves the message asserting a cause they have already ruled out. The watch now reports a distinct `query-failed` conclusion and carries the underlying error into the job log as an error annotation, so `timeout` keeps meaning timeout and the failing step says which of the two it hit. Adopters running GAIA CI get this on their next `/update-gaia` (#1700)

- GAIA's release staging now refuses a tracked path holding a literal newline instead of resolving it by chance. Staging read the tracked set as a NUL-delimited stream, which is what stops git rewriting a non-ASCII path into a spelling that names no file, and then translated it straight back to newlines, because both consumers downstream are newline-oriented and macOS ships an rsync with no `--from0`. A path holding a newline is the one input that conversion cannot represent: it splits into two records, and what happens next depends on what those two halves happen to name. When either names nothing, rsync exits 23 and the release aborts on a `stat` error naming a path fragment nobody wrote. When both happen to exist, or the exclude filter drops both, or any mix of the two, rsync exits 0 and the tarball publishes without the file while `.gaia/manifest.json` records it as shipping, because the manifest's exclude patterns are compiled without the multiline flag and so cannot match a spelling that spans a line break. So the same defect was an illegible blocked release in one direction and a silent divergence between the published tarball and its own manifest in the other. All five sites that built that list, the release workflow, the three distribution-harness scripts, and the staging build the health runbook prescribes, now discover through one shared boundary that refuses such a path outright and names it with the newline rendered, which is the only repair that behaves the same way in both directions and the only one available on the macOS half of the surface. A comment in the release workflow asserting the opposite, that the file would be dropped without failing the release, is corrected in the same pass. Latent rather than live, since no tracked path holds a newline today; a check now holds the tree to the boundary rather than to a list of sites, so a sixth one is covered without anyone naming it. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone stages a release or runs the guard (#1669)

- `gaia wiki commit-classify` now sees a commit that edits a wiki page whose name carries a non-ASCII character. It read the commit's touched-path set with a bare `git diff-tree` and split the result on newlines, so under git's default quoting a page such as `wiki/日本語.md` came back C-quoted, as a spelling whose first character is a double quote. The classifier decides whether a commit is worth narrating by prefix-testing each path against `wiki/decisions/`, `wiki/concepts/`, and their siblings, and a quoted spelling fails every one of those tests, so a commit whose only wiki edit was to such a page was classified as not touching the wiki at all. Nothing threw and nothing logged, so the wrong answer was reported exactly as confidently as a right one, and the page it describes drops out of the sync that would have narrated it. A page whose name holds a literal newline was worse still, splitting into two entries that name no file. The reader now uses the NUL-terminated form the base-tree reader, the label checker, and the manifest builder already use, which recovers the real name for every path git rewrites and keeps a newline-bearing path as one record. Latent rather than live, since no tracked path carries a non-ASCII byte today, but the trigger is ordinary, a `wiki/` page whose title carries an accented or CJK character, which the naming convention permits. This is the fourth call site of the same class, after the manifest builder, the label checker, and the base-tree reader; that it keeps recurring one site at a time is now tracked on its own. `wiki commit-classify` ships in the adopter CLI, so the misclassification reached an adopter's own wiki and the repair reaches them on their next `/update-gaia` (#1682)

- `gaia wiki diff-size` now counts the lines changed on a wiki page git recorded as a rename. The command reads its changed-line total from a NUL-terminated `git diff --numstat` stream and split that stream into independent records, which is correct for an ordinary change and wrong for a rename: a rename leaves the record's path field empty and emits the old and new names as two further records, so the counts and the name they belong to land in different pieces and none of the three matches what the reader was looking for. Every line added to or removed from a renamed page was therefore dropped from the numerator, silently and with no partial credit. A wiki restructure is exactly the change that renames pages and edits them in the same pass, and it is also the largest kind of wiki change there is, so the gate reported the smallest ratio precisely when the real one was biggest, and automatic merging proceeded on a diff that should have been held for a human. The stream is now walked as a stream, so a rename's counts are attributed to the new name and a page whose name carries a tab is no longer truncated on the way through. This is the numerator half of the same fault repaired in the denominator by gaia-react/gaia#1671, and both made the gate read a wiki as smaller than it is. `wiki diff-size` ships in the adopter CLI and gates automatic merging, so the under-count reached an adopter's own wiki and the repair reaches them on their next `/update-gaia` (#1675)

- `gaia wiki diff-size` now measures every wiki page, including one whose name carries a non-ASCII character. It listed the base tree's blobs with a bare `git ls-tree` and split the result on newlines, so under git's default quoting a page such as `wiki/日本語.md` came back C-quoted, as a spelling that names no blob. The line count for that page was then read with `git cat-file` against the quoted spelling, which failed, and the failure was swallowed as a count of zero. The page contributed nothing to the denominator, so the gate under-reported the wiki's real size and a change could clear a size threshold it should have crossed: a decision reported as confidently as a correct one, on a wiki the command never finished measuring. A page whose name holds a literal newline was worse still, splitting into two entries that parse as no blob at all and dropping out entirely. The reader now uses the NUL-terminated form the manifest builder and the label checker already use, which recovers the real name for every path git rewrites and keeps a newline-bearing path as one record. Latent rather than live, since no tracked path carries a non-ASCII byte today, but the trigger is ordinary, a `wiki/` page whose title carries an accented character, which the naming convention permits. `wiki diff-size` ships in the adopter CLI and gates automatic merging, so the under-count reached an adopter's own wiki and the repair reaches them on their next `/update-gaia` (#1671)

- `gaia labels check` now reads every tracked file, including one whose name carries a non-ASCII character. It listed the tree with a bare `git ls-files` and split the result on newlines, so under git's default quoting a path such as `wiki/日本語.md` came back C-quoted, as a spelling that names no file on disk. The scan joined that spelling onto the repository root, the read failed, and the failure was swallowed: the file was skipped with no diagnostic and every label literal in it went unread. That is the worst direction for a check whose whole job is to catch a label name absent from the registry, because a green run is indistinguishable from a run that never opened the file. The exclusion list was defeated the same way, since it matches literal paths. The reader now uses the NUL-terminated form the manifest builder already uses, which recovers the real name for every path git rewrites and keeps a path holding a literal newline as one entry rather than two names that match nothing. Latent rather than live, since no tracked path carries a non-ASCII byte today, but the trigger is ordinary, a `wiki/` page whose title carries an accented character, which the naming convention permits. `labels check` ships in the adopter CLI, so the silent skip reached an adopter's own tree and the repair reaches them on their next `/update-gaia` (#1667)

- GAIA's manifest builder now reads the tracked file list the same way release staging does, so a file whose name carries a non-ASCII character can be kept out of the distribution at all. It read `git ls-files` with neither `-z` nor `core.quotepath=false`, and under git's default quoting any such path comes back C-quoted, as a spelling that names no file on disk: a page called `café.md` arrives as `"caf\303\251.md"`. Everything downstream then worked on that spelling, and two things followed, both silent. No line in the distribution boundary could ever mask the file, because those patterns compile from the literal path, so it was recorded as shipping no matter what the maintainer answered, and the answer gate offered the mangled name as the thing to answer about, a name it then refused to accept because it carries a backslash and a quote. Meanwhile release staging already read the NUL stream, applied the exclusion, and left the file out of the tarball, so the manifest and the tarball disagreed about what shipped and an adopter's `/update-gaia` would read an entry for a file the release does not contain. The reader now uses staging's own spelling, which recovers the real name for every path git rewrites, a control character or a double quote as much as an accent. Latent rather than live, since no tracked path carries a non-ASCII byte today, but the trigger is ordinary, a `wiki/` page whose title carries an accented character, which the naming convention permits. The builder is maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it; the manifest it writes ships (#1662)

- GAIA's release CLI can now withhold a wiki page from the adopter distribution. The distribution boundary has exactly one legitimate writer, and the flag that writes it rejected any path carrying whitespace, so no page under `wiki/` could be withheld at all: every decision page's title carries spaces. The rejection was over-broad rather than protective, and the two validators over the same boundary disagreed about it. The one that reads the committed file rejects only surrounding whitespace and a set of glob and regex metacharacters, so it accepts an interior space and always has, which is why five such lines already sit in the file; the answer gate rejected every whitespace character, so the CLI could read a line it refused to write. An exclude line is a literal path to everything downstream of it, compiled into an anchored regex and applied by `grep` and `rsync`, and a space is literal in all three, which the boundary's existing space-bearing entries demonstrate by masking their files correctly today. The gate now mirrors the reader: a trim comparison for surrounding whitespace, plus an explicit refusal of a line break, which stays rejected on its own merits rather than by inheritance, since a newline would emit a second, uncommented exclude line that nothing had checked membership for. The diagnostic named only the metacharacter case out of the six conditions reaching that branch, which sends a maintainer hunting for a glob character in a path whose real problem is a leading space, so it now names the whole set. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it (#1661)

- every Code Audit Team member can now write its findings report on a pull request reviewed from a git worktree. The five member specs prescribed that write one way, as a shell heredoc feeding the shared writer, and worktree isolation refuses a heredoc outright, so on any worktree-based review the single prescribed form could not run at all. Nothing about that failure is visible downstream. The findings file is each member's report of record, and the detector that tells a real review from one whose report went missing reads exactly that file, so a member whose write fails is classified as having returned nothing and burns the one retry the gate allows on a review that actually finished. What happened instead is that each member improvised its own way past the refusal and both improvisations happened to work, which is the drift the single-writer design exists to prevent, arriving through a form that could not be followed rather than through one nobody read. All ten sites now use a single-quoted `printf` piped to the writer's existing standard-input path, which runs under isolation, stages no file, and keeps a `$` or a backtick inside a finding literal the way the old quoted delimiter did; an apostrophe inside a finding is the one character that now needs escaping, and the specs say so. The guard that had frozen the old form is rebuilt around the new one and each of its checks was driven to failure before being relied on. Adopters run the same audit team under the same isolation, so this reaches them on their next `/update-gaia` (#1654)

- the guard holding GAIA's pre-commit hook and its lint-staged config in step now reds on a config it cannot read, rather than passing on a directory it read wrong. To compare the two files the guard reduces each lint-staged glob key to the directory it hands ESLint, and it took that directory by cutting the key at its first `/**/`, so a key carrying a second recursive segment lost everything past the first one: `app/**/routes/**/*.ts` reduced to a bare `app/`. The guard then reported the `app/` hook arm as covered, when the key actually hands ESLint only `app/<any>/routes/<any>/*.ts` and leaves `app/other.ts` unlinted. That is a silent green in exactly the case the check exists to catch, and it is the dangerous half of the pair: the sibling direction's defect was a false red, which fails closed, while this one lets an uncovered directory merge under a guard reporting clean. The reader now refuses a key with a second recursive segment outright, so it yields no directory and reds both directions, which is the posture the file states for every other key shape it cannot read. Refusing the shape is not interchangeable with cutting at the last `/**/` instead, which would red a doubled globstar that is legitimately the same directory as one, and a test case pins that difference. No key in the current config has the shape, so what this removes is a trap set for whoever writes the first one. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it (#1652)

- GAIA's tech-debt provenance line no longer promises to record who filed a finding. Its `mode` and `unit` fields are derived from the branch name alone, and three shipped documents described them as recording the work or the session behind the filing, a stronger claim than the derivation supports and a false one whenever a session files from a checkout parked on someone else's branch. That is observed rather than predicted: four issues filed while an unrelated drain held its branch checked out all carry that drain's unit, and two separate readers later attributed those findings to work that had nothing to do with them, each reading the field names exactly as the prose invited. The derivation itself is unchanged and stays branch-derived on purpose, since it is cheap, survives branch deletion, and is right the large majority of the time; what changes is that the contract now states outright that `mode` and `unit` describe the checked-out branch rather than the filer, that concurrent work in one checkout inherits that branch's stamp, and that neither field is authorship. The capability manifest's justification for the helper is corrected in the same pass: it described a parser reading issue bodies and denied any git access, where the helper emits the line and makes two read-only git reads, so a maintainer auditing the permission grant was reading the wrong script. The declared capability set was and remains correct; only the justification was wrong. The filing skill, the provenance helper, and the manifest all ship, so adopters get the corrected prose on their next `/update-gaia` (#1288)

- `gaia labels sync` now applies both halves of a registry edit that renames a label and recolors it. The rename landed and the color did not: color reconciliation only ever reached an entry the repository already carries under its registry name, and a renamed entry is by definition absent under that name, so the new color surfaced on the next run as ordinary color drift, which sync reports and leaves alone unless the operator passes `--adopt`. Nothing distinguished that leftover from a recolor the operator had chosen themselves, so the honest reading of the advisory was to ignore it, and a label could sit at its old color indefinitely while the registry and the pull request that changed it both claimed otherwise. A rename now carries the registry's color in the same `gh` call, under no flag: sync cannot tell a registry recolor from an operator's own, so it resolves a renamed entry's color in the registry's favour rather than leaving it unreconciled indefinitely, accepting that an operator's recolor of the old name does not survive the rename. An unmanaged entry's color is still never touched, and a recolor of a label the registry did not rename still waits on `--adopt` exactly as before. The CLI ships, so adopters get the fix on their next `/update-gaia` (#1532)

- the guard holding GAIA's pre-commit hook and its lint-staged config in step no longer reds on a config that is fine. The two files are halves of one contract: an arm decides the gate runs at all, a glob decides lint-staged has anything to hand ESLint, and a directory that one covers and the other misses is how a commit lands unlinted and untypechecked. To compare them the guard reduces each glob key to the directory it covers, and it refused any key whose directory was nested deeper than a single segment, so `app/routes/**/*.ts` reduced to nothing and the check failed saying the key had no recursive directory to work from. It plainly has one, and the repair that message sends its reader toward, rewrite the key, is wrong a second time over: the hook's arms are deliberately unanchored substring greps, so the `app/` arm already reaches every file the nested key names. The reader now accepts a nested directory, and the direction that checks a glob's directory against the arms now models those greps rather than demanding an exact name. The opposite direction still demands one, because there a nested key genuinely does leave part of an arm's directory unlinted, which is the miss that direction exists to catch. It failed closed and no key in the current config reached it, so what this removes is a trap set for whoever added the first nested key. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it (#1642)

- GAIA's pre-merge audit no longer refuses a merge over a commit it did not make. The reviewer withholds its clearance whenever it repaired something itself, and it tracks that with a flag it sets on its own repairs, but nothing in its instructions said the flag was the only admissible evidence. So when the orchestrator applied a repair the reviewer had reported and is forbidden to make itself, the reviewer read that new commit as evidence of a boundary breach, refused a pass whose precondition was actually met, and named a read-only helper as the culprit. A refusal is a first-class artifact only its author can retire, so clearing it cost a full extra review round plus an operator acknowledgement, on a branch where nothing was wrong. The reviewer's instructions now state that the flag is the sole admissible evidence and that a commit merely observed on the branch is never evidence of a repair, because the orchestrator owns every commit on that path and the cross-remit repair in question is exactly what the merge workflow prescribes. The reviewer definition ships, so adopters get the correction on their next `/update-gaia` (#1637)

- the comments on the react-perf capture harness GAIA ships now describe what the code does. Four of them described behavior the code beside them does not have: the file header credited the 5000ms install-check timeout with defusing a `window.stop()` landmine when that timeout is the only thing in the file that calls `window.stop()`, the block comment on the renderer inspection promised a production-build abort that its own call site had made unreachable, and two more narrated a dependency bump rather than the code, which goes stale at the next one. The unreachable production branch is deleted along with the internal `productionDetected` diagnostics field it set and the capture-side consumer that read it; a production target still fails one step earlier with a message naming the same remedy, so no capture an adopter runs behaves differently. References to a planning-document phase that has since landed, and to a capture option spelled by a name that exists nowhere, are replaced with the command an adopter can run and the option a reader can grep. The three harness files ship, so adopters get the corrections on their next `/update-gaia` (#1634)

- the Dockerfile GAIA ships now runs a Node version at or above the floor the project declares. All four build stages pinned `node:22.19-alpine`, below the `>=22.22.0` that `package.json`'s `engines.node` has required since the React Router 8 upgrade, and nothing caught it: local development and CI both resolve Node from `.node-version`, and no workflow in the repository builds this Dockerfile, so a green run said nothing about the image an adopter deploys. Anything relying on behavior landed after 22.19 would have passed everywhere it was exercised and failed only in the container. The four pins now read `node:22.23-alpine`, matching `.node-version`. The Dockerfile ships as an owned file, so adopters inherited the drift and get the repair on their next `/update-gaia` (#1626)

- staged changes under the root `test/` tree are linted again at commit time. The pre-commit hook has always carried a `test/` arm and it fired correctly: staging a change there printed `running lint-staged` and ran typecheck and Vitest. But lint-staged's own glob list named only `app/` and the two tooling directories, so it matched zero files and exited 0 having run ESLint on nothing, and the commit landed on a green local signal with the violation surfacing only later in CI. ESLint was the single check silently skipped, for the whole tree, which is the shape that reads as covered rather than as missing. The asymmetry is what marks it an omission rather than a design choice: `.storybook/` and `.playwright/` each received both a hook arm and a glob, `test/` received the arm alone. `test/` now joins those two on the existing tooling glob rather than getting a second entry of its own, because two keys sharing one ESLint cache file would have had lint-staged's per-glob workers, which run concurrently by default, overwrite each other's cache entries and re-lint already-clean files on the next commit. `.lintstagedrc.json` ships as an owned file, so every adopter inherited the gap and gets the repair on their next `/update-gaia`. Two guards added alongside keep the halves of that contract coupled: one derives the hook's arm directories from the hook itself and fails when one is not covered by a glob whose task chain actually invokes ESLint, rather than by any glob at all, since a chain running only prettier leaves exactly the hole the guard exists to find; the other fails when an arm is assigned but never read by the guard that decides whether the gate runs. Both count what they read against a deliberately wider count of the arm assignments present, so an arm spelled in a way neither derivation can parse reds the suite instead of shrinking its coverage, and the CI filter that decides whether this suite runs at all now arms on the config file the guard reads, which it did not before (#1625)

- GAIA's capability oracle no longer reads a repository path that a message string or an array literal merely names as a script the caller executes. A path alone at the start of a line is an execution, and a path alone at the start of a line inside a multi-line deny message is the same bytes, so the oracle fabricated a call edge and pulled the named script's entire reach into a caller that never runs it. The same fabrication came from a `;`, `|`, `&`, or a word like `then` sitting inside a quoted sentence, each of which the oracle reads as the boundary a command sits behind although the shell will never act on any of them. The repair an author reaches for when the check reds is to declare the reach until it goes green, and those declarations ship, so a fabricated edge ends as a published overstatement of what a hook touches; it can also mask a real over-declaration by making a term nobody reaches look reached, which is the silent direction. Filed as latent, it was live: one registered hook was declaring a GitHub write it does not make, computed entirely from its own denial text explaining how to merge, and that entry's stated reasoning already said the declaration should not be there. The declaration is withdrawn, so the shipped hook manifest now says what the hook does. Neither obvious repair was available, which is why this took the shape it did: refusing to read a path at the start of a line costs the most ordinary execution there is, and blanking a path inside quotes breaks the rule the whole reader rests on, that a real command sits outside the quotes while its operand routinely sits inside them, so `bash "$dir/x.sh"` has to keep resolving while the sentence beside it does not. What separates the two is the quoting, and the oracle had none: it read one line at a time and could not tell a string that opened on an earlier line from code. It now carries that state across lines, as a stack rather than a count of quotes, because a command substitution spanning several lines is real code whose calls are real reach, and a reading that could not tell it apart from a message body would have lost every one of them silently. A line that ends its string and then runs something keeps what it runs. Carrying that state costs a walk over the line, and the first version of it walked one character at a time, which bash charges by the length of the whole line each step: every quoted line in the tree cost its own length squared, and the test shard carrying the shell lint went from passing to exceeding its CI time cap. The walk now jumps between the characters that can change the state and skips the runs between them, which is checked as a differential against the same walk with the jumping disabled, over every shell file the repository tracks, rather than against fixtures for the shapes somebody thought of. Both capability surfaces were measured before and after: the script surface computes byte-identical reach, and the hook surface differs by the one withdrawn declaration. One limit is stated rather than closed, on the line-local half: a line carrying a command substitution keeps its boundaries, because a separator inside one is real, so a path behind a `;` inside a quoted sentence on such a line is still read as a call. The boundary that had no paired negative test in the suite now has them, together with the tests that bound them, each proven against a mutation that makes it fail. Four ways the same walk could still lose a line were found by review and closed, each proven against a mutation and against the reading the branch forked from: a comment inside a nested quoted body, an `awk` program's own, was handed to the here-document reader, which took a delimiter out of the prose and swallowed the rest of the file, live on one of this repository's own gate scripts and hiding 263 of its lines from a merge-blocking check that went on reporting clean; a `#` was read as opening a comment only after whitespace where the shell opens one after anything that ends a word, so `true;# don't` left a quote open that dropped every line below it; and `$'...'`, the one single-quoted form where a backslash escapes, was read as an ordinary quoted span, which closes it at an escaped quote and reopens it at the real terminator; and an arithmetic expansion was read as an ordinary command substitution, whose opener spends one paren where `$((` spends two, so its `))` closed one frame too many and left every enclosing quote off by one, which both dropped the rest of a file as string body and fabricated a call out of the next line of an array literal. That reading was compared against the one the branch forked from over every tracked shell file, line by emitted line rather than by the last line each reading reached: three files gain the lines a swallowed here-document or comment had been hiding, and the only two whose reading now stops earlier stop at the opening line of a multi-line quoted body. The oracle is maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it; the declarations it gates do ship (#1536)

- the suite covering GAIA's shell gate no longer costs a CI shard its entire time budget. The gate folds in seven whole-tree guards, so one run of it walks every tracked script, and the suite was starting a fresh run for each assertion it made about that run: seven separate tests drove byte-identical output apart only by which line they grepped, and nine more paid the same whole-tree cost to ask about one pass. The bill lands on the shard the file is assigned to, and because a file-level sharder cannot split one file, no repartition could relieve it: the shard was cancelled at its cap on two consecutive runs, failing the aggregating check on the cancelled dependency rather than on anything it ran. The seven are now one run asserting over a pass set derived from the gate itself, which also covers a guard the hand-written set had silently fallen behind and never named, and the rest is split at the seam the gate already has, the pass its own `--only` flag selects. The sizing argument on the decision page recording this matrix is re-derived against the result, including the part of it these suites had made stale: the two legs it named as the floor are no longer what binds the group, and a suite whose cost sits in what it invokes rather than in what it contains is why. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs any of it (#1619)

- GAIA's capability oracle no longer reads a command name that sits against punctuation inside a quoted message as an executed command. The part that keeps prose in a double-quoted string from reaching the detectors required a space on both sides of the word, so `(rm` in a sentence kept its operand and the oracle emitted a term for a write that never happens, and `(bash <path>)` fabricated a call edge pulling the named script's whole reach into the caller's. The repair an author reaches for when the check reds is to widen a declaration until it goes green, and those declarations ship, so a fabricated term ends as a published overstatement of what a hook touches. Which punctuation counts as a boundary was decided by measurement rather than by reading, because the two constraints on it pull against each other and one of them is easy to get backwards: a boundary must not be a path character, since these words are ordinary path components and blanking one out of `"$dir/rm"` or `install.sh` would corrupt the target the detector exists to read; and blanking leaves a space behind, so a boundary that is itself something the oracle reads as command position lets that new space carry a following path into a fabricated call, which is worse than the over-report being repaired. Every combination of candidate, command word, side and target was driven through the real scanner: `;`, `&` and `|` each turned 39 measured shapes from silent into a fabricated call and are excluded on that; `(`, `)` and `,` fix 60 between them and fabricate nothing. Both capability surfaces compute byte-identical reach before and after, so the defect was latent rather than live, and the walk costs the same. The test that holds the skip predicate in step with what the blanker removes now asks the blanker itself rather than a list, so a boundary taught to one cannot silently outrun the other, and a second test bounds the blanking's cost, because the first draft of it rebuilt the span once per word it removed and turned a long dense line from a sixth of a second into three and a half minutes. Two residuals are pinned rather than fixed, so both are re-runnable claims instead of sentences: a path whose own component is spelled like a command name and sits between two of these characters is still blanked and its write still lost, which is the reason membership is decided by whether such a path exists here rather than by what a filename may legally contain. One residual on the reported line is independent of this and of #1600: its `rm` sits against an escaped backtick, which the oracle reads as a live substitution and declines to touch by design. The oracle is maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it; the declarations it gates do ship (#1609)

- GAIA's capability oracle no longer treats every line carrying a command substitution as unreadable. The part that keeps prose inside a quoted string from being read as code gave up on any such line whole, so a usage block or a deny message sitting beside an ordinary `"$(date)"` was passed through untouched and its words read as an executed command, producing a term for a write that never happens. The repair an author reaches for when the check reds is to widen a declaration until it goes green, and those declarations ship, so a fabricated term ends as a published overstatement of what a hook writes. The line is now given up on only when doing otherwise would cost something: when a substitution carries a quote of its own, which defeats the reader that decides what is quoted, or when it carries anything the reader would strip, since the inside of a substitution is code and stripping a command or a redirect out of it loses a real write or a real network call. That second condition is the one worth stating, because it is not a misreading: the reader is right about the line and stripping it is still wrong, and the first version of this change had exactly that hole. Reach the oracle never sees cannot surface as a finding, so both conditions resolve toward keeping the old answer rather than toward the tidier one, and a test now derives the strippable set from the oracle itself so a token added later is covered the day it lands rather than the day it costs something. Measured over every line of the tree the oracle walks: 145 lines it previously refused are now read, and the 486 capability records the two surfaces compute are unchanged, so the defect was latent rather than live. One residual on the same line the issue reported is independent of all of this, reproduces with no substitution present at all, and is tracked separately. The oracle is maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs it; the declarations it gates do ship (#1600)

- the shipped page describing GAIA's issue-claim label now matches what the release hook does. It said the hook fires on `gh pr merge` and strips the claim from every issue the merged pull request closes, flatly, while the hook bails before any label write on several shapes, and its Known-limitations section listed three of the hand-off cases the always-loaded rule beside it enumerates. A reader trusting the page walked away believing a claim had released itself when it had not, and that failure is silent on both ends: the merge lands, the issue closes, and only the label stays. The page now carries the full set with the release sentence qualified, and a seventh case joins both surfaces, found by this repair's own merge. A merge naming no pull-request number and passing `--delete-branch` releases nothing, because deleting the branch moves the checkout onto the default branch before the hook runs and the hook then looks up the current branch's pull request and finds none; that is the spelling the merge gate's own denial text offers as always readable, so the two guards pull opposite ways and naming the number is what satisfies both. A test now holds the two surfaces in step per entry rather than by count, so a path added to one alone reds; it is maintainer-only and release-excluded, while both surfaces it guards ship, so the corrections reach adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1585)

- a wikilink on GAIA's shipped Worthiness Audit wiki page now resolves. It hard-wrapped across a line break in the markdown source, and no wikilink renderer resolves a `[[...]]` pair containing a newline, so the link to the TDD RED Verification page failed silently while reading as correct. A sweep of all 767 wikilinks in the tree found it was the last one left in that shape. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1583)

- the check GAIA's maintainers run over their own shell no longer certifies a library load that nothing actually checked. It flags a `source` that can run with errexit armed and is not bracketed against an unparseable target, and it accepts a parse check on the file being loaded as one of the safe shapes. Two ways to earn that credit were not checks at all: text sitting inside a heredoc body, which is data the shell never runs, and any earlier mention of the word bash on the line, which this tree's commonest library-header idioms hand over for free. Either one read a genuinely unguarded load as guarded, and because the gate reports per file, one such line let a real defect through clean. The credit now requires a `-n` flag on a bash invocation in command position, in the three spellings this tree writes, and the backward window honours the same heredoc classification the rest of the scan already applied. A test helper that claimed to read a function definition in every spelling bash accepts, while missing one whose opening brace sits on the next line, is corrected alongside them: it backs a pin that would otherwise have stayed green over exactly the drift it exists to catch. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs any of it (#1591, #1592, #1593)

- two of GAIA's shipped audit hooks no longer exit with a bare failure when their own shared-library directory is missing. Both resolve that directory into a variable before degrading on it, and an assignment carries the exit status of the command that fills it, so with the directory absent, after a partial `/update-gaia`, an interrupted checkout, or a hand-deleted folder, the hook ended on the resolution line itself and the degrade path written directly below it never ran. What the author wrote for exactly that case, a declined stamp and a declined status each naming its reason, was replaced by silence and a non-zero exit. Both hooks now degrade as written, and a test drives each with the directory removed, deriving the set from the tree so a hook that later grows the same resolution is covered without anyone remembering to add it. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1590)

- the pre-merge audit's no-op guard no longer reports a healthy review round as a lost report, and no longer fires before the round it is judging has begun. Two defects in one guard, both of which spent the single re-dispatch it is allowed on a member that was working correctly. The first: the guard needed the path of the member's findings sidecar, and the runbook told the orchestrator to work that path out once and reuse it, on the stated grounds that the key never moves between rounds. It moves, once per round that comes back clean, so from the second round on the guard looked at a path nothing writes to, read the absence as a lost report, and stopped the run with a false diagnosis. The classifier now finds that artifact itself rather than being told where it is, and takes a stamp written just before each dispatch so a leftover from an earlier round cannot pass for this one's. Earlier rounds' reports stay on disk, which is what the merge-time findings summary reads. The second: the runbook required each member to be dispatched with a parameter the agent tool does not offer, and told the orchestrator to judge the member the moment the dispatch call returned. That call returns before the member has done anything, so the judgement landed on an empty hand. Dispatch is now described as what it is, and the guard waits for the member's own files. **Action required:** none, unless your own automation calls `.gaia/scripts/audit-noop-detect.sh --shape audit-team-member`; `--path` is now optional there, and `--findings` has a resolving alternative, `--findings-root <root> --findings-since <stamp>` (#1537, #1569)

- GAIA's pre-merge audit no longer tells you its review team has five members when your copy has two. The team's size was written out as a literal count in the prose describing the audit machinery, and the three maintainer-only members are stripped out of an adopter bundle, so that count was already wrong in every clone that received it. Nothing was broken by it: the checks derive the roster from the agent definitions they find and never read the number, so the defect was a statement asserting something no code consults. The roster counts this sweep found now name the set instead of counting it, and three of them were already stale rather than merely rot-prone, one in a file that ships. No deterministic check enforces the rule against writing such a count, so the sweep was done by reading rather than by pattern and is bounded by that: it is not a guarantee that none is left. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1571)

- the findings summary a local audit posts on your pull request now carries what every review round found, instead of only the last one. Each round files its findings under a key that moves forward whenever a round comes back clean, and the producer read only the final round's, which is always clean because a clean round is what lets the merge happen: on one measured pull request the comment carried none of the ten findings the loop produced, on another one of three. That comment is what `/gaia-harden` counts to decide which kinds of defect recur often enough to deserve a permanent check, so the findings somebody had already judged worth fixing were exactly the ones it never saw. **Action required:** none, unless your own automation calls `.gaia/scripts/post-findings-block.sh`, which now takes no `--base` argument and rejects one; drop the flag (#1573)

- the check that verifies what each automatically-firing hook can reach no longer misses a library loaded from inside an `if` condition. Three of GAIA's merge gates load their shared arming library exactly that way, and the analyzer read that shape as no call at all rather than as one it could not resolve, so the library and everything it reaches sat outside those hooks' computed reach while the check still reported the tree clean. Silence is the worse direction here, because the record it produces is what an adopter reads to find out what a registered hook can do, and an omission there looks exactly like a hook that reaches for nothing. The declarations themselves were already correct, so no shipped manifest content changes; what changes is that they are now verified against the shape instead of around it. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone runs the analyzer (#1549)

- the last hooks that could refuse every matching tool call when a shared library is unusable now degrade instead. This closes the residual the previous round left behind, the arming library's two internal loads, which no consumer could guard from outside because a parse check does not recurse into what a file sources, together with two blocking guards no round had looked at, where an unparseable `repo-scope.sh` denied an ordinary `git status` on every bash version rather than on stock macOS alone. All seven remaining load sites now suspend `errexit` across the load and let the capability check that already followed it decide, which costs no subprocess and holds at any source depth, so the class stops needing a fresh round each time the residual moves one level deeper. Fixing the loads then exposed a second, worse defect on the same degrade path: the `rm -rf` guard reads its safe-scratch whitelist from one of those libraries and only ever assigned that variable when the library loaded, so on bash 4.4 and newer an unreadable registry killed the guard on an unset variable before any of its deny arms ran. Its own comment promised the opposite, that an unreachable registry keeps every protection and loses only the scratch carve-outs, and because that exit is not a refusal the guard silently stopped guarding instead of failing safe. The whitelist now starts empty, which is the degrade the matching helper was already written for (#1564, #1565)

- five hooks that fire automatically on your tool calls no longer refuse the call, or silently lose their work, when a library they load is unusable. This is the sibling of the tally-hook fix below, found in the same audit and worse in what it costs: two of the five are blocking guards, so a failed load there is not a dropped record but a refused tool call, with a raw `No such file or directory` as the only explanation. Two ways a library goes unusable and the hooks survived neither. It is missing, which under `set -e` abandons the shell before the trailing `|| exit 0` written to handle exactly that case ever runs, on the bash 3.2 that stock macOS ships as `/bin/bash`; that exits 1, an advisory, so the call proceeds and what is lost is the hook's work plus a confusing diagnostic. Or it is present but unparseable, which is what an unresolved merge conflict inside it looks like; that exits 2, the code that refuses the call, on every platform rather than macOS alone. The sharpest case was the guard that fires on every `Edit`/`Write`: a corrupted shared library made it deny the very edit that would repair the library, and it is a guard whose entire stated contract is to allow anything it cannot adjudicate. Loads that sit past a hook's own gate are now parse-checked before they are sourced, so an unusable library degrades the hook instead of stopping your work. Loads that run ahead of the gate, on every call, are checked the same way: the first version of this fix gave them a cheaper guard on the belief that checking them would roughly double what every call pays, and re-measuring showed the real cost is a surcharge of a few milliseconds, not a doubling, so they are checked too. That matters most for the hook that guards merges, where the cheaper guard would have left every command refused on stock macOS with no reason printed at all. One residual is deliberately left and tracked separately: the arming library loads two helpers of its own that no hook can reach from outside, so an unparseable copy of either still refuses commands on a stock bash 3.2, and for one of them that reaches any command sharing the verb's first word, a plain `git status` included. Closing it needs a different mechanism rather than a wider version of this fix, so it is filed on its own (#1556, residual tracked as #1564)

- GAIA's merge gate no longer slows down with the square of your pull-request body's size. The gate reads the first command of a tool call by walking it one character at a time, and inside quotes a whole prose body is a single unbroken word, so that read alone cost roughly 0.9 seconds at 16KB and 3.1 at 32KB, paid synchronously in front of you on every armed merge. It now accumulates that text in bulk, which brings the same two reads to about 11 and 30 milliseconds and takes the whole gate from 2.9 and 5.2 seconds to about 2.1 at both sizes, the remainder being the gate's own fixed work rather than anything that grows with what you wrote. What the gate concludes is unchanged, verified byte-for-byte against the old scanner across several thousand command shapes on both the bash 5 CI runs on and the bash 3.2 stock macOS ships. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1558)

- the hook that records a plan's token cost on each per-phase `git commit`/`push` no longer refuses the git operation when a library it loads is unusable. Two ways that happens, and it survived neither: the library is missing, which it was reaching for by a path relative to wherever the command ran rather than relative to the hook's own location, and the library is present but unparseable, which is what an unresolved merge conflict inside it looks like. Under `set -e` a failed source abandons the shell before the hook's own `ERR` trap can convert it, and before a trailing `|| exit 0` runs at all on the bash 3.2 that stock macOS ships as `/bin/bash`. The hook then exits non-zero, and what that costs depends on which failure it was: a missing library exits 1, which loses the tally row and lets the command through, while an unparseable one exits 2, the code that refuses the command outright, so the commit that would have repaired the conflicted library was itself blocked. Its library loads now resolve from the hook's own location, and the two that run only on an armed commit or push are parse-checked before they are sourced, so an unusable library skips the tally instead of stopping the commit. The third load runs ahead of that gate, on every command, where a parse check would cost more than it returns; it takes the cheaper guard, which covers every case except an unparseable library on bash 3.2. The hook's one remaining path that resolved against wherever the command ran, the call to the tally script itself, is anchored the same way: a commit made from any subdirectory of the repository satisfied every gate, reached that call, failed to find the script, and had the failure discarded, so the tally row went missing with no diagnostic (#1551)

- the merge gate's three clearance signals no longer clear a merge for a pull request nobody audited. A member's content-digest marker, the `GAIA-Audit` commit trailer, and the `GAIA-Audit` CI status each prove that a member read *this checkout's* content, and none of them read the pull-request number the `gh pr merge` command carries, so from a branch whose dispatched members had all cleared, naming any other number merged that pull request with no audit behind it. These are the arms #1543 deliberately scoped out, and binding them makes "the merge names the pull request the clearance is for" a whole-gate invariant rather than a bypass-only one. Two consequences worth knowing: the merge must be the first command in its tool call, spelled so the gate can read it exactly, which is what the merge workflow already prescribes; and a checkout whose pull-request record cannot be read at all, no `gh`, no authentication, or no open pull request for the branch, denies any merge that names a number, whatever clearance is present, since a gate that cannot tell which pull request it is on cannot confirm the merge names it. That denial is not lifted by re-running the audit, in either case: the repair is to respell the merge. A merge naming no pull request is unaffected, that is `gh`'s current-branch default and already the one the clearance covers (#1544)

- writing a merge or PR-create command into a file through a heredoc no longer denies an unrelated tool call. Every hook that arms on a `gh` or `git` verb now shares one arming decision instead of keeping its own copy, so the heredoc fix reaches all of them rather than one gate at a time, and a verb whose characters are quoted now arms every one of these hooks rather than only the merge gate, a stricter answer worth knowing if you relied on quoting to slip past the others (#1542, #1545)

- the merge gate no longer skips the merge it is meant to gate when the command is quoted, and its three remaining bypasses no longer clear a pull request nobody audited. The gate armed by searching the command for the literal run of characters `gh pr merge`, so any spelling that broke that run, a quoted verb, a quoted subcommand, a line continuation inside the verb, was not matched at all and the merge proceeded with no Code Audit Team marker from any member. Arming now also asks the shared tokenizer whether the tool call's first command really is the merge, whatever quoting spells it, and keeps the text match alongside it rather than in place of it, because the tokenizer reads only the first command and dropping the text match would stop gating a merge written after a separator. Separately, the chore(deps), self-modification and out-of-scope bypasses each proved something about the checkout they were run from, the pull-request title, the changed file set, while the merge command named a pull request number they never parsed, so from a branch whose own pull request qualified, a merge naming an arbitrary unaudited number cleared with no marker; all three now require the command to name the pull request whose record they read. **Action required:** none, but a merge that names a pull request this gate cannot confirm offline now denies where it previously cleared. The turnkey spelling that omits the number is unaffected, since an absent number is the current branch and therefore the pull request the bypass already read. What this does not change is stated in the gate itself rather than implied: the text match still over-arms, so a heredoc body or quoted prose beginning with the verb still denies an unrelated tool call, and neither arm reads a command prefix or a subshell. This gate targets accidental and inattentive merges, not deliberate obfuscation (#1540, #1541)

- the record of what each registered hook reaches for is complete again for a hook that runs a script by its own path. The capability oracle read a call only behind `bash`, `sh`, `source`, or a bare `.`, so a bare-path execution was not an unresolved call, it was not a call at all: the target and everything it reaches stayed outside the closure with nothing on any unresolved line to say so, and the manifest entry claiming to name that hook's whole outbound reach omitted it. The silence is what made it worse than an ordinary gap, because the check reported the tree clean while a mutation appended to the missed script went undetected. The oracle now reads a bare path in command position as the invocation it is, and the manifest declares the resolver, the version normalizer it sources, and the four audit predicate libraries it loads. The detection is deliberately narrow and enumerates where it stops rather than naming the misses that came to mind first: it reads strict command position only, so an operand behind plain whitespace stays prose; it requires a directory separator, so an extensionless executable is missed; and a path in the condition of an `if` is missed exactly as a `.` there already is. It also over-reads in one direction, which the code now states plainly rather than leaving to be found: nothing blanks a repo path inside a double-quoted string, so a path written into a message directly behind a separator reads as a call. That direction fails loudly rather than silently, nothing shipped triggers it today, and it is tracked as its own issue rather than papered over. A shape the detection accepts and cannot resolve is reported rather than dropped, and the boundary that ends the token carries the separators rather than whitespace alone, so a call whose operand list simply ended is read as the call it is. The manifest ships, so adopters pick up the corrected record on their next `/update-gaia`; the check that reconciles it remains maintainer-only (#1528)

- GAIA's command-reachability guard now runs on the pull request that breaks it. The guard asserts that every CLI subcommand is invoked from somewhere outside its own router, and it reads seven surfaces to decide that: the skills, commands, hooks, and agents directories, the CI workflows, the wiki, and the bundled workflow templates. The job that runs the guard woke up for only three of them, so the one-sided edit it exists to catch, the removal of a command's sole invocation from a skill, a command, a hook, or a wiki page, matched nothing, the suite skipped, and a declared-required check reported green having run the guard zero times. The gap now closes through a second, narrower path filter that wakes only the workspace install and the test run, leaving the expensive half of the job on the original one, so a prose or wiki pull request pays a fraction of a full run rather than all of it. A new test binds the filter's surface list to the guard's own in both directions, so a surface added to one without the other fails the build instead of silently leaving the guard unarmed on the only edit that can break it. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1524)

- GAIA's audit-grading divergence guard now runs on the pull request that breaks it. Every Code Audit Team member declares in a machine-readable marker which severity gradings it can emit, and a suite holds every declaration against the shared map the audit parser reads, so a member that learns a fourth grading fails that suite until the map and the parser learn it too. The job that runs the suite only woke up for changes under the CLI's own directory and a handful of named root files, and the one-sided edit the guard exists to catch touches only the agent files themselves, so the required check reported green having run the divergence check zero times and the change merged checked by nothing on that axis. No sibling workflow covered the gap: the one that does wake up for those files runs shell suites only, and the single shell suite naming the marker explicitly declines to reimplement the parity mechanism rather than approximate it. The filter now arms on the agent files by name pattern, so a member added or removed later is covered without another edit. The floor that suite asserts is a rot guard rather than a roster count, and the roster is larger than it, so on a removal it is the filter, not that assertion, that re-runs the job. The wider version of the same gap, four more directories that this job's other whole-repo reader scans, is deliberately left to a separate decision: arming on those would run this required job on roughly two in five pull requests instead of one in thirty, which is a cost worth choosing rather than inheriting. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1513)

- a merge referenced by a pull-request URL copied out of the browser no longer costs that merge its findings block or leaves the issue claims it closes standing. Both hooks that act on your repository off a merge matched the URL more strictly than `gh` itself does, anchoring immediately after the pull-request number and comparing the authority with any port still attached, so a `.../pull/7/files` address from the Files tab, a `?` or `#` suffix, and `https://github.com:443/...` each declined while `gh` went ahead and merged the pull request they name. The matcher now follows gh's own on both counts, and the whole URL-to-pull-request decision moved into the shared library beside the `--repo` half of the same boundary, so the two hooks can no longer answer the question differently and the next repair to it is one edit rather than two. Adopters pick this up on their next `/update-gaia` (#1517, #1518)

- the hook that posts the audit findings block on a local merge no longer decides which repository a merge targets by pattern-matching the raw command text, so it neither posts onto a pull request here for a merge aimed somewhere else nor stays silent on an ordinary one. Three everyday redirections ahead of a merge, a `pushd`, a subshell, and a `cd` to a path that does not resolve, all read as "this repository" and reached the posting path, while a `-R` belonging to an entirely different command later in the same tool call (`gh pr merge 42 && grep -R app/routes .`) read as the merge's own target and cost that merge its findings block. No shape test on the flag's value can tell those apart, because a path argument carries a slash exactly as an `owner/repo` does; telling them apart takes knowing which command the flag belongs to. Both hooks that act on your repository off a merge now share one scan that answers exactly that: the merge has to be the first command in its tool call, and its own flags are read with the shell's quoting rules rather than a regex, so a quoted `--repo="owner/repo"` is understood too. The same hook resolved *which* pull request to post onto from a pattern over the raw text as well, three lines below the boundary it had just consulted, and that read has the identical defect: it takes its first match anywhere in the string and only matches a number sitting immediately after the verb, so a merge spelling its flags first (`gh pr merge --squash 1515`) alongside a later mention of another one landed the block on the wrong pull request. It now takes the reference the scan already read, and each of the three shapes gh accepts for it carries its own boundary: a pull-request URL names its own repository, so it is checked against yours the same way the flag is and declines when it names another, and a reference gh cannot resolve declines too rather than quietly falling back to whatever pull request your current branch has. The narrower rule has a cost worth knowing: a merge run behind an earlier command in one tool call posts no findings block, so run the merge as its own step, which the merge workflow already prescribes. Adopters pick this up on their next `/update-gaia` (#1515)

- GAIA's own label registry is now covered by the checks that pin it on the pull request that changes it, rather than on some unrelated one weeks later. Two suites read the committed registry and the JSON Schema beside it, one holding the registry's entry count and one holding the schema's enumerations to the CLI's constants, but the job that runs them only woke up for changes under the CLI's own directory. A pull request that only added or removed a label therefore touched neither, so the required check reported green having run those two suites zero times, and the now-stale pin fired on the next contributor to touch the CLI, reddening a change that did not cause it with no bound on the delay. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1512)

- your repository's audit CI no longer announces a pending gate that was never posted. The audit posts a `pending` status while a specialized reviewer only a local run can clear is outstanding, and when GitHub rejected that post, a rate limit, a 5xx, an auth blip, nothing was published and nothing recorded the rejection, so the pull-request comment still told you the gate was pending. You would then read a comment naming a status the checks list does not show, which reads as broken CI rather than as the transient API error it was, and it is the same wrong-explanation shape two neighboring arms of that comment already existed to prevent, on claims they merely could not verify rather than knew to be false. The comment now reports both facts in one sentence, the reviewers only a local run can clear and the rejected post that means no status landed at all, and it keeps the instruction that actually opens the gate. The gate itself is unchanged and was never wrong: it failed closed before and it fails closed now, because an absent required check blocks a merge exactly as a pending one does. Adopters pick this up on their next `/update-gaia` (#1405)

- `gaia labels sync --enforce-blocked` no longer deletes a label that only pull requests carry. The enforcement gate counted a label's carriers with a single issue query, and GitHub treats issues and pull requests as separate result sets, so a label sitting on pull requests and no issue counted zero and read as safe to delete, which strips it from every pull request carrying it with no way to restore the association. That is not hypothetical: this project's own live label migration measured exactly that state on one label and kept it by hand. The count now spans both surfaces, and a label it cannot count on either surface is still never deleted; the report now says so out loud, since a silent refusal to enforce reads exactly like a run that was never asked to enforce. Adopters pick this up on their next `/update-gaia` (#1499)

- a labels sync that could not read your repository's labels no longer presents its manual command list as the work you have left. The sync degrades to a list of manual `gh` commands when a token lacks label scope, and it degrades that way on a refused read as well as a refused write. The two lists are not the same thing: a refused write leaves a genuine remainder, while a refused read leaves the plan computed against an assumed-empty repository, so every registry entry becomes an owed create and an adopter with a long-established repository was handed a create command for roughly every label they already had, presented as outstanding work. The real remainder is not computable without the read that was refused, so the sync no longer pretends to compute it. It records which refusal happened, in its own degraded output and as a `degradedAt` field under `--json`, and `/update-gaia`'s summary now says plainly that a read-refused list is the whole registry rather than the remainder, and that a create for a label you already have fails harmlessly (#1502)

- the last three shapes the shell gate for unreachable `$?` reads called wrong are settled, and its own list of what it still misses is honest again. Two of them were real false positives on correct code. A redirection whose target is itself a command, the process-substitution form, was broken on rather than read through, so the gate never reached the command word behind it and reported a line the shell runs; that operand is now followed to its end the way a command substitution already was. The other is that a statement made only of assignments takes the status of the last substitution it ran, not the first, so a second one anywhere in the statement means the status reaching the read below is not the flagged line's at all; the gate now counts substitutions and stays quiet there, which also closes the wider spelling the original report scoped too narrowly. Where that second substitution sits turns out not to matter, since the shell draws no distinction between a later assignment, a redirection target, and a second one inside the same value, so neither does the count; two kinds are excluded because neither can be the last one the statement ran, a nested substitution and one inside a process-substitution operand, which runs in its own async subshell. The third was never a false positive: its verdict differs between bash 3.2 and bash 5, and the reason it looked like one is that only the 3.2 reading makes the line unreachable. Under bash 5 the read does run, and always reads zero while the command failed and the variable is empty, so the failure is swallowed instead of announced. Those are the same defect from opposite sides and the repair the gate prints fixes both, so it reports under either and targets no version. What is left is one undecidable family rather than a list of unimplemented shapes, and the header names it as that: whether a statement running two or more substitutions exits depends on whether the last one fails, which is a runtime fact, so the exemption misses the failing case. Quiet is chosen because the message the gate prints names the flagged substitution as the one whose failure exits, and with a later one present that sentence is false, which is the direction that gets a gate bypassed rather than obeyed. The header now also states what the differential shell matrix cannot do, since it checks agreement on rows it is given and can never surface a false positive nobody listed. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1494)

- the shell gate that flags an unreachable `$?` read no longer reports lines the shell actually runs. An assignment can stand as an environment prefix on another command, where the exit status belongs to the prefixed command and nothing below it is unreachable, and the gate recognised that shape by inspecting a single word after the assignment. The shell accepts an arbitrary run of assignments and redirections in that position, in any order, so the check stopped at the first of them and reported a correct line, printing a repair that cannot be applied to an environment prefix at all. Four fixes landed on it across four review rounds, each widening the check by one more spelling, and the fourth opened a silent hole where a quoted redirection target let a genuine defect through, which is the worse direction and was reverted. The check now consumes the whole prefix rather than enumerating spellings, and correctness is no longer measured against anyone's list: a matrix of prefix forms runs through a real shell on every test run and the gate is required to agree with what the shell did.
