That is what closed the class rather than the next shape in it, and it immediately showed sixteen wrongly reported forms where the tracked report had named four. A clobber redirection was separately being read as a pipeline, which suppressed real defects with no message at all. Three forms are still called wrong, each needing a mechanism this repair does not add; they are named in the gate's own header and pinned by tests so the list cannot drift. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1486)

- GAIA's audit machinery no longer quietly empties its own review scope on macOS. A comment inside one of the audit library's data blocks carried an apostrophe, and bash 3.2, the version stock macOS ships as `/bin/bash` and the version these scripts declare support for, reads it as the start of a string that never closes, which makes the entire file unparseable. Sourcing an unparseable file kills the host shell outright rather than returning an error, so the script that decides which commit an audit reviews against died with no output at all and the degraded fallback it carries for exactly that case never ran; a caller reading the resulting empty base then asked git for the files changed since nothing, received an empty list at success status, and reviewed an empty scope instead of failing. Bash 5 parses the same file without complaint, which is why every CI runner reported it clean and why the only place the defect was observable was the maintainer's own machine. The apostrophe is gone, and the maintainer's shell gate now parses every tracked script with bash 3.2 on each run so the class cannot return: where no bash older than 4 exists that pass skips loudly rather than silently, since a silent skip would recreate the same false clean one layer up, and it fails rather than reporting clean when it cannot run at all. Adopters on macOS pick up the fix on their next `/update-gaia` (#1487, #1488)

- GAIA's CI auto-merge and post-merge revert steps now report failures instead of dying silently. Both read a command's exit status in a form that cannot run under the shell options GitHub's runners set, so a failing command ended the step one line early and every branch written to handle that failure was unreachable: the tolerance that treats an already-enabled auto-merge as a notice, the annotation that names a genuine merge failure, and the path that reuses a revert pull request already open. The last one carried the real damage, because three later steps run only when the revert step succeeds; a revert that could not be opened therefore skipped the escalation too, so a post-merge CI failure that could not be reverted was reported to nobody. Adopters running GAIA CI pick this up on their next `/update-gaia` (#1477, #1478)

- forty maintainer working-document identifiers and a handful of internal path references stopped shipping in the release bundle, and the checks that were supposed to catch them can no longer miss a file. Twelve bundle-time leak checks each carried its own hand-written list of directories to scan. Six of them ask the same question, does a maintainer-only token appear in a file adopters receive, and differ only in which token they match, so their lists should have been identical; they were not, and the differences were spelling drift rather than intent. Every previous repair of this shape added one more directory to one more list, five times over, which is why it kept coming back: the defect was the list, not any particular missing entry. Those six checks now scan the whole staged tree, which is by definition exactly what ships, so there is no list left to be wrong. Widening them surfaced what the gaps had been hiding, and all of it is fixed here: SPEC identifiers that resolve only inside a working tree adopters never receive, a help string and an error message in the shipped CLI naming a source file no adopter clone has, and several comments pointing at internal test directories. The per-entry provenance in the state registry keeps its field, since the schema that ships alongside it requires the field; a new bundle-time transform rewrites the identifier out of the value rather than deleting the key and handing adopters a file that fails its own validator. A `test:sandbox` script that ran a harness no adopter receives is now stripped from the bundle's `package.json` alongside the one already removed. Scanning everything rather than a curated subset costs the release about eighty milliseconds (#1439). A seventh check joins them for the same reason: the ban on machine-specific absolute filesystem paths is repo-wide, stated in `CLAUDE.md` and in the repo-relative-paths rule, while the check enforcing it read `.claude/` alone, so every other shipped surface was unguarded against the exact literal that rule forbids. Planting a developer's home directory into a shipped script or a wiki page and running the real scrub reported no leak, and no other checker covered the gap either. Nothing is leaking today, so this closes a missing guard rather than a live defect; what it prevents is a future absolute path reaching an adopter's clone, where it names a directory that does not exist on their machine. The two files that legitimately carry the pattern, a captured stack trace and a comment documenting shell wrapper levels, are exempted by the placeholder form they use rather than by filename, so neither exemption outlives the content it was written for (#1479)

- a change that touches only the release manifest no longer skips the job that checks it. The distribution harness runs when a pull request touches one of a listed set of paths, and the manifest was absent from that set even though two of the harness scenarios read it, so a manifest-only write reported the check green having run nothing that looks at the manifest. Most regenerations arm the job anyway through a second file written alongside it, which is why the gap went unnoticed; the manifest is now listed in its own right, with a test that keeps it listed. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1473)

- the distribution harness that replays the release scrub can no longer report a clean run having checked nothing. It reads two of the report's sections by their length, and the JSON tool it uses answers zero for a section that is absent, so a report that simply stopped emitting one of them satisfied the assertion while verifying nothing about it. The guards added alongside the derived mutation counters could not cover that case: they all assert that no unexpected key has appeared, which catches a section renamed and sees nothing at all when one is deleted. The harness now requires both sections to be present and list-shaped before it reads either length, and the producing end pins the same contract in its own tests, so neither side can drop the section unnoticed. Separately, the enumeration that derives which transforms to assert against walks each one exactly one level deep, while the note above it claimed that as total coverage; a transform reporting its count one level further in escaped the enumeration, a sibling number kept the counterless guard quiet, and a real mutation would have gone unasserted on a run that read clean. A fourth guard now rejects any value inside a transform section that is neither a count nor a file list, failing in the same direction the existing guards do, and the coverage claim is bounded to the depth it actually walks, including the two residual holes it still does not close. Both gaps were latent: no transform nests a count today, and neither section is optional in the producer's own type. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1484)

- the bounded retry around the audit CI package install now actually runs, instead of being unreachable on the one failure it was added for. The retry makes up to three attempts inside a five-minute step cap, and the acquire-level timeouts beside it bound a stalled transfer. Neither bounded a call that connects successfully and then takes minutes anyway, which is the failure the runners keep producing: the mirror listed first answers immediately with nothing, the fallback mirror connects cleanly, and the time goes after that. So a single package-list refresh consumed the whole step budget, the cap fired part-way through the first attempt, and the second and third never happened. Two separate runs failed that way without ever printing that an attempt had failed, which is what makes the diagnosis unambiguous rather than inferred. Each call is now individually bounded, so exhausting one ends that attempt rather than the step, and the budget is sized so all three attempts plus their backoff fit inside the cap with room to spare. A transient stall now recovers on the next attempt, which is what the retry was supposed to deliver and had not once. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1484)

- a stalled Ubuntu package mirror no longer costs the audit CI matrix a red required check, or thirteen minutes to learn about it. The step that installs the YAML parser and zsh for the bats legs carried no bound of its own, so a fetch that hung mid-download held its leg until the job's entire budget expired, the test step behind it never started, and a declared-required check reported no test result at all for a reason having nothing to do with the change under test. That step now breaks a stalled fetch in seconds and makes a small number of attempts before giving up, which absorbs the transient case a manual re-run was already absorbing by hand, while a genuine mirror outage still fails rather than hanging. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1472)

- the merge gate no longer lets an out-of-scope finding be waived into the pull-request body when the file it names belongs to the branch you are stacked on rather than to your own change. The rule that permits a waive asks whether the pull request already changes the file, and the answer was worked out against the repository's default branch, so on a pull request opened against any other branch it silently included everything that branch had changed too, and a finding that should have been filed as tracked debt was recorded as prose instead. The question is now answered against the branch the pull request actually merges into, on both the side that decides a waive and the side that checks it, so the two cannot disagree; where that branch cannot be established the previous, wider answer still stands (#1441)

- two comments in the audit CI workflow your clone receives no longer contradict each other about the same three lines of shell. One said no test could catch the removal of the line that reads a staged-but-reverted edit, which invites a reader to delete it as dead weight; the other described the path set the self-heal gate judges as exactly what a commit carries, when it is a superset of that. Both now state the narrow truth, that dropping the line changes whether the run refuses rather than what reaches your repository, and a new maintainer-side test covers the case the first comment called uncoverable. No behavior changes: the shell was correct throughout (#1467)

- a shipped wiki page no longer tells you the tech-debt backlog carries a label marking the issues filed before provenance existed. Tech-debt filing and the provenance record arrive in your clone in the same release, so provenance is writing from your very first filing and that cohort is empty in your repository permanently; a reader following the sentence goes looking for a label and a set of issues their clone cannot have. The design rationale the sentence carried, that an honest missing value beats a backfilled guess because nothing downstream can tell the two apart once they sit in the same field, is true everywhere and stays. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1462)

- a merge is no longer blocked by a finding the previous pull request already disposed of correctly. The audit records an out-of-scope waive in a file named after the reviewer's own content, which does not change every time a pull request does, so one file can be read while judging several in a row. What kept an entry from being measured against the wrong pull request was a test of whether its recorded commit was still reachable anywhere in the repository, and after a squash merge that deletes the branch it is not: the squash writes a new commit and the branch is gone, leaving exactly the shape a rebase or an amend leaves behind on the branch under review. The earlier pull request's waive was therefore re-judged against a diff it was never about, and because a waive can be earned by naming a file that pull request changed, a file the next one does not touch, it read as an abuse of the waive and denied the merge. Nothing unsafe could merge, but the block was real and the remedy it offered was to delete a correct record and file a finding that already had one. The record now also carries the branch it was written on, which answers directly what reachability could only approximate and stays offline as a lookup against GitHub would not. A record written before this change carries no branch and behaves exactly as it does today; because the file is local and rotates with the reviewer's content, that gap closes on its own. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1454)

- the audit now decides whether it is allowed to run at all by asking about the branch the guardrail it models actually checks. The action that runs the reviewer refuses whenever the audit workflow on a pull request differs from the copy on the repository's default branch, and the check standing in for that refusal compared against the pull request's own base instead. On a stacked pull request the two answers disagree in both directions: one sharing a workflow with its base where both differ from the default branch ran an audit the action silently declined and was then reported complete with no fixes needed, and one whose base had edited the workflow while its own head matched the default branch was skipped citing a change its author never made. Neither direction left the merge gate open, so the cost was a wasted or silently skipped review plus a reason that was not true. The comparison now uses the default branch, a comparison that cannot be resolved fails the step rather than guessing, and the message an author sees names the real condition and points a stale branch at a rebase. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1442)

- an uncommitted edit to a governance surface no longer vanishes instead of stopping the audit's self-heal. The workflow puts back the sensitive paths the action swaps out before the reviewer runs, which is the correct repair for a pull request that legitimately changes them, but `.claude` is the one path in that set the self-heal's own repair boundary also covers. An edit the reviewer made there and never committed was therefore quietly reverted and the rest of its self-heal committed and pushed, when the run should have stood the whole self-heal down for human review. Nothing forbidden ever reached the branch, and a committed edit was always caught; what was lost was the refusal itself and the wholesale stand-down it applies to everything else in the same run. The restore is now told apart from the reviewer's own edit by comparing against the base branch's copy, so the false positive stays fixed and the real signal survives, and a base that cannot be resolved refuses rather than assuming the benign case. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1443)

- the audit's self-heal repair boundary now sees edits the reviewer staged but never committed. The gate reads the working tree to decide whether a self-heal touched a surface it is forbidden to repair, and both of its inputs were blind to the index: one compares the working tree against the staged content, the other reads committed history, and a path sitting in between appears in neither. The step then stages and commits everything tracked, so an edit staged to a forbidden surface rode along to the branch with the refusal never firing, and the surface reached that way included the tests whose whole purpose is to catch a bad repair. It took no adversary to reach: a reviewer whose commit is rejected by a pre-commit hook leaves exactly that state, and the local guard that would otherwise catch it does not run in CI. The gate now judges everything that differs from the last commit in either the index or the working tree, which is precisely the set the commit it is about to make can carry. Found by the audit's own pre-merge review of the two fixes above, which is the review this gate exists to protect. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia`

- a pull request stacked on a branch other than the default one is now reviewed against its own base rather than against everything that base branch already carries. The audit resolves a base commit to scope what it reads, and it took that base from the repository's default branch instead of from the branch the pull request actually merges into. Where the two differ, every reviewer was handed the base branch's entire divergence presented as this pull request's own diff: one such pull request changed nineteen files, and each member received a hundred and forty-five across thirty-three commits. The wasted tokens were the smaller cost. A finding raised against history the pull request never introduced is indistinguishable, in a reviewer's output, from one raised against the code it did, and narrowing the scope by hand is not a workaround, because doing so makes some members self-skip, write no clearance, and deadlock the merge with no legitimate way out. The base now comes from the pull request itself, in CI from the base ref GitHub supplies on every pull request event and at merge time from the pull request record, with the old default-branch answer kept as the fallback whenever no base is declared or the lookup fails; that fallback is the wider diff, so it fails in the safe direction. The merge gate's two no-review-needed shortcuts read the same base, so a stacked pull request that changes only prose is no longer pushed into a ceremonial review of code its base branch already carried. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1057)

- the CI audit no longer throws away its own fixes on a pull request that touches `.claude/`. Before running the reviewer, the action replaces a fixed set of sensitive configuration paths with the base branch's copies, so the pull request's own changes to them are missing from the runner's working tree. The audit workflow puts each of those paths back afterwards, but its list had drifted from the action's and omitted `.claude` itself. What was left looked like the reviewer having edited a surface it is forbidden to touch, so the workflow discarded the entire self-heal, told the author the audit had edited something off-limits when it had not, and still marked the audit successful on a commit that no longer carried the repairs that verdict rested on. The lists agree again. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1057)

- the statusline no longer shows two `/gaia-harden` nudges side by side. When the harden tally reported both recurring candidates and an unclassified cluster, each raised its own segment naming the same bare command, so the pair read as two invocations of one thing with nothing to tell them apart and no way to act on one without the other. Both are discharged by the same run, so the two reasons now stack inside a single segment, the shape `/gaia-audit` already used for its own stackable reasons. The wording for a lone recurring pattern is unchanged, and the invariant that no command occupies more than one segment is now pinned by a test over the rendered line. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1406)

- silencing the "unclassified findings keep recurring" nudge no longer silences it against evidence that has since disappeared. That signal fires when audit findings the vocabulary cannot classify recur across enough recent pull requests, and you quiet it by recording the count it currently shows; it returns once fresh evidence raises that count by three. Every other kind of decline is released once its own findings age out of the 90-day window, so a class that goes quiet and later comes back is heard again at the ordinary threshold of three. The unclassified one was exempt from that release, deliberately, to keep a failed read of the window from wiping it. The cost of the exemption was that its recorded count outlived the findings that justified it: quiet the signal at eight, let all eight age out, and a genuinely unrelated cluster of classless findings then had to reach eleven pull requests to be heard rather than three, permanently, with nothing about the nudge's absence to say why. The exemption is gone and the signal now follows the same lifecycle as every other decline. That is safe because the failed-read case it was standing in for is already handled upstream by a separate fix in this release: an unread window no longer reaches the prune at all, so a released baseline now means the evidence is genuinely gone rather than merely unreadable. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1416)

- an auditor's refusal can no longer be merged straight past. When a Code Audit Team member reads content and withholds its clearance, the merge gate treats that refusal as absolute and outranking any clean marker for the same content. That guarantee was armed on one merge path only, the one that runs GAIA's own hook, and GitHub's auto-merge is a second path that completes on the required check alone and never consults it. So a refusal arriving after some earlier wave had already reported success left that success standing, and the pull request merged over a live refusal with the artifact sitting on disk and no diagnostic anywhere. The window is ordinary rather than exotic: a member re-dispatched after the check went green, two waves of the same member overlapping, or one member still working when the last of its siblings reports. Recording a refusal now also turns the required check red for that same commit, so every merge path sees it, and a later genuine clean pass turns it green again, so nothing gets stranded. The status post is best-effort and never interferes with the refusal itself, which stays on disk either way. Separately, the helper that answers "which members still owe a review" was reading only the clean markers, so it reported that nobody was owed while a refusal was blocking the merge; it now applies the same refusal-first precedence the gate does, and says who to re-run. A third reader had the same gap, and it was the last one left: the audit trailer stamped onto the reviewed commit certifies that every dispatched member cleared that content, and it counted only the clean markers, so it could attest a clean pass with a refusal for the same content sitting beside it. Nothing merged over that attestation, since the gate itself still denied, but it did mislead the next review round into re-anchoring past the refused commit, so the member that refused was re-dispatched onto a near-empty increment and could clear content it never re-read. The stamp now reads refusals first, and it withholds the stamp rather than writing one it cannot verify. Withholding costs nothing a merge needs, because the clearance marker and the required check stand as independent signals either way, and an attestation nobody can check is worse than none. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1419, #1430)

- the rule an orchestrator holds in context while disposing an out-of-scope review finding no longer states a superseded version of that rule. Two surfaces summarized the eligibility test for recording a finding in the pull request body instead of filing it, and both still described the two-term test from before it gained two further disqualifiers, so acting on either summary records a waive the current rule forbids and no deterministic check catches it. Both now defer to the one page that owns the rule rather than restating terms that will drift again on the next amendment, and the summary carried identically by all five audit members additionally names the disqualifier requirement, pinned by the test suite that keeps those five in lockstep. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1411, #1410)

- a background check could wipe your whole record of declined hardening candidates. It reads the recent merged-pull-request window to work out which finding classes still recur, then prunes any decline whose class has stopped appearing there. A failed read of that window, from being offline, unauthenticated, or rate-limited, comes back empty rather than as an error, and the prune read that emptiness as proof that nothing recurs any more: every decline it held was dropped, along with the count each one was declined at, so every candidate you had deliberately turned down returned as a nudge. Because the check runs unattended on a timer behind the statusline, the loss arrived with no output and nothing that looked like a failure. The prune now runs only after a window read that actually succeeded, since an unread window is not evidence that a class stopped recurring, which is the direction the surrounding code was already careful to fail in. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1418)

- the check that stops git's path escaping from silently disabling a gate now reaches a command a documentation page tells the agent to run, which is where the last instances of the class were sitting. Its scan surface was shell, hooks, and workflow files, so a fenced command block on a page that is executed instruction rather than illustration was invisible to it, and every instance in that position was found by hand, two of them only because someone happened to look while fixing a third. Markdown joins the surface, and what decides whether a line is a call site is whether it sits inside a fenced block, not a list of which pages count as executed: such a list is always one page short, while the fence is a property of the text itself. A mention outside a fence stays prose and is never read, which is what keeps the widening from turning documentation into findings. Swept across every tracked page it reported the two live instances and nothing else, and both are repaired: the wiki sync's count of newly added pages, where an escaped path splits one section's pages across two group keys so each falls below the threshold and consolidation quietly under-triggers, and the release audit's staging walk, where an escaped path is looked up literally and either dropped from the staged tree or errors. Nothing is hidden today, since no tracked path here carries a byte that triggers the escaping, so what closes is a latent failure rather than a live one. The wiki sync half ships, so it reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia`; the check itself is maintainer-only and release-excluded (#1400)

- the check that decides whether the Quality Gate runs at all could answer "nothing to check" about a commit full of source. It asks git for the staged file list and matches each path against a set of source extensions, but it asked in the spelling that lets git escape a path carrying a non-ASCII byte, and it anchored the extension match to the end of the line. An escaped path ends in a quote character rather than in its own extension, so it matches nothing, and the instruction the page gives for an empty result is to skip the gate: typecheck, lint, tests, and build are all passed over on a change that needed every one of them. The direction is what makes it worth closing rather than the likelihood. A gate that runs and finds nothing and a gate that never ran are the same silence from outside, and this is the gate every commit is routed through, so the skip is self-concealing in the one place there is no second line of defence. It is also the third shape of a class already repaired twice elsewhere, and the first of the three that no check could see: the guard for this class reads shell scripts, hooks, and workflow files, and this call site is a fenced snippet on a wiki page, which is executed instruction here because the always-loaded rule tells commits to run that page's steps as written. The call now asks in the spelling that suppresses escaping and converts back before matching, since suppressing the escape alone would leave the anchor half of the defect standing, and the surrounding prose now states the skip direction positively, so a check that cannot answer runs the gate instead of skipping it. Whether a guard should reach an executed snippet on a documentation page at all is a wider question, tracked separately. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1399)

- the commit hook that proves a new test can actually fail could clear a commit having examined none of it. It asked git for the staged files in a spelling the repository's own path-quoting check could not see, and that blind spot is the one the previous round of this work stated in the check itself and deferred rather than closed: the check matched its call name as a single fixed string, so an option written in the middle of the call hid the call entirely. It now reads the call's whole option region, so no spelling hides, and every site it newly objects to is repaired. Only one of those failed open, and it is the one that matters: a staged test file whose path carries a non-ASCII byte arrives from git in an escaped form, matches no source-directory pattern, and is dropped, so the gate reports the commit clear having verified nothing. The rest either aborted a run that should have proceeded or reported a wrong file count. The pre-commit hook's four arms survived only by accident, because the escaped spelling happens to still contain the fragment each arm looks for, so tightening any one of them later would have turned all four into silent misses with nothing to catch it. Nothing is hidden today, since no tracked path here carries such a byte, so what closes is a latent fail-open rather than a live miss. Both commit hooks and the pre-merge audit workflow template carry the repair, so those halves reach adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1396)

- a lint gate could report a clean tree having never opened one of the files it was meant to scan. Git prints a tracked path in an escaped form when it carries a non-ASCII byte, a control character, a quote, or a backslash, and the discovery loops that build each gate's scan list then tested that escaped spelling against the filesystem, found nothing there, and dropped the entry with no message and no failure. The empty-result hard error each of these gates carries cannot catch it, because the other files matched normally, so the set is not empty and the gate reports success having skipped a file. Nothing is hidden today, since no tracked path here currently carries such a byte, so what closes is a latent fail-open rather than a live miss. The same class had already been repaired by hand seven times and a check existed specifically to stop it recurring, but that check recognized only one of the two commands the class appears in, which is how it recurred in five discovery sites, two of them the guards for this very class. The check now covers the second command and is renamed for what it actually inspects, and it exempts a call whose contract is its exit status rather than its output, where demanding otherwise would be a change with no failure mode behind it. One hole is left open deliberately and is stated in the check itself rather than left to be rediscovered: it matches its call names as literal text, so an option written in the middle of one still hides that call from it. Closing that reaches a different set of files, including a commit hook, and is tracked on its own. Every site the widened check reds on is repaired, including the release staging walk, where an escaped path would have been left out of the published tarball without failing the release. Mostly maintainer-only and release-excluded; the pre-merge audit workflow template carries the same repair, so that half reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1389)

- the release gate now reads `.gaia/VERSION` the same way the audit gate does, instead of being the one reader left answering differently. It deleted every whitespace character in the whole file and concatenated what was left, where every other gate takes the first non-blank line and trims it, so the two disagreed on any version file holding more than one non-blank line: one resolved `1.6.1` where the other resolved `1.6.12.0.0`, and the release halted naming a version string present nowhere in the file and matching no tag anyone could cut. Nothing fails today, since the file holds a single line, but the place this drift surfaces is a release tag, which is the expensive place to meet a confusing error. The gate now calls the same helper the audit path calls rather than growing its own matching copy of the read, because a copy agrees on the day it lands and diverges silently afterwards, which is the failure that helper exists to prevent. The test that pins the read to one file also widens to reach workflow and template files, closing the gap that let this copy sit outside every check, and a second assertion holds the release gate to the helper in the direction that pin cannot see. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1386)

- the version literal the audit gate compares to decide whether a clearance still applies is now derived in one place rather than seven. Reading `.gaia/VERSION` and normalizing it is a three-line idiom, and it sat copy-pasted byte-for-byte across the producers that stamp that literal into the GAIA-Audit trailer and commit status and the readers that compare it back, spread over three separate trees. Seven copies of one equality is not redundancy: correct any single one of them and that side starts answering differently, the comparison stops matching, and the audit re-runs on every push with no error reported anywhere, because every component is behaving exactly as written. A byte-order mark in the version file is the realistic trigger, since it survives the carriage-return strip and is not whitespace, so it is carried into the version and the two sides agree today only by the accident of being identically wrong. The idiom now lives in one sourced helper every site calls; the normalization itself is unchanged, so no marker already stamped is invalidated, and a test asserts the idiom exists in exactly one tracked file, which means a reintroduced copy reds where it lands rather than releases later where the two literals finally disagree. Each site keeps its own answer for a missing version file, which the sites differ on deliberately, and each now refuses rather than guesses when the helper cannot be loaded. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1383)

- the guard that keeps `vitest/globals` out of a tsconfig now recognizes every way JSON can spell the slash inside that string. It matched the literal `/` and nothing else, so both backslash-escaped spellings reached the file untouched even though a TypeScript config loader decodes each of them to exactly the same banned option. The evasion was silent in the only direction that matters: a block message is this hook's sole output, so a write that slipped past was indistinguishable from a write that had nothing to block, and `describe`/`expect`/`test` went ambient with no sign that a guard had been consulted at all. Nothing escapes a forward slash in a hand-written tsconfig, so what closes here is a deliberate-evasion class rather than a live failure, and the limit that remains, that a unicode escape can spell every other character of the string too, is unbounded and is now stated beside the match instead of left to be rediscovered. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1342)

- a tech-debt issue dropped from a batch mid-fix can no longer close itself on merge with nothing fixed. `/gaia-debt` designates the pull-request body as the carrier of the `Closes #N` line for each issue in a batch, and that is the copy it corrects when a member leaves the unit, but a squash merge concatenates the branch's commit bodies into the merge commit message and GitHub reads closing keywords out of that message too. A trailer written into a commit while the member was still in the unit therefore survived the descope and closed the issue anyway, marked completed, and the correction to the pull-request body had no bearing on the outcome it was made to produce. The direction of the failure is what made it worth closing: the issue leaves the backlog, stops appearing in the ordering, and stops counting toward the statusline total, so the work is not deferred but lost, and a closed issue looks exactly like a fixed one. The playbook now forbids a closing keyword in any commit message on a debt branch, so the pull-request body is the sole carrier in fact rather than by convention; a member dropped after its commits exist has those commit messages rewritten alongside the body edit; and after the merge lands the run checks the issues the merge actually closed against the ones it intended to close, reopening and reporting a mismatch rather than leaving it to be noticed. The two screens that peel a member before any code is written, the security divert and the spec handoff, owe no rewrite only because they run ahead of the commit step, which the playbook now says outright so a later reordering is a deliberate act rather than a silent reintroduction. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1378)

- five ways the CI-workflow guard suite could report green over something it had not established are closed. It read the per-leg package list from a gate without checking the gate's polarity, so an inverted condition would have yielded an identical list meaning the exact complement; it consumed the sharder's file listing in a form that discarded the sharder's own failure status, so a shard that could not be listed read as a shard needing nothing; it scanned test suites but not the helper scripts those suites source, where the same dependency can hide; it folded a scan's hard-error status into its clean-miss status at one call site, so an unreadable tree would have been reported as a specific wrong finding; and one fixture skipped rather than failed when its precondition was unmet on CI, where every neighbouring gate fails. Each fix was checked against a copy of the guard with that fix reverted, so the tests are demonstrated to catch what they claim. Separately, the CLI lint cache key now covers the formatting config that decides part of that lint's verdict. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1362)

- a state-registry conformance test no longer reds purely because of which checkout it ran from. One assertion was anchored on the directory the test file itself sits in, while the function under test correctly answers the main checkout's location, so running the suite from a linked worktree failed that single test and the red looked like it belonged to whatever branch was being worked on. That misattribution was the common case rather than an exotic one: GAIA's own isolation guidance routes work into a worktree whenever HEAD is off the default branch, and the repository's pre-release checklist names a runner that hits this directly. The assertion now anchors on the resolved main root, which is the property the function actually has, and the suite passes from a worktree and from the main checkout alike. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1367)

- a plan run under worktree isolation can follow its own instructions again. The plan folder lives in the main checkout by design, and the guidance told the orchestrator to write its progress ledger and its run sentinel there with the ordinary edit tools, on the grounds that GAIA's own worktree write-guard exempts exactly those ledgers. That exemption is real, but it is not the only thing deciding the outcome: Claude Code isolates a worktree session to that worktree and refuses an edit whose target resolves to the shared checkout, above and before GAIA's guard ever runs, and a linked worktree reaches the shared state through a single symlink, so both spellings of the path land on the same refusal. The instruction then closed by forbidding the one route that does work, so an orchestrator following it literally could not write its ledger at all, while one that improvised broke a stated prohibition; neither is what the paragraph intended, and the ledger is what carries sub-agent findings across context compression and makes a run resumable. The guidance now splits by isolation mode, keeps the edit tools where they work, sanctions a shell write at the main-checkout path where they do not, and requires reading the file back to confirm its content and location, since the failure the discipline exists to prevent is a write landing in the wrong tree; the prohibition it replaces still holds for every other target. The SPEC save step, which writes into the main checkout the same way, now names the same fallback. This is not a rare path: isolation is forced to worktree mode whenever a run starts from a branch other than the default one. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1368)

- a change to `.gitignore`, `.editorconfig` or `LICENSE` no longer pulls in a code auditor with no lens for it. The merge gate's out-of-scope allowlist did not carry them, so such a change resolved an empty member set, fell through to the legacy gate, and was held until the default member cleared it. That member's globs reach none of those three files, so it certified a surface outside its own remit, and it did so at the cost of its entire prompt plus three whole-repo analysis passes that had nothing to look at. The allowlist now carries them. One predicate answers this question for all three places that ask it, so they move together rather than apart: the spawn oracle stops naming a member, the merge gate stops demanding that member's clearance, and the content digest stops rotating over files the member never reads, which means an unrelated `.gitignore` edit no longer invalidates a standing clearance and buys a fresh review. Everything else holds still: a root file that is in scope and owned by nobody fails closed to the default member as before, and `public/**` deliberately stays in scope, because the tree carries a service worker under it and executed JavaScript is precisely what that member reviews. Reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1358)

- editing a bats suite no longer invalidates every pre-merge audit clearance. The machinery-path definition's own header says bats suites are excluded from it, since a test file decides none of the things that definition turns on, but a directory-prefix entry a few lines below swept in the fourteen suites sitting beside the audit gate scripts, so the definition argued for one behaviour and implemented the opposite. A machinery path enters every member's content digest and widens the CI base resolver to the whole pull-request diff, so a test-only edit rotated five standing markers and bought five full-diff re-reviews. The exclusion is now applied when a path is classified rather than left to the shape of the list, so a new gate script landing under that prefix stays covered instead of trading one fail-open for another, and review coverage of the suites themselves is unchanged, the roster's own bats globs still dispatch a real member to them. The suites in question are release-excluded, so the corrected classification reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` and is inert there unless their own clone carries bats files (#1357)

- a shipped wiki page no longer points you at a maintainer-only directory your clone does not contain, and the release check whose whole job is catching that class no longer lets the bare form through. Nearly every directory that check watches was written so only a reference continuing past the directory name matched, so naming the directory on its own passed silently while an unrelated release scan failed the build on the very same line, the two checks disagreeing about one line with the responsible one failing open. The check now matches a directory named on its own as well as one used as a path prefix, and the single live instance that surfaced, in the init workflow page's account of how the router recognises a template-source tree, now describes what the router looks for instead of naming the path (#1352)

- the bash empty-array lint gate now covers GAIA's own test bash under `.gaia/tests/`, the last framework tree outside its reach. On stock macOS `/bin/bash` (3.2.57) a bare expansion of an empty array aborts under `set -u`, and the test suites all run under bash 5, where it does not, so a static scan is the only thing that can see the class at all; that scan simply did not look at this tree. The gap is not theoretical: the last time the class appeared there it was a human reading a diff who caught it, which is precisely the job this gate exists to take over. The twenty-nine expansions the widened scan reports are guarded here, every one of them provably non-empty at its call site so nothing changes at runtime, which is the point: a static gate is worth having only when it has no exceptions to remember. Maintainer-only enforcement, and the widened gate reaches adopters on their next `/update-gaia` inert, since a clone never receives `.gaia/tests` (#1344)

- the guard that keeps `vitest/globals` out of your TypeScript config now covers every write it is registered for. It is registered for the multi-edit tool alongside single edits and whole-file writes, but that tool carries its text somewhere the guard was not looking, so the guard scanned nothing, found nothing to block, and allowed the write, with no message and no failure, which is indistinguishable from an edit it genuinely had nothing to say about. It also matched only a config named exactly `tsconfig.json`, leaving out the sibling files a Vite-shaped project splits its config across, so `vitest/globals` reaching `tsconfig.node.json` or `tsconfig.app.json` made `describe` and `expect` ambient there and hid missing imports from the type checker just the same; the match now covers any JSON file whose name carries `tsconfig` in any case, deliberately generously rather than by a list of known spellings, because `tsc` reads whatever config it is pointed at and this guard answers with a remedy rather than a build failure. Case matters more than it looks: on a case-insensitive filesystem `TSConfig.json` resolves to the real config, so the old case-sensitive match let the write land there under a different spelling of the same file. It stays inside one path segment, so a directory happening to be named `tsconfig` does not drag every JSON file beneath it into the guard. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1317)

- recording your audit mode during `/setup-gaia` no longer aborts at random on a busy machine. The helper that writes your entry into `.gaia/audit-ci.yml` reads the current value back through GAIA's config reader, and it stopped reading the moment it found the line it wanted, which kills the reader while it is still writing the lines that follow. Whether it is still writing at that instant comes down to scheduling, so the failure came and went with machine load, wrote no value, and gave no reason; on GAIA's own pull requests it was turning a required check red about half the time, which is how it surfaced. The suite that exercises the helper also now isolates itself from an inherited git environment, which could otherwise have sent those writes at the real config file rather than the test fixture (#1318)

- a hand-run of the RED-ledger signal helper against a large test file no longer ends in a stack trace when you pipe it into a reader that stops early. The helper emits one record per test, so a file with a few thousand tests produces more output than a pipe holds, and a reader such as `head` closing partway left the unfinished write unhandled and the run exiting non-zero. Nothing GAIA runs on your behalf was affected, because every internal caller reads the output to the end; this reached you only on the manual invocation the helper's README documents. The three sibling helpers beside it now follow the same exit shape, which matters less for their own small output than for the next helper somebody writes by copying one of them: the shape they carried before is the one that truncates output silently once it grows past what a pipe holds (#1333)

- the `/gaia-plan` reference no longer points adopters at issue numbers in their own tracker. One passage carried a pair of bare GAIA issue numbers, and on your clone those resolve against whatever issues you happen to have, or against nothing. The numbers said nothing the passage did not already explain, so they are gone rather than rewritten. The shipped-reference gate cannot see this class, because it deliberately skips Markdown, so the remaining GitHub references on the files GAIA ships were swept by hand and the rest were confirmed to stand on their own (#1336)

- a `gaia` subcommand named after a built-in JavaScript object member is now rejected as unknown, rather than being accepted as real. Eleven of the CLI's command dispatchers looked their subcommand up in a way that also found members every object inherits, so `gaia wiki constructor` and `gaia update toString` exited zero having run nothing, and `valueOf`, `hasOwnProperty`, and `__proto__` failed with an internal error instead of a usage message. The silent success is the damaging half: a mistyped or mis-templated subcommand in a script or a continuous-integration step reported success, and the step it stood for simply never ran. The same defect let the service scaffolder accept one of those names as a field type and write a service whose generated parser was not valid source, while reporting the files written. Ordinary unknown subcommands and every other command's behavior are unchanged, and the lookup now lives in one place with a check that fails if a future dispatcher reintroduces the shortcut. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia`

- the changed-paths coverage check now understands a filter whose value is written as a single glob instead of a list. The filter action accepts both spellings; read as a bare string, the value was walked one character at a time and each character treated as a glob of its own, so a filter written that way would have reported every one of its step's inputs as unreachable. That is the safe direction to be wrong in, a wall of false alarms rather than a false clearance, but it is still wrong, and because nothing in the tree uses the shape today a regression here would be invisible to every other case in the suite, so a test pins it. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1314)

- the pre-merge audit's findings block now reaches the pull request, instead of a placeholder standing in for it. The local reviewer composes one machine-readable block summarising what every dispatched reviewer found and posts it as a single comment that later runs update in place. What it actually posted was a reference to the temporary file holding that block rather than the block's contents, and the API accepted it and answered success, so the step reported the findings posted every time. Two things followed. The recurring-finding analysis behind `/gaia-harden` reads those comments to decide which findings keep coming back, and had been reading nothing, so an empty candidate list was indistinguishable from having no recurring findings. And the update-in-place lookup identifies its own comment by a marker the posted body no longer carried, so each reviewer added another comment rather than updating the one before it. Every test suite covering this shared the same wrong assumption about the posting call and so passed against the defect; they now model what the call really does and fail on it, and the script is checked for the mistake directly besides. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1303)

- the boundary that stops a code review audit from repairing its own merge gate now covers the whole `.github/` tree rather than the workflow files alone. The audit decides whether to post its success status by running a few scripts kept beside the workflow, under `.github/audit/`, and that directory sat outside the refusal set, so the one reviewer permitted to push its own fixes could edit the script that computes which reviewers still owe a clearance. Emptying that list makes the gate report success while a co-dispatched reviewer never cleared, which is the gate failing open, the one direction it must never fail. Both enforcement points read the same refusal set, the local edit guard and the push gate in continuous integration, so neither one caught it. Nothing under `.github/` was ever a legitimate target for an automated repair, so the tree is refused whole, which also covers whatever is added beside it later instead of waiting for an audit to notice the next gap. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1291)

- a file whose name carries an accented character, or any other byte git treats as unusual, no longer makes a required check report green having done nothing. The same path-quoting class fixed one file at a time four times over now has a check behind it, and turning that check on found five more live instances, three of which failed open in the worst available direction: such a path could make the test suite skip entirely, make the code review audit skip entirely, and let an automated fix touch a protected surface the refusal set exists to keep it away from. Each of the three reported its required check green while doing none of the work, which is indistinguishable from a genuinely clean run. Every place that reads the file names git prints for a change now switches git's path quoting off, and each keeps the failure behaviour it had before, so a git error still fails the step it always failed. The new check is maintainer-only and covers the continuous-integration workflow files as well as shell, which is where the three fail-open cases were and where no existing lint could see them. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1229)

- the CLI subcommand reachability guard no longer reads a live command as dead when the only thing that invokes it is a quoted invocation. Some subcommands are invoked through a release-resolved path rather than by bare name, which is what lets an adopter whose installed binary predates a subcommand reach it during an update at all, and the closing quote on that path sat exactly where the guard required a space, so the guard saw no invoker and reported a command nothing had stopped using. Clearing that red meant mentioning the command's bare form somewhere else purely to satisfy the guard, and every future subcommand invoked only that way would have hit the same thing. A quoted invocation now counts as evidence on its own, which is consistent with the guard already counting an invocation written in ordinary prose. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1037)

- `/gaia-wiki sync` no longer drops a commit from the window it recovers after its recorded stopping point is orphaned. A squash merge routinely orphans that marker, so the recovery is the ordinary path rather than a rare one, and it resolved a replacement starting point by timestamp: the newest commit at or older than the moment the previous sync ran. A commit's date is not when it reached the trunk, though. A merge commit carries the merged branch's original dates onto the trunk when it lands, so a commit written before the last sync can arrive after it, and the recovery could settle on that very commit as the starting point. The window begins after the starting point, so the commit that most needed evaluating was the one excluded, along with anything behind it on the same branch, and nothing announced it: no conflict, no warning, and a drift count that reads zero. The starting point is now resolved along the trunk's own first-parent chain, which lands on an integration point instead. The trade is deliberate and runs one way: the new starting point is at or before the old one, so a sync may re-catalogue commits it has already seen rather than miss commits it never saw. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1059)
- the session-start janitor's reap of abandoned worktrees no longer refuses every real candidate, and no longer deletes one on a question it could not answer. It reclaims a worktree under `.claude/worktrees/` once its branch is provably merged, and proved that per commit, by patch id; a squash merge lands the whole branch upstream as one commit, so the test matched nothing as soon as a branch carried two commits, and nothing at all for the empty trailer commit the audit gate leaves. That is why abandoned worktrees accumulated instead of being cleaned up. The comparison is now over the branch's combined diff, and it is made byte-for-byte rather than with whitespace normalized, so an unpushed formatting-only commit still reads as work and still stops the reap. The more serious half was silent: a failed read of the branch's diff, of its patch id, or of the worktree's own working-tree status printed nothing, and the old code took that silence for "nothing unpushed" or "tree is clean" and reaped on it, destroying commits that existed nowhere else. Every read now carries its own exit status, so an unanswerable question keeps the worktree every time, and a branch whose commits cancel each other out is kept rather than treated as empty. A second, independent cause of the same symptom is fixed alongside it: the sweep that reaps spent audit markers was overwriting the default branch name that later sweeps read, so on any checkout holding even one marker, which is every real one, the reap could not resolve what to compare against and declined everything. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1256)
- the `Run /gaia-audit` indicator can now clear itself when the audit's answer was to file rather than to trim. Three of the four conditions behind that indicator reset when an audit completes; the fourth is a live measurement of how far the always-loaded files have grown past their budgets, and it has no such reset, so an audit that correctly recorded an oversized file as tech-debt instead of shrinking it left the indicator returning the moment it was dismissed. From the operator's seat the command appeared to do nothing: you ran it, watched the indicator clear, and watched it come straight back. A file that already has an open tech-debt issue is now left to the debt indicator to report, so a completed audit leaves the audit indicator clear and the work stays visible in the queue that tracks it. Suppression is deliberately keyed to the issue rather than to anything local, so one developer's audit run cannot quiet a condition for everybody else, and the size at which a file was set aside is remembered so that a file which keeps growing starts nudging again rather than hiding behind a stale issue. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1150)
- `gaia init rename` no longer reports a rename it did not perform. Its rewrite of the seeded language files skipped any value wrapped in one quote while holding the other, so a project whose `common.ts` had diverged to `siteName: "Steve's Template"` came back byte-unchanged at exit 0 with the step recorded as completed, and a title carrying the quote the command itself had left bare could never be renamed a second time. Those values are now rewritten, and a key that is present but holds something no rewrite can reach, a template literal, a computed value, a bare identifier, now fails the step by name before any file is touched, the way a missing `CLAUDE.md` heading already does. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1197)
- the local rate card no longer announces itself as active while pricing nothing. Its shape check accepted any array as a model's list of rate windows, so a card naming a model with an empty list, or with bare numbers in place of rate objects, merged and got announced as in effect: the first priced that model at zero on the same readout that reported the model as unpriced, and the second failed the whole dollar figure with a stated reason that never mentioned the card you had just hand-written. Both are now refused up front, naming the file, exactly as every other malformed card already is, and pricing falls back to the shipped rates. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1199)
- the two gates that hold a pull request to answering its own distribution surface no longer wave through a file whose name carries an accented character or any other byte git treats as unusual. Both compare the file names git prints for a change against the release manifest's list of unanswered files, and both left git's default path quoting switched on, so such a name arrived quoted on one side and raw on the other and matched nothing: the required check reported green and the local pre-flight allowed the pull request to be opened, for a newly-shipping file with no ship-or-withhold answer. Neither failure announces itself, since a green check and a silent pre-flight are also what a correctly-answered branch produces. Both gates now execute their real logic under test against a repository carrying such a path, the local one and its continuous-integration mirror alike, because the pre-flight's whole value is being the cheap early warning for the check that gates the merge, and the two have to agree. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1228, #1230)
- two more gates no longer wave through a file whose name carries an accented character or any other byte git treats as unusual. Both read the file names git prints for a change, and both left git's default path quoting switched on, which wraps such a name in quotes and escapes its bytes so it matches nothing either gate is looking for. The audit's incremental review base stopped noticing that gate machinery itself had changed, which is the condition that widens the next round back to the whole pull request, so that round quietly reviewed only the newest slice under rules that had moved underneath it. The merge-time worthiness check stopped seeing the very test files it exists to hold and reported nothing to hold, which is indistinguishable from a clean run. Both take the same spelling as the membership fix above. Reaching either needs a tracked file whose name carries such a byte, so this is a narrow case, but neither failure announces itself: each looks exactly like the ordinary nothing-to-do outcome. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1224, #1225)
- a file whose name carries an accented character, or any other byte git treats as unusual, no longer merges without the specialist reviewer whose remit it falls in. Every derivation deciding which Code Audit Team members a pull request owes left git's default path quoting switched on, which wraps such a path in quotes and escapes its bytes, and the resulting token matches no member's declared ownership. The membership resolver then answers with no owners at all, the dispatch falls through to the default member, and the merge completes looking audited while the reviewer who owns the file never saw it; a member reaching that same list on its own skipped itself for the same reason, and a clean skip is indistinguishable from having nothing to review. The roster check gained an assertion that stops a future derivation being added without the fix, because this defect existed only in the first place because an earlier repair landed on one of two sibling derivations in the same file and nothing noticed the other. Note that this changes the audit agent definitions, which invalidates any clearance already earned on an open pull request; re-run its audit members before merging. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1213)
- the guard that keeps lint errors from being silenced in config now tells you what it is actually refusing. It denies every edit to `eslint.config.*` on the filename alone, including the `...lint.reactRouter` spread the React Router item above asks you to confirm, while its message asserted that any edit there is a lint error being silenced: wrong about that migration, and leaving Claude no next step but to give up or work around the hook. The message now says the denial is on the filename alone, names that migration as a legitimate edit, and points you at making it by hand, or at asking whoever runs the project to disable the hook briefly, while telling Claude not to disable it on its own. The denial stays blunt on purpose, because adding a preset spread is both the sanctioned migration and a way to turn rules off (a later preset overrides an earlier one), and nothing in the text of an edit tells those two apart. The guard also now covers `eslint.config.mts` and `.cts`, two of the six filenames ESLint actually resolves: a project using either had no guard at all and no message saying so. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1218)
- a released CHANGELOG section no longer carries every heading twice. Cutting a release put the auto-drafted block, rendered from commit subjects, and the hand-written `## [Unreleased]` entries under the same dated version heading, so the shipped version block listed `### Added`, `### Changed` and `### Fixed` once per source and the duplication then entered permanent history. The hand-written entries are the release content, since every merge already gates on one and they carry the prose a commit subject cannot; graduating now renames the `## [Unreleased]` heading in place and leaves them where they are. The drafted block stays a review aid printed for the maintainer to fold in by hand, and a release with nothing written under `## [Unreleased]` is refused rather than dated. Maintainer-only tooling; adopters see it in the release notes it produces (#1217)
- `gaia update regen-regions --help` no longer sends you looking in the wrong part of your tree for a write it reported. The command confines what a region's regeneration writes outside the paths that region declares, and the help text described that as a straight split: undone inside the region's own directories, reported anywhere else. Most reported paths are in fact inside those directories, because the command reports any path it cannot put back, or cannot establish it would put back safely, rather than guessing. The text also read as though every reported path is a write, when a path holding nothing to read, such as a FIFO or a socket, is reported on every run whether or not the regeneration touched it. The help text and the update-workflow documentation now state the rule the command follows. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1215)
- GAIA's two workspaces no longer check your code against different versions of the TypeScript rule set. The shared lint preset pulls `typescript-eslint` in indirectly through a floating range, so each workspace's lockfile froze its own version and the existing pin check could not see the gap: the repository and GAIA's maintainer CLI were linting against rule sets six minor releases apart, both reporting green. Both now resolve the same version, and a check fails when they drift apart again rather than leaving the divergence silent. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1155)
- installs into GAIA's own maintainer CLI workspace now run under the same supply-chain rules as the rest of the repository. That workspace is a separate pnpm root, so it inherited none of the repository's hardening and resolved dependencies with no waiting period on freshly published versions and no check for a package's trust level dropping. Switching the rules on rejected nine packages that the previous unhardened resolve had already committed, so that workspace's lockfile is rebuilt under the policy. Maintainer-only; nothing an adopter installs is affected (#1152)
- re-pricing the cost ledger no longer discards a cost record written while it runs. The re-price rewrites the whole ledger, and cost recording deliberately writes without waiting when the shared lock is busy, so that it never delays a commit; a record landing in that gap was dropped outright, and the backup taken beforehand did not hold it either. The re-price now carries through anything that arrived before it replaces the file, and refuses outright rather than guessing if the ledger changed underneath it in a way it cannot reconcile. The remaining gap is now a fraction of a second rather than the length of the whole run, and both the code and the cost documentation say so plainly instead of claiming the case is closed. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1091)
- `gaia init` no longer writes unusable language files when the project title carries an apostrophe or a dollar sign. The rename step copies your title into the seeded English strings, and an ordinary title such as `Steve's App` closed the quoted value early, while a title carrying `$1` or `$&` pasted a fragment of the file back into itself; either way the project's own language files were left unparseable while the step reported success and recorded itself done, and re-running the step compounded the damage instead of settling on the title you asked for. Your title is now escaped for the value it is written into, everywhere the step writes one, and it is still accepted rather than refused, since an apostrophe belongs in a legitimate project title. Both `gaia init` steps that write a title into a source file now share one escaping rule, so the two cannot drift apart. Affects new projects at initialization; existing adopters pick the fix up on their next `/update-gaia` (#1193)
- `gaia init` no longer leaves a freshly scaffolded project unable to start Storybook when the project title carries a line break. The step that removes GAIA's branding writes your title into the Storybook sidebar's configuration as a quoted value, and a title with a line ending in it split that value across two lines and left the file unparseable, so Storybook failed to start or build while the step reported success and recorded itself done. A blank title is refused on the same terms, since it writes a generated README whose heading carries no project name. Both are now refused before anything at all is written, matching the rename step, and both `gaia init` steps that take a title share one check so the rules cannot drift apart. Ordinary punctuation is still accepted rather than refused: an apostrophe or a `$` is part of a legitimate title, and the branding step writes it correctly. Affects new projects at initialization; existing adopters pick the fix up on their next `/update-gaia` (#1191)
- a pre-merge audit reviewer no longer risks publishing another reviewer's findings as its own. The reviewers run at the same time in one shared workspace, and each was told to hand its report to the writer from the same staging file, so one reviewer's findings could overwrite another's before they were published, and findings left behind by an earlier round could be published again as a fresh report. That report is what the merge gate reads to tell a real review from one whose findings were lost, so neither case showed up anywhere: the gate still went green, over findings attributed to the wrong reviewer. Reviewers now hand their findings straight to the writer instead of staging a file at all, so there is no shared name to collide on and nothing left behind for the next round to pick up. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1187)
- `gaia init` no longer accepts a project title carrying a line break, which quietly added lines to your `CLAUDE.md` every time the rename step ran. The title is written into the file's first `# ` heading, so a title with a line ending in it landed there as two lines, and the check that makes the step safe to re-run compared only the first of them against the heading it had just rebuilt. That never matched, so each run appended the extra lines again while reporting success and recording the step as done, against the step's own promise that re-running it with the same arguments changes nothing. Such a title is now refused before anything is written, with a named error, rather than trimmed or escaped: the title is a value the step is given, not one it may rewrite, which is how the companion project-slug argument already behaves. A blank title is refused on the same terms, since writing one leaves the file with a heading and no title, the state the step already declines to produce when the heading is missing outright. Affects new projects at initialization; existing adopters pick the fix up on their next `/update-gaia` (#1182)
- `/gaia-audit` can once again propose moving detail out of an over-budget instruction file and onto a wiki page. Doing that means writing the section to the wiki and taking it out of the file it came from, and the playbook expressed those as two separate steps in a fixed order that ran the removal first; the write then saw the file it was about to read from change underneath it and stood down, which would leave a link pointing at a page that was never created. The route was withdrawn rather than left in that state, and since it is the only one of the three ways to shrink an over-budget file that reaches the wiki, a file whose real fix was that route had no fix at all. The write now takes the section out of the source itself, as one step, and it touches your file only after the wiki page is written, so a failure anywhere leaves the file exactly as it was. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1143)
- `/gaia-audit`'s optional verification round no longer offers to skip itself on the reports it most exists to check. The round asks once whether to run, and it could recommend skipping whenever every proposed edit was reversible through git, but that wording also covered the edits that resolve a contradiction between one of your local files and the wiki, which the same gate lists as a reason to run. A report holding nothing but contradiction fixes satisfied both answers at once, so the safe-looking one could be the one presented as recommended. Skipping is now offered only when no proposed edit resolves a contradiction, and everything else is recommended for verification. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1184)
- `/gaia-audit` no longer reports an auto-loaded file that is over its size budget as unfixable when only one of the three ways to fix it is unavailable. The playbook offered three, moving detail to the wiki, consolidating duplicated sections, or splitting the file into narrower ones, but presented them as a menu with nothing saying they are independent. Only the first needs the step a same-file ordering hazard blocks, so a run that hit that blocker stopped there and proposed nothing at all, for every over-budget file, on every round, while the two remedies that were never blocked went unevaluated. The three are now considered in turn, a blocker is recorded against the remedy rather than the file, and a split, which none of the audit's action types can carry out, is filed as tracked work naming the section to lift and the sizes both files land at, rather than dropped. The budget is also stated as per file, matching the check that raises the over-budget indicator, so splitting one oversized rule into two that are each under budget is a real answer rather than an open question. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1149)
- `gaia init` no longer reports a successful rename while leaving your `CLAUDE.md` untitled, and no longer rewrites a line of your own code instead of the title. That step gives the file's first `# ` heading your project's name, and it looked for that heading with a search that read the whole file at once and did not know what a fenced code block is. A `CLAUDE.md` with no heading matched nothing, so nothing was written and the step returned as though it had worked; a `CLAUDE.md` whose first `# ` line sat inside a shell example matched that comment, so the step reported success, still left no title, and replaced a line of the example with your project's name. The title is now taken from the first `# ` line above the file's first fenced block, and a `CLAUDE.md` with no heading there stops the step with a named error before anything at all has been renamed. Add a heading, re-run that one command, and `gaia init resume` carries on with the remaining steps; a `CLAUDE.md` you deleted outright is still passed over. The heading the step depends on is restored to the shipped template and pinned by a test, so a later template edit that drops it fails in GAIA's own checks rather than in your scaffold. Affects new projects at initialization; an already-initialized project is unaffected beyond picking up the template's heading line on its next `/update-gaia` (#1176)
- `/update-gaia`'s run summary no longer undercounts the files it updated, and its region reporting no longer mischaracterizes what it asks you to look at. The summary carried a row for every bucket of the merge except one: files GAIA shares with you, or seeds into your wiki, that it updated cleanly because you had not edited them were counted and never printed, which on a wiki-heavy release is the largest group of the run and the easiest to miss, since every row you did see was accurate. They now print on a `Merged:` row of their own. The confined-writes report also told you a flagged entry meant a stray write outside a region's own directories that could not be undone, when several of the cases producing one sit squarely inside them and one is not a write at all, so an untouched named pipe, or a file the runner deliberately declined to restore, read as debris the regeneration had left behind. And a failed regeneration now carries the remedy for each kind of failure, where the report had pointed at paragraphs that stated none and left the advice to be improvised. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1117, #1131, #1132, #1171)
- `/gaia-wiki sync` no longer refuses to land a wiki-only change that renames a page whose name carries a non-ASCII character. Git reports such a rename as a single quoted, byte-escaped pair of paths, and the check deciding whether the working tree holds anything outside `wiki/` split that pair down the middle into two half-paths, one of which no longer begins with `wiki/`, so the sync announced non-wiki changes and aborted; the wiki chain's own commit step read the same answer and took the same wrong branch. Both now ask git for NUL-delimited records, which git never quotes and in which a rename's origin path arrives as its own entry, and that parsing is now shared with the region-regeneration runner that already worked this way rather than kept as a second copy free to drift. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1115)
- `/update-gaia`'s region regeneration no longer stalls forever on a named pipe, and no longer silently skips a region whose declared path is written in an unusual form.
