Copying a declared path aside before regenerating it opened that file, and opening a named pipe waits for a writer that never arrives, so the update stopped dead partway through a half-written tree with no message, no error, and no exit; a declared path that is a named pipe, a socket, or a device node, or a link to one, is now reported and the run carries on, none of them being something a backup could ever put back. Separately, a declared path carrying a trailing slash or an interior `/./` never matched the runner's own record of that file, so the runner read the region's freshly written output as a stray write, put the old content back, and still reported the region as regenerated; such a path is now recognized as the file it names. Neither is reachable through a stock release: one needs a hand-edited region declaration, the other a named pipe where a shipped file belongs. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1170, #1174)
- `/update-gaia`'s region regeneration no longer deletes files you already had, and no longer writes outside the project, while undoing a regeneration command's stray writes. Its promise is that anything the command touched beside a generated region is put back and nothing else is written anywhere; two things broke it. A file or a directory the runner could not read when it took its before-picture was indistinguishable afterwards from something the command had just created, so a command that did no more than relax its permissions was enough to have it deleted and the loss reported as a stray write cleaned up; anything the runner cannot read into, including a node such as a FIFO or a socket that holds nothing to read in the first place, is now recorded as present, reported, and left alone. And where a directory beside or above the region was a symlink, or the command left one where such a directory had been, a revert could resolve through that link and land wherever it pointed, including outside the project entirely, while the report named a path inside it; links are now never followed at any depth, above the region's directories as well as inside them, and the command's own creations are undone before anything is put back. One consequence is worth knowing: nothing inside a region directory that is a symlink, or that sits behind one, is reverted any more, because none of it can be checked without following the link that leads there. The directory is reported instead, so a later run cannot mistake what it never examined for something the command created. A permission-only change beside the region is also reverted now, rather than passing as no change at all. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1128, #1130)
- a `.gaia/audit-ci.yml` the release build cannot read no longer sends the maintainer to fix roster membership. Any failure to read the roster was swallowed into an empty auditor set, so the build failed by naming a Code Audit Team agent definition and advising that a roster member be re-added or that the file's generated-region markers be deleted, neither of which repairs a syntax error or a truncated write in the roster itself. The build now names the roster, tells a file it cannot read apart from one that is not valid YAML or carries no settings at all, and passes the parser's own line and column straight through. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1044)
- `gaia --help` now lists all four `update` subcommands instead of one. The summary named only `merge-workspace` and printed that subcommand's flags, so `merge-audit-ci`, the command `/update-gaia` itself runs on your machine to resolve a conflicted `.gaia/audit-ci.yml`, could not be found from the top-level help at all, and the flags printed beside it are rejected by `regen-regions`, which takes `--manifest` and `--root`. `gaia update --help` is unaffected and remains the single flag reference. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1164)
- the commit classifier behind `/gaia-wiki sync` no longer answers a failed git invocation with "there is nothing to review". A git call that failed to run was reported as an empty commit range and a success exit, which is byte-for-byte what a genuinely up-to-date wiki looks like, so anything downstream that trusted the zero count read a broken git as a clean sync. It now reports a structured `git_failed` error and exits non-zero. This makes the failure visible rather than preventing it: the difference is between a sync that quietly does nothing and one that tells you to run it again (#1135)
- `/gaia-debt`'s backlog dedup-key parser no longer drops the key for any issue whose cited path contains a space, a shape that covers roughly half of all wiki pages. A truncated key silently disabled that issue from clustering with related findings, with no visible symptom (#1118)
- `gaia update merge-workspace` no longer treats a token named after a built-in JavaScript object property (`constructor`, `toString`, `valueOf`, `__proto__`, `hasOwnProperty`) as a known flag, and the release manifest no longer treats a shipped file named after one of those properties as a path it has already accounted for. A malformed `merge-workspace` invocation swallowed the argument after the bad token and then reported a clean, no-change merge rather than an error, which is the worst way for a mistyped command to fail: it looks like a successful answer. On the release side, a file with such a name would have been dropped from the drift report and the distribution gate that together guarantee every shipped file has been accounted for, so it was fixed while no such file exists rather than after one does. This is the same fault already fixed in `merge-audit-ci` below, and with these two, every command that reads a lookup this way now rejects those names. Separately, the `merge-workspace` source no longer contains a raw NUL byte that made git and standard text tools classify the whole file as binary, so changes to it now show up in an ordinary diff and in `git grep`. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1111, #1112, #1113)
- `gaia update merge-audit-ci` no longer treats a token named after a built-in JavaScript object property (`constructor`, `toString`, `valueOf`, `__proto__`, `hasOwnProperty`) as a known flag. Such a token slipped past the unknown-flag rejection and consumed the argument after it, so `gaia update merge-audit-ci constructor --json` silently discarded `--json` and emitted human-readable output to a caller that had every reason to expect JSON. Only a hand-typed invocation reaches it, and no value ever landed anywhere but the command's own local options object. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1041)
- `/update-gaia`'s region regeneration now keeps the write-confinement promise it documents. A file the regeneration command deleted alongside the region it owns was silently lost, even though the update was holding a copy of it and its own documentation promised to put back anything it could: an adopter-authored file next to a generated region could disappear during an update with the run still reporting a clean success. Such a file is now restored, and only a region's own declared files may be deleted and stay deleted. Symlinks next to a region are covered by the same promise rather than exempt from it: one the command creates is cleaned up like any other file it had no business writing, and one that was already there is put back on its original target if the command deletes it, repoints it, or swaps it for an ordinary file. Putting a link back never touches whatever it pointed at. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1033)
- four smaller misreports from the same regeneration runner are fixed with it. A regeneration program that prints more than a megabyte, or that hangs, is no longer cut off and then described as one that never started, and it is now given generous output and time limits rather than the tight ones it inherited by default, and told which of those two limits stopped it when one does; a reported path containing a space or a non-ASCII character is no longer garbled, including both sides of a rename; a project that lives in a subdirectory of a larger repository no longer has every one of its own legitimate region writes reported back as something the adopter must investigate; and a release manifest whose region list is present but the wrong shape is now named as a manifest that could not be read, instead of reporting exactly as a release that ships no regions at all. Only the last of these was reachable through a GAIA-shipped region; the rest need an adopter-authored regeneration command or a nested checkout (#1031, #1032, #1043)
- the check that keeps the pre-merge audit members writing their findings under one key no longer reports a clean result for a tree it never read. It searches the audit member definitions inside a repository it is handed, and it went straight to searching without first confirming that what it was handed was a repository root at all. A search that cannot run finds nothing, which is byte-for-byte what a search that ran and found nothing wrong reports, so a path outside any repository, a bare repository, or a subdirectory of a real one all came back clean, the one answer the check exists to withhold. It now says it could not run, and says that distinctly from saying no, which is the same guard its sibling check next door already carries; the two now read identically (#1100)
- the check that keeps the pre-merge audit members on one review base no longer clears a member that reaches its base through a renamed variable. It works by exempting the one derivation that is correct, and it recognized that derivation by a name it matched as a fragment rather than as a whole word, so a member routing the wrong derivation through a variable whose name merely ended in the right one, most plausibly one of the branch names CI already puts in the environment, was cleared for exactly what the check exists to reject. Its own notes now also record the nearest drift it cannot see at all, a member that keeps the correct shape and changes only where its base comes from, and point at the behavioural suite next door that does catch that one (#1101, #1102)
- a slow job in a re-dispatched workflow can no longer leave a required check missing after a self-heal commit. When the pre-merge audit runs in CI and pushes a fix, it re-dispatches the workflows behind the required checks and waits about 25 minutes for each one to finish before mirroring its result onto the new commit. The Tests job allowed itself an hour and neither Chromatic job set any limit at all, so any of the three could still be running when that wait expired, and a check that is never mirrored ends up absent rather than red: branch protection blocks the merge with no failure to read and nothing to re-run. Each of those jobs now stops itself comfortably inside the wait, which turns the bad case into an ordinary red check, and every one of them carries the same limit as the rest of the repo's jobs. The limits are generous against what the jobs actually take: a full test run is about four minutes and a Chromatic run about two. Part of what makes them that generous is that CI now installs only the browser it runs. The test job asked Playwright for its browsers without naming one, which fetches Chromium, Firefox and WebKit for a suite whose CI configuration runs Chromium alone, and that download was the job's longest step by a wide margin, occasionally by twenty minutes. **If you add a CI Playwright project for another engine, name that engine in the install step too**, or the run fails on a missing browser; the step's own comment says so. Browser binaries are deliberately still not cached, because Playwright advises against it and the system packages the install pulls are not cacheable anyway. A maintainer-only test asserts all of this for every job in every re-dispatched workflow, reads the ceiling from the audit's own wait so the two cannot drift apart, and charges the queue-time allowance once per job along a `needs:` chain rather than once per run, since a chained job waits in the queue on every hop. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1030)
- a queued merge no longer waits indefinitely on a `GAIA-Audit` status the pre-merge audit itself stranded. On a pull request no frontend auditor reviews, which on an adopter clone means a workflow-YAML-only change, the passing status was recorded against a commit the audit then superseded with its own audit-trailer commit, so the required check went missing from the head branch protection reads and `gh pr checks` reported the pull request as having no checks at all. Every auditor now publishes that trailer commit before recording the status, and the status is withheld rather than recorded against a commit that is about to be replaced. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1085)
- the forensics and lib test runners' fallback installer now names a package that exists. Both advertised `npx -y bats-core@latest` for a machine without bats installed, and both READMEs documented it as a supported install route, but no such package is published to npm, so on the one machine the fallback exists to serve it failed with a registry error and the suite never ran at all. The project and the Homebrew formula are both named bats-core, which is where the wrong spelling keeps coming from, so each runner now carries a note saying so; this finishes the repair the sandbox runner already received. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1081)
- the Claude Integration wiki page no longer tells adopters that `permissions.deny` carries a `Write(.env)` rule. Claude Code's file permission checks match only `Read(path)` and `Edit(path)` rules, and an `Edit` rule already covers Write, Edit, MultiEdit, and NotebookEdit, so a `Write(<glob>)` rule is accepted, never matched, and draws a startup warning naming the settings file it sits in; an adopter who followed the old page into adding one got that warning in every session and no enforcement to show for it. The page now names `Edit(<glob>)` as the write-side rule form. Maintainer-only: the same change flips the bats assertion that pinned the removed rule, and had left the release-excluded sandbox suite red on `main`, into one that pins its absence (#1074)
- the statusline's right side is no longer permanently blank in GAIA's own source repository. The mid-init gate hides every indicator while `.claude/commands/gaia-init.md` exists, which is right in a scaffolded project because `/gaia-init` deletes that file when it finishes, but in the source repository the same file is a tracked product artifact that ships to adopters and is therefore never deleted, so the maintainer never saw their own update, audit, harden, and debt nudges. The gate now makes an exception for a checkout that carries the CLI's TypeScript source, which no adopter machine has, scaffolded or not; the exception sits inside a maintainer-only block the release strip removes, so an adopter's copy is the plain gate and behaves exactly as it did before (#1058)
- the secret-scanning write guard closes two ways a real secret could reach a commit through its own placeholder allowlist. A value merely *starting* like a placeholder was allowed whatever followed it, so `API_KEY=example-<a-real-key>` passed; and the `<…>` arm matched its own closing bracket the way the command-substitution arm used to, so a literal spliced between two brackets passed. Each arm now has to match the whole value. The placeholder arms bound each *segment* rather than the total length, because what separates a placeholder from a secret is structure, short words joined by punctuation versus one unbroken run, so `your-github-personal-access-token` passes at any length while `your-aB3xK9pQ7zR2wL5t` does not; a length cap gets both directions wrong. The allowlist reads shape rather than meaning, so the honest limits are unchanged: a nested `$(… $(…))` is denied, a substitution that prints a secret is allowed, and a short enough secret behind a placeholder prefix still fits. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1054)
- the secret-scanning write guard reads shell declarations, not only dotenv lines. A leading `export`, `declare`, `typeset`, `local`, or `readonly` used to take the assignment out of the scan entirely, so `export API_KEY=<a-real-key>` was never examined at all; those keywords are now recognized along with their own options, so the idiomatic `declare -r` and `local -r` are scanned like the bare forms. Because that brings shell lines into a rule written for dotenv values, the ordinary expansion forms are allowed alongside them as whole values: a positional in either spelling, an expansion whose operand is empty, a value that is nothing but braced references, and an expansion followed by a path (`$1`, `${1}`, `${VAR:-}`, `${A}${B}`, `${ROOT}/dev.pem`). A non-empty default is not, because a default value is where a real secret would sit and a segmented secret is shaped exactly like a placeholder; the path suffix is bound segment by segment for the same reason, so the separator buys a path and not an unbounded tail. The value is judged whole before any of that, so a separator sitting *inside* it rather than after it, as in the guarded `$(cmd 2>/dev/null || true)` idiom, is never mistaken for a trailing clause; only when nothing matches does a trailing comment or `;` / `&&` / `||` clause come off, so an everyday `export FOO_KEY="$BAR" # note` is no longer hard-blocked by a deny telling its author to use the environment variable the line already uses. The tail is read rather than dropped: an executable one whose own assignment leads it is judged by the same allowlist as the first, and the guarded-substitution idiom survives there too, so a second declaration parked after a `;` is no more hard-blocked than the first one is. Every other tail is judged by shape, a run of 13 or more alphanumerics mixing letters and digits: a comment tail, and an assignment sitting behind a `{`, a `(`, or a pipe. That shape bound is the honest limit, since an all-letter or short enough secret parked in one of those positions clears it. `fake-` and `dummy-` join `your-` as placeholder prefixes. That shape rule also stopped firing on the tails carrying the most secret-shaped material of all, so a comment or trailing statement long enough would carry a key straight through while a short one holding the very same key was denied; it now fires on both, which is the direction a secret guard should fail in. Expect the guard to deny some declaration lines it previously ignored. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1065, #1066, #1067)
- the secret-scanning write guard no longer refuses an assignment whose value is computed at run time. A line such as `AUDIT_KEY="$(mint_key)"` was denied as a committed secret even though the source line holds no literal secret to leak, and because the guard gave no way through, authors worked around it in ad-hoc ways, an intermediate throwaway variable in one file, a renamed variable in another, leaving residue that the next person to tidy either file would trip over with no explanation. A value that is wholly a single command substitution is now allowed; a literal value is still denied, and so is a substitution spliced together with literal text. The allowance reads the shape of the value and not the command inside it, so it is a false-positive fix rather than a stronger guarantee: a substitution that prints a literal secret still passes, and a nested substitution is still denied. The guard also gains the test suite it never had, pinning both the widened case and every deny it must keep making. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia`
- the Audit CI Tests job now re-runs when the local shell gate it guards is edited. Every other suite in that job pairs its test directory with the source directory it covers, but the gate script sat at a path no pattern matched, so a pull request touching only that file resolved the job's change filter to false, skipped every suite, and reported green having run the assertions written to catch exactly that edit zero times. Nothing else covered the gap: the linter workflow still ran the gate and still passed, because a gate that checks fewer files simply reports fewer findings, so a dropped pass was indistinguishable from a clean tree. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1025)
- running Vitest as a directly spawned binary, rather than through a package.json script, no longer aborts a suite before any test runs. The test setup defaults the server environment variables a clean checkout would otherwise be missing, but not the package version, which pnpm sets only for the processes it launches from a script, so `pnpm exec vitest` and editor Vitest integrations failed schema validation at import time and reported zero tests while `pnpm test --run` stayed green. Those are the invocations a developer reaches for while iterating on one file. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#1021)
- a self-heal commit from the pre-merge audit can no longer wedge a pull request permanently. When the audit runs in CI and pushes a fix, GitHub fires no pull-request event for the new commit, so the audit re-dispatches the workflows that produce the required checks and mirrors their results onto it. Three of this repository's required checks were unreachable that way: their workflows could not be dispatched at all, their jobs were gated to pull requests, and none was named in the re-dispatch list, so a self-heal commit would have left them absent on the new head with no bypass short of an admin override or a hand-made empty commit. All three now run on that path, and a test asserts the invariant for every required check, because nothing in the pull-request lane exercises the self-heal path and a break is otherwise invisible until it blocks a merge. The requirement is now documented in full for adopters listing their own workflows: the workflow needs a manual-dispatch trigger, and the job producing the check must admit that event, or the job skips and the mirrored result satisfies nothing. The three workflows are maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1019)
- the committed maintainer CLI binary is rebuilt from its source, and the check that catches such drift now blocks a merge instead of only reporting it. A fix to the release leak scan changed the CLI source without rebundling, so the binary a maintainer actually runs was one fix behind and `/gaia-release` still hit the phantom leak that fix had removed. The pull-request job that rebuilds both binaries and compares them did fire, and did go red, but it was never in the branch ruleset's required set, so it could not stop the merge. `Vitest (.gaia/cli)` is now declared required and promoted in the live ruleset; it already ran on every pull request and reported green when its path filter missed, so nothing unrelated can hang on it. That check also covers the committed scaffold templates now, closing the same drift class one directory over: the templates are regenerated by the same bundle command but resolve at runtime, so comparing binary bytes alone could never see them go stale. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#1015)
- the tech-debt statusline count no longer discards a refresh request raised by a parallel worktree. The background refresher decided whether to clear its staleness marker from state sampled before a network round trip, so a `/gaia-debt` fix merging in another worktree during that window had its request deleted before anything serviced it, and the `Run /gaia-debt` nudge kept reporting a count from before that merge until the next backlog change or the six-hour expiry. The refresher now re-reads that marker immediately before clearing it. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#999, #1003)
- the worktree-creation hook now resolves the main checkout from whichever working tree fires it, so a worktree created from inside another worktree lands beside its sibling and still gets typed routes. It read its root from the current working tree instead, so such a run nested the new worktree under the inner one and skipped typegen, because the toolchain it borrows and the paths it writes to both belong to the main checkout; that skip is deliberately silent, so the only symptom was the lint noise against error typed values the typegen step exists to prevent. Every supported entry point declines to nest, so this was reachable only by invoking the hook directly from inside a worktree. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#993)
- the pre-commit gate now runs on a commit that touches only end-to-end files. Its change-detection guard named the application, test, and Storybook directories but not the Playwright one, so a commit scoped to end-to-end specs alone matched nothing, skipped typecheck, lint-staged, and the unit-test run outright, and landed unlinted and untypechecked, even while the lint configuration held a rule set waiting for exactly those files. Continuous integration still lints the same change, so this closes a developer-feedback gap rather than a hole that reached the default branch. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#994)
- a new git worktree now starts with React Router's generated typed routes, so linting inside it reports the truth. Those types are gitignored, so a fresh worktree never received them and every route file importing its generated types resolved to an error type: a lint run inside a worktree reported eight unsafe-assignment failures that do not exist on the base branch, against code the branch never touched. An agent working there either burned a cycle chasing them or edited real route code to silence an artifact of its own environment, and worktree isolation is the standard mode for agent work. The worktree borrows the main checkout's installed toolchain instead of installing its own, so creation stays fast and costs no second dependency tree. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#987, #991)
- a self-healing pre-merge audit member can no longer edit the tests that would catch its own bad repair. The repair boundary refused the shared test directory but not the Playwright tree or the Storybook configuration, which hold tests for the same reason, so a member that wrongly repaired an application file could weaken the end-to-end assertion or the visual-regression setup that would have caught it, in the very same pass. Both trees are now refused whole and the refusal is pinned by tests, so the boundary cannot silently narrow again. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#985)
- the same repair boundary now also stops a dispatched member from running the remit writer through a multi-line command, a subshell, or a brace group. The guard read only the first line of a command and treated a subshell or brace opener as though it were the command itself, so three ordinary invocation shapes reached the writer, whose run rewrites every audit member definition and invalidates every clearance marker on the pull request. The check now reads the whole command and inspects it two ways, so refining how it splits a command apart can no longer silently drop a refusal. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#988, #1006)
- the document shell no longer logs React hydration mismatches on every dev-server page load. One of the React Router components in the shell was not handed the request nonce, so the server and the client disagreed about the nonce on a dev-only stylesheet link and React reported the divergence a dozen times per test run. The Quality Gate holds a zero-console-warning standard, and a permanent floor of hydration errors is exactly what hides a genuine one; production output and the CSP nonce are unchanged (#975)
- the Tests workflow now runs the end-to-end suite when a pull request changes only end-to-end files. Its test-relevance allowlist covered the Playwright config but not the directory holding the specs and their helpers, so such a pull request reported the required check green having run no browser tests at all (#975)
- the pre-merge audit gate's shell member no longer claims a file that no member reviews. Its declared remit listed a committed workflow-template build artifact, but no roster glob reaches it: that whole committed template tree is deliberately ownerless, because each file is a byte-identical copy of a source template another member does review, pinned by a drift guard that fails the moment a copy diverges. An engineer reading the remit line could therefore believe a hand-edit to that artifact was reviewed, when it would in fact merge unaudited and then be regenerated away by the next bundle. The stale claim is dropped and the reason a replacement does not belong there is recorded in its place, so the carve-out reads as intentional instead of as an oversight to correct. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#970, #971)
- the pre-merge audit gate no longer deadlocks on a refusal that nothing can clear, and no longer mistakes a lost audit report for a clean pass. A Code Audit Team member that withheld its clearance and later cleared that same unchanged content — the case where its finding is resolved by an explicit operator acknowledgment rather than a code change, so nothing re-keys the content — left behind a refusal that outranked every later clearance, and the remedy the gate itself advertised, re-running the member, provably could not clear it; hand-deleting the marker was the only exit, which is precisely the bypass the design forbids. A member now retires its own refusal as an explicit, recorded act while clearing (`--supersede-refusal`), and an ordinary clearance still never retires one, so re-running an auditor until it passes stays blocked. Separately, a member whose report never reached the orchestrator was indistinguishable from one that genuinely found nothing, because its clearance alone satisfied the no-op detector and suppressed the retry; the detector now also requires the member's durable findings record, so a lost report is retried instead of merging behind a green gate with every finding, including the suggestions the clean-pass contract obliges an operator to resolve, silently dropped. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#931, #941)
- the release runtime-dependency scan now reads a shipped script that names a family of files by pattern as the family it is. A reference whose pattern opens part-way through a filename (`.claude/agents/code-audit-*.md`, or the far more common `${var}` form) was truncated at the pattern and the surviving fragment checked as if it were a whole path: it matched nothing shipped, so the scan reported a dependency leak against a legitimate line, and because the gate fails closed that phantom would block every distribution test scenario and `/gaia-release` with them. Such a fragment is now checked as the directory holding the family, the same question a pattern opening at a directory boundary (`.claude/hooks/*.sh`) already asked, so prose naming a file family no longer costs a release while a family sourced from a release-excluded directory is still caught. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#981, #984)
- the release runtime-dependency scan now reports an IO failure as an IO failure. An unreadable or vanished script encountered during the leak scan threw past the subcommand's own handler and exited 1 — the code the module reserves for "leaks detected" — so a maintainer or a CI log reader was sent to debug a phantom dependency leak instead of the real IO error. The read is now wrapped like its sibling IO helpers and exits 2 (unexpected), so every IO path in the scan honors the documented exit-code contract. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#925)
- the release scrub can now strip a single element from a JSON array, so a maintainer-only hook registration can live in the committed `.claude/settings.json` and be removed from the adopter bundle at build time. The existing `json-strip` transform could only delete an object key, so the distribution-preflight-check hook had to stay registered in each maintainer's gitignored local settings (#916), where it never travelled to another maintainer clone; a new `json-strip-array-element` transform removes an array element by predicate, leaving a well-formed array and doing nothing when the predicate matches nothing, so the registration moves back into committed config and is scrubbed at bundle time. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#917)
- the CLI no longer truncates large output when a caller reads it through a pipe. Every subcommand exited the moment its work finished, discarding whatever the operating system had not yet accepted, so any output past roughly 64 KB was cut mid-character; a `gaia wiki commit-classify --json` over a few dozen commits handed its caller unparseable JSON while the identical command redirected to a file wrote all of it. The wiki sync reads exactly that command through a pipe, so on any non-trivial range it was working from corrupt input. Output now drains fully before exit, and exit codes are unchanged. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia`
- an automated revert pull request now gets a title the rest of the tooling can read. It was opened as `Revert: <original title>`, which is not a valid conventional-commit subject, so after a squash merge it landed on the default branch as a subject the release bump and the wiki classifier both skipped. Revert PRs are now titled `revert: <original subject>`, and `revert` is a recognized commit type that contributes a patch-level release. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia`
- the destructive-command guard no longer denies legitimate removals under its own whitelisted scratch directories. Those directories were whitelisted in their relative spelling only, so writing the absolute path that `.claude/rules/shell-cwd.md` requires of every Bash call was refused, leaving the always-loaded rule and the guard impossible to satisfy at once; both spellings are now accepted, with an absolute path that escapes through a `..` segment still denied. The guard also judges each command segment on its own `-r`/`-f` flag, and recognizes a flag only as its own shell word, so a hyphenated directory name ending in `r` or `f` is no longer read as a destructive flag and a plain `rm` of a scratch file is no longer denied on the strength of the path it sits in. Together these are what let an agent working in a linked worktree delete the scratch files it created, instead of being refused every spelling of `rm` and routing around the guard with an unguarded tool. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#943, #950)
- the worktree write-guard no longer stops guarding when the hook process's own working directory leaves the repository. It located the main checkout from that directory rather than from the calling agent's, so a process directory outside any repository ended the adjudication before any of the agent-aware logic ran, and the guard allowed the stale-path wrong-checkout write it exists to deny, saying nothing, because an inert guard here allows rather than blocks. Both locations it compares now come from the calling agent's reported working directory, with the process directory as the documented fallback, and a working directory belonging to an unrelated repository still cannot arm or disarm it. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#957)
- a plan run under worktree isolation can write its phase-findings ledger again. The plan folder lives in the main checkout by design and the orchestrator writes `PROGRESS.md` there after every phase, but the worktree write-guard denied exactly that write, so any `/gaia-plan` run that resolved to worktree mode lost the findings record that carries sub-agent observations across context compression and became unresumable, surfacing only as an unexplained tool refusal partway through rather than as anything naming the conflict. The guard now exempts the main checkout's `.gaia/local/plans/` and `.gaia/local/specs/` ledgers, which have no worktree-side copy that a stale path could be confused for, while every other non-shared path under `.gaia/local/` stays guarded as before. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#952)
- filing a tech-debt issue no longer risks losing that issue to its own cleanup. The recipe created the issue and removed its scratch body-file in a single command invocation, and a `PreToolUse` hook decides an entire invocation at once, before any of it reaches the shell; a guard that denied the cleanup therefore dropped the creation standing beside it, leaving no issue filed and no output naming the cause. Observed on a real filing, where only a manual cross-check against the issue list revealed the finding had been silently lost. The two steps are now separate calls, so a denied cleanup costs at most a leftover scratch file, which is visible and recoverable. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#951)
- the wiki sync's first-pass commit filter now actually filters. Its type rules matched only unscoped conventional-commit subjects (`fix:`), so on a repo that writes scoped ones (`fix(hooks):`) no rule could fire and every commit fell through the fail-open default to `WORTHY`, promoting a whole range into the expensive per-commit deep read the first pass exists to avoid; on GAIA's own history 94 of 138 commits landed on that default and a single sync deep-read essentially everything. Type matching now reads a parsed commit prefix, so scoped, breaking, and bare subjects all reach the same rule, and the `debt`, `ci`, and `build` types, which had no rule at all, are now classified instead of defaulting to worthy. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#935)
- `/gaia-debt` no longer silently truncates the backlog it orders, and no longer re-derives values its own query already had. The ordering query carried no result limit, so a backlog past `gh`'s default of 30 issues was cut off with nothing said, and it projected raw label objects while the surrounding prose parsed the dedup key and the handler value back out of each issue body by hand. The query now carries the limit and emits those values directly, with the key's line number as a number so a citation on line 4 no longer matches one on line 42, and it emits each issue's body unconditionally so the two security screens can read the content they exist to screen (#930)
- the pre-merge runbook's post-merge cleanup now carries a worktree arm, so the documented sequence no longer dead-ends under worktree isolation. It prescribed a single cleanup path beginning with `git checkout main`, which cannot succeed from inside a linked worktree because the main checkout already holds `main`; an agent following the runbook verbatim stranded there, after the merge had already landed. The merged-state poll is now stated once as the whole verification for both isolation modes, with separate cleanup arms beneath it, and the `fatal: 'main' is already used by worktree` error is documented as the expected, benign outcome on that path rather than something to retry the merge over. This matters because GAIA's own isolation reference steers callers into worktrees, including a forced arm that never prompts and a policy value that isolates every run (#929)
- the release runtime-dependency scan no longer reports phantom leaks and exits 1 on a clean tree when run locally. Without `--staging` it walked the whole repo, so a release-excluded script that names a maintainer-only workflow in a trailing comment counted as a leak even though neither file ever reaches the tarball. The local scan surface is now the release manifest, and the report names that scope instead of implying it covered everything, so a bare run agrees with the authoritative post-scrub staging run it exists to approximate rather than training maintainers to skim past a checker that cries wolf. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#924)
- the worktree-path guard no longer denies two classes of correct write. It resolved a target through the deliberate symlinks that share audit markers, debt state, telemetry, and setup state between a linked worktree and the main checkout, so every write to that shared state from inside a worktree, including the tech-debt filing recipe's own body file, was blocked as a wrong-checkout write; those symlinked paths are now exempt, while the rest of `.gaia/local/`, which each worktree owns its own copy of, stays guarded. Separately, the guard inferred "this session's worktree" from a process cwd that concurrently active agents share, so once one teammate entered a worktree, another agent's writes to its own worktree were judged against the wrong root and denied. It now adjudicates only whether a target resolves to the main checkout, the one determination that stays correct no matter which worktree the shared cwd sits in, and the silent-wrong-write case the guard was built for; a write from one linked worktree into another is left to the caller's own `RESOLVED_ROOT` discipline. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#921, #922)
- the pre-merge runbook now mandates a synchronous audit dispatch, so a merge no longer loses every Code Audit Team report. The documented dispatch asked each member to return its findings and its abort reason as returned text, but never said to run the member synchronously, and a backgrounded subagent routes no text back to the orchestrator. Findings vanished and the no-op classifier had nothing to classify, leaving the operator a correctly fail-closed marker gate with no diagnosis of why it was stuck. The dispatch template now carries `run_in_background: false` and matches the real tool's name and parameters (#919)
- the health audit's bundle-simulation step no longer reports a phantom pair of anomalies on every run. It matched the bare `gaia:maintainer-only` marker name, so two shipped wiki pages that merely document the marker convention counted as unstripped markers, contradicting the authoritative scrub in the same step. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#910)
- three health-audit heals that reach existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia`. `/gaia-wiki lint` no longer reports `.gaia/automation.json` as a dead path on a clone that has not run `/setup-gaia`, since that file is adopter-written rather than GAIA-shipped; `/gaia-fitness` now flags a wiki state file whose recorded commit is unreachable after a rebase, a staleness case its freshness check structurally could not see; and two shipped wiki pages no longer point adopters at a maintainer-only workflow page they never receive. A fourth heal, a false positive in the release runtime-dependency scan, is maintainer-only and affects no adopter clone (#908)
- the shipped code-review audit workflow now stamps the required `GAIA-Audit` commit status on `chore(deps)` / `chore(deps-dev)` PRs, which previously skipped the audit without ever posting it. Where `GAIA-Audit` is a required check this left a dependency-bump PR unmergeable: it passed every other check yet stayed blocked because the required status is a posted commit status, not a job conclusion, so a job-level skip could not satisfy it. The skip path now posts it member-aware, `success` when the bump touches only the frontend auditor's surface (the common case), `pending` when a specialized auditor CI cannot run is also dispatched. Reaches existing adopters on their next `/update-gaia` (#882)
- the distribution suite only exercised `gaia init` against the real staged release tree; every other bundled subcommand had zero staged-artifact coverage, so a `release-exclude`/scrub regression on a shipped template or CLI code path could ship silently, with `run-all.sh` staying green. Two new adopter-flow scenarios close part of that gap: one runs all four `gaia scaffold` kinds and asserts the shipped templates render correctly, the other runs the `gaia setup` subcommands behind `/setup-gaia` and asserts their JSON contract on a scrubbed binary. `gaia setup-ci`, `sandbox`, `ping`, `wiki`, and `update`/`update-deps` remain uncovered. Maintainer-only: `.gaia/tests/` is release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#672)
- the manifest-write guard hook (`.claude/hooks/block-manifest-write.sh`) inspected only the first line of a command, so a write to `.gaia/manifest.json` on a later line of a multi-line command, or one using a no-space redirect (`>.gaia/manifest.json`), slipped past the guard entirely. The guard now tokenizes every line of the command and recognizes the glued redirect-target shape, closing the bypass while keeping its allow-on-ambiguity bias. Defense-in-depth hardening only; the OS-level filesystem sandbox remains the airtight protection for the manifest (#852)
- the release scrub reported 20 distribution-boundary leaks on `main`: a dangling `UAT-NNN` narrative reference, and maintainer-only paths and `code-audit-maintainer-*` agent names left unwrapped by the recent audit-machinery work, hard-exiting the distribution suite at the scrub step before a single scenario ran. Incidental mentions are reworded where the surrounding prose is adopter-relevant; genuinely maintainer-only entries (internal build-source paths, maintainer-only agent names, the release-excluded `resolve-audit-spawn.sh`) are now wrapped in `gaia:maintainer-only` markers. All post-v1.6.1 regressions; nothing shipped. Maintainer-only tooling, so no adopter clone is affected (#847, #849)
- creating a worktree whose name collides with a concurrently-launching session's no longer risks deleting that peer's in-flight checkout. Worktree creation now serializes per name, so a losing race only ever inspects a fully settled peer and leaves it alone, closing the residual timing window the earlier collision fix (#759) could not, since any inspection-only check is a snapshot. The same change self-heals a worktree left half-registered by a session killed mid-creation, which previously wedged that name until it was cleaned up by hand (#762)
- `/gaia-plan` task sub-agent dispatch now carries the same fail-loud tree self-check the pre-merge Code Audit Team dispatch got (#765/#797): a task's first action verifies its working root's tree matches the orchestrator's freshly-captured expectation and stops with no edits on any mismatch, closing a gap where a stale or wrong worktree root could let a task silently edit the wrong tree (#798)
- harden the Code Audit Team merge gate against three latent gate failures plus two smaller defects. The `GAIA-Audit` trailer stamp is serialized behind a portable mutex, so parallel Code Audit Team members finishing near-simultaneously on a multi-member diff can no longer double-commit or wedge on `index.lock`. The CI trailer check now reads the newest `GAIA-Audit` status per context and requires that newest entry to be success, matching the local merge gate, so a newer failed or pending re-run shadows an older success instead of a stale success clearing CI. A pre-merge audit dispatched from a git worktree now reviews that worktree's own tree and hard-stops with no marker if pointed at the wrong tree, closing a silent fail-open where a worktree audit could pass on the main checkout's empty diff. The local success status also surfaces the sha it actually posted to (the pushed PR head) rather than local HEAD. Maintainer-only: this machinery is release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#765, #792, #794, #795, #796)
- the Code Audit Team merge gate now requires every dispatched member to clear the audited tree before the shared `GAIA-Audit` trailer is stamped, so one member's clean pass can no longer stand the whole gate down on a diff that dispatched more than one member. The required `GAIA-Audit` status now posts to the pushed PR head commit (the one branch protection checks) instead of an unpushed local commit, so it reliably lands on the empty-commit stamp path, and the local merge check reads the latest status per context so a later failure supersedes an earlier success (#726)
- the builtin fallback audit roster now grants `code-audit-maintainer-shell` the same five declarative machinery surfaces the committed `.gaia/audit-ci.yml` roster does (`.gaia/audit-ci.yml`, `.gaia/VERSION`, the `code-audit-*` agent definitions, `.claude/rules/**`, and the bundled `code-review-audit.yml` template). That fallback is consulted only when `.gaia/audit-ci.yml` is absent or its `auditors:` block is unparseable, and in that degraded state a change to one of those surfaces was ownerless, so the merge gate dispatched nobody and would have merged it unaudited. A regression test now enforces that every machinery path stays roster-claimed under the fallback roster too. Maintainer-only: the roster entry and test are release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#786)
- file-write `deny` rules in `.claude/settings.json` written as `Write(<glob>)` were silent no-ops: Claude Code matches file mutations only through `Edit(<glob>)` rules (which already cover Write, Edit, MultiEdit, and NotebookEdit), so a `Write(...)` deny never fired. Most consequential, the audit-clearance sentinel guards (`.gaia/local/audit/*.ok`, `*.refused`, `*.carried`) were unenforced, so any tool could write a sentinel and fabricate an audit pass; they are now `Edit(...)` deny rules that block every file-writing tool. The redundant `Write(.env)` and `Write(pnpm-lock.yaml)` denies (already covered by their `Edit(...)` twins) and the four no-op `Write(.gaia/local/**)` allow rules (covered by `Edit(.gaia/**)`) are dropped (#783)
- the code audit's progress-breadcrumb log (the CI step-summary observability file) was a single fixed path shared by every concurrent GAIA session on a machine, since the drop-zone it lives in is symlinked into every linked worktree. Two sessions auditing at once could overwrite each other's breadcrumbs, and a reader, human, orchestrator, or the public Actions step summary, could attribute a peer session's audit run to their own with no signal it wasn't theirs. The log is now keyed to the audited tree, the same way the merge-gate marker already is, so concurrent sessions never collide (#772)
- the bats suites guarding GAIA's own framework bash are now audited before merge. They were owned by no Code Audit Team member: every member's globs match `*.sh`, a `.bats` file is not a `.sh` file, so a pull request touching only test suites dispatched nobody and rode the merge gate's out-of-scope bypass to merge unreviewed. Those suites are the only enforcement standing behind the framework's shell, which made a commit that weakens, skips, or deletes one both the least affordable to merge unaudited and the easiest to slip through. `code-audit-maintainer-shell` now owns them (`.gaia/**/*.bats`, `.github/**/*.bats`, covering every bats tree in the repo, not only the one the report named), and the agent gains a lens for the failure modes a suite's own green run cannot catch: an assertion that structurally cannot fail, a silently loosened or deleted assertion, and a `skip` that retires coverage. Maintainer-only: the roster entry and agent are release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#769)
- creating a worktree whose name collides with one you already have no longer destroys the existing worktree. A collision makes worktree creation fail, and the failing hook's cleanup step force-removed the target path without checking whether that run had created it, so it deleted the worktree already sitting there, along with any uncommitted work in it, and then still reported failure. The cleanup now removes only a path its own run created, and tells you it left the other one alone. An existing worktree is protected whether it was on disk before the run or a concurrently-launched session created it (#759)
- the CI audit workflow's out-of-scope skip and local-mode stand-down resolved their target sha from `git rev-parse HEAD` instead of the pull request event payload the other status writers already use, leaning on an implicit invariant (no commit-creating step precedes either) rather than an explicit one; both now read the sha from the event payload, matching the rest. The same skip step's non-clobber guard could also stand down on an unreadable status read with nothing published for that case, so its terminal PR comment fell through to asserting the gate was "pending, not green" without ever having verified that; a new output and comment branch now tell the author the gate's state is unknown instead. The step's `status_posted` output is also renamed to `success_stamped`, since it read `false` on the members-pending exit even though a `pending` status had in fact been posted (#723, #742, #743)
- the merge gate's non-clobber guard (`.github/audit/audit-success-present.sh`) could report no `GAIA-Audit` success was live for a commit that had one, since a pipe into `grep -q` under `set -euo pipefail` risked a SIGPIPE'd upstream write flipping the match into a false "not live" exit. Unreachable in production today (the endpoint it reads returns at most one short status line, well under the pipe buffer), but the trap stayed armed for a future change to that read. Replacing the pipe with a here-string removes the hazard (#745)
- the pre-merge audit procedure now tells you to run the self-healing auditor first and alone, rather than spawning every dispatched Code Audit Team member at once. Markers are keyed to the tree, which makes the members order-independent and parallel dispatch safe in general, but the frontend auditor is the only member that edits files, and a self-heal is a real content edit: it produces a new tree and so invalidates every marker for the old one, including the ones its siblings are in the middle of earning. Dispatching the whole roster in parallel therefore throws that work away and forces a full re-spawn whenever a self-heal lands mid-flight. Plain parallel dispatch still applies when the frontend auditor is not in the spawn set, or the diff is one it refuses to self-heal by design (#747)
- the pre-merge audit now spawns the Code Audit Team members the diff actually dispatches, instead of always spawning the frontend auditor and no one else. The roster and its merge gate were already correct, but the instructions that decide *who to spawn* named a single auditor, so a diff with no frontend changes still paid for a frontend review, and a diff owned by a specialized member spawned nobody at all, leaving the author to discover the requirement only when the merge was denied. A new spawn oracle (`.gaia/scripts/resolve-audit-spawn.sh`) answers "who must audit this diff?" in one deterministic call, every pre-merge surface now spawns exactly the members it names (zero, one, or several), and the frontend auditor stands itself down when the diff is not its own. **Action required:** none for adopters, though `/update-gaia` will offer one new `.claude/settings.json` allow entry, `Bash(bash .gaia/scripts/resolve-audit-spawn.sh:*)`, which lets the merge procedure run the oracle without prompting (#736)
- a PR reviewed only by an auditor CI cannot run could never clear its own merge gate. CI and the local auditor both write the `GAIA-Audit` commit status, a status has no compare-and-set, and CI's "members pending" write frequently landed last, overwriting the success the local auditor had just posted. The check reverted to pending, branch protection refused the merge, and nothing re-posted, so the only way through was to notice the overwrite in the status history and re-run the status helper by hand. CI now reads before it writes: every step that posts a pending status leaves a live success for the current tree alone, including the local-mode stand-down that fires on the ordinary path where CI defers the audit to the author's machine. It still fails closed in every other direction, both for a success naming a stale tree and for a status it cannot definitively read (#734, #736, #739)
- the `rm -rf` guard denied a **path-qualified** command word's target, and blamed a path the user never wrote. `/bin/rm -rf dist` was blocked even though `dist` is explicitly whitelisted, with the deny text naming an absolute path `/rm` that appears nowhere in the command: the guard anchors each segment at the `rm` word, so the `/` in front of the word became the segment's leading byte and the resulting `/rm` token was judged as though it were the operand. It failed in the safe direction (a false deny, never a bypass), but it was inexplicable from the message, and it also meant the genuinely dangerous spellings were denying for the wrong reason, tripping over `/rm` rather than over the real target. The command word is now skipped by its **position** in the segment, where the anchor guarantees it sits, rather than by its spelling: `/bin/rm -rf dist` and `/usr/bin/rm -rf dist` pass, the dangerous shapes still deny and now deny through the target's own arm, and the `rm` binary named as a target (`rm -rf /bin/rm`) stays denied as the absolute path it is, which a spelling-based skip would have quietly turned into an allow. The hook also gains a `main` entry point so it can be sourced by its suite: the quoted-separator walk is position-preserving, which is the entire reason the guard may skip that walk for a command holding no `rm`, and no end-to-end payload can tell a correct walk from a broken one, so that property is now asserted directly on the helper instead of resting on a comment (#715, #724)
- a multi-member code audit could never clear its own merge gate on the natural path. Code Audit Team markers were keyed to the HEAD commit sha, but `code-audit-frontend` stamps the `GAIA-Audit` trailer, and on an already-pushed HEAD that stamp lands as an empty commit: it advances HEAD while leaving the tree byte-identical. Every sibling member's marker was orphaned the instant it landed, so on a diff that dispatched more than one member, all members could audit the identical tree and the gate would still block, reporting the others as pending. It failed closed, so nothing unsafe merged; the cost was that the obvious move, running the members in parallel, always lost, and the only way through was to run the frontend first and alone and then re-run every other member against the post-stamp HEAD, which nothing documented. Markers are now keyed to the tree sha instead. A marker attests that a member reviewed content, and in git the tree is the content, so the trailer stamp no longer invalidates anything and the members can run in any order. The gate is not loosened: a commit that genuinely edits the tree still invalidates every marker for the old one, which is the re-audit the gate exists to force. The local janitor learned the new key in the same change, since it reaps a marker whose key names nothing live and would otherwise have deleted every tree-keyed marker on its next run (#709)
- the `rm -rf` guard hook (`.claude/hooks/block-rm-rf.sh`) guarded `.git` but not `.claude`, and recognized only the literal lowercase spelling of the `rm` command word. `rm -rf .claude` was **allowed**, which removes every hook, skill, agent, rule, and setting, while `.git` sitting directly beside it was denied and the guard's own deny text for the dotfile glob already named `.claude` as a thing worth protecting. Separately, the command word was matched as a case-sensitive, contiguous `rm` substring even though the *target* half of the guard normalizes properly (it strips quotes and backslashes, which is what catches `rm -rf "$HOME"`), so any spelling bash still resolves to `rm` exited the hook before a single deny arm was reachable and carried every protected target out with it: `RM -rf ~` really deletes home, because macOS mounts case-insensitively by default and `/bin/RM` therefore is `/bin/rm`, as do the quote- and backslash-split forms `r""m`, `"r"m`, `r"m"`, `'r'm`, and `r\m`. Both are closed: `.claude` denies alongside `.git`, and the command word is matched the way bash resolves it, case-insensitively and through quote and backslash removal. Ordinary cleanups still pass, including neighbours like `.claudia`, a word that merely contains `rm` such as `charm -rf x` or a commit message mentioning a "warm restart", and every whitelisted scratch path. Note that a recursive removal of `.claude`, or of any path under it, now denies: removing a worktree or a cached artifact there goes through `git worktree remove` or a deliberately scoped command instead (#713, #714)
- the CI audit workflow could tell a PR author the merge gate was satisfied when it had stamped no status at all. On a PR whose un-audited delta touches nothing the auditor owns, the workflow stamps a `GAIA-Audit` commit status so the merge clears without a ceremonial local audit run, but when `.gaia/VERSION` is missing or empty (a botched merge, a partial checkout) it bows out having posted nothing, and the terminal comment announced the free skip regardless. The gate itself fails *closed*, an absent required check shuts the merge button, so this was misdirection rather than a bypass: the author was told the button was open while it was shut, and the only truthful signal was buried in the step log. The comment now reports whether the status actually landed, and when it did not it says the gate is unsatisfied and names the likely cause. It is the same green-sounding-comment-over-a-closed-gate class as the member-aware fix before it, reached through the version guard instead (#711)
- the release scrub's leak-check for maintainer-only Code Audit Team member names skipped `.github/audit/**`, the one shipped directory whose scripts name audit members by design and so the likeliest place for a maintainer-only member name to reach an adopter. Its sibling leak-check for maintainer-only paths already scanned that directory as "exactly where a maintainer-only path reference would most plausibly leak", so the omission was an oversight rather than a deliberate exclusion. Nothing leaks today (the shipped gate script names only the auditor CI runs), so this closes a hardening gap rather than a live defect; the scrub gates the build instead of rewriting content, so the bytes an adopter receives are unchanged (#710)
- the code audit's security screen was specified so that it either did nothing or blocked everything. A finding carrying "no stable `finding_class`" counted as security-class and could never be filed to a public issue, but `holistic/unclassified`, the fallback class every out-of-scope finding receives when it maps to none of the deliberately-small seeded vocabulary, is precisely that classless state. Read literally, every out-of-scope finding on a public repo had to divert, so the `tech-debt` backlog would always be empty and `/gaia-debt` could never fix anything; read loosely, the trigger was ignored and a genuine security finding's never-public guarantee rested on model judgment rather than on the stated rule. The screen now keys on a finding's content and severity and never on its class: the fallback class is the expected verdict for most out-of-scope findings and carries no security signal, so it is not a trigger, while a genuinely absent or malformed class field still diverts as a broken finding record. `/gaia-audit` had already carved out this exception for its own findings; that carve-out becomes the root rule instead of a local workaround, so the agent, `/gaia-audit`, and `/gaia-debt` now screen identically (#698)
- the release scrub failed against GAIA's own source tree, which broke the distribution harness, the only end-to-end oracle for what actually reaches an adopter. Every harness that stages a tree died in the scrub phase before reaching a single assertion, so the suite reported the same red whether the distribution boundary was correct or catastrophically wrong, and it had been silently broken for everyone running it. The cause was the obscurity rule that keeps the mentorship telemetry location out of shipped surfaces: it matched any `events-<date>.jsonl` filename, but the separately-consented cloud telemetry stream is written by the same NDJSON writer and shares that filename convention by design, so the rule flagged the cloud reaper's own functional code as a leak. The filename shape is shared convention; the directory is the thing worth hiding. The rule now anchors on the mentorship directory instead, which is net *tighter* on the axis that matters, since any mention of that directory now trips it rather than one exact path spelling. Two shipped surfaces that named release-excluded maintainer-only paths are reworded alongside it. The scrub's leak-check gates the build rather than transforming content, so the bytes an adopter receives are unchanged (#716)
- the `rm -rf` guard hook (`.claude/hooks/block-rm-rf.sh`) allowed `rm -rf .*`, which removes `.git` and `.claude`. The dotfile glob sat precisely between `rm -rf .` and `rm -rf *`, both already denied, so the one shape here that is plausibly an *accident* rather than evasion was the one that got through: anyone reaching for `.*` is trying to clear dotfiles, which is exactly when `.git` is what they least want to lose. The same hole covered the `.[!.]*` and `..?*` idioms people reach for to skip `.` and `..`, the brace form `{.,}*`, the `$PWD` spellings of an already-denied target, and `~user`. Separately, a quoted `;`, `&`, or `|` inside an operand truncated the guard's command splitting, so every operand after it went uninspected and `rm -rf ";" $HOME` deleted home unchallenged. Both are closed, and the guard still lets ordinary cleanups through: a glob nested inside a whitelisted path, `$PWD/dist`, and scoped brace lists all remain allowed. One known false-deny stays on purpose, and the hook header now explains why: the guard denies commands that merely *mention* a target inside a quoted string (a commit message describing this fix, for instance), because the change that would fix it would also let `bash -c`, `ssh host`, and `eval` forms smuggle a real deletion past the guard. Deliver such text via `--body-file` / `-F` or a variable (#712)
- the `file-tech-debt` skill left a scratch file behind on every filing. It wrote each issue body to a file under `.gaia/local/audit/` so the body would never reach `argv` (where a `--verbose` CI run would echo it into a public log), but never deleted it afterward, and no janitor sweep matched the leftovers, so they accumulated in the working tree indefinitely. The recipe now deletes the body-file once the create returns, whether it succeeded or failed, and gives each run its own uniquely-named file so a concurrent CI-plus-local pair cannot delete each other's in-flight body (#708)
- the `rm -rf` guard hook (`.claude/hooks/block-rm-rf.sh`) missed most of the ways a dangerous command is actually written, and reliably denied only the sloppiest spelling of each. Every one of these was **allowed**: a quoted target (`rm -rf "$HOME"`), the brace form (`${HOME}`), a backslash escape (`rm -rf \/` handed *root* to rm), a target on a line continuation, an operand written before the flags (`rm $HOME -rf`, which GNU rm permutes and deletes home all the same, so it is live on Linux while looking harmless when hand-tested on macOS), and a dangerous target in any but the first rm of a chain (`rm -rf dist && rm -rf /`). `--no-preserve-root` was also unreachable when written ahead of the recursive flag. The common thread is an inversion worth naming: the guard matched the careless spelling of a target and missed the deliberate one, so writing *good* shell made you more likely to slip past it. The fix is to normalize a target the way bash does, stripping quotes, escapes, and continuations before matching, and to inspect every rm in a command rather than the first. This is defense-in-depth rather than an open door, `settings.json` pre-approves only narrow scratch-path `rm` forms, so a bypassing command still met a normal permission prompt; the missing second layer is what is restored. The hook's header now states plainly that its deny list is a floor and never a ceiling, and names what a text matcher cannot see (computed targets, command substitution, `xargs`, a missing `jq`) along with the literal shapes still open (tracked as tech debt). It also gains the table-driven bats suite it never had, 74 deny/allow cases asserting that each target denies for the *right* reason; the suite and an adversarial audit found six of the seven gaps, only one having been filed (#699)
- `/gaia-debt` no longer recommends batching unrelated issues that happen to sit in the same directory. Its clustering rule pairs issues that share a finding class and a directory, but every filed issue currently falls back to the same unclassified sentinel, so the class half of the test was always true and the rule silently degraded into directory-only matching, which the skill itself rejects as too weak to batch on. It offered a five-issue batch of unrelated hook fixes as a result. The rule now requires a real seeded class, so it stands down while the backlog is uniformly unclassified, related issues still batch by shared file, and directory-level batching resumes on its own once classes are seeded (#707)
- the wiki-style rule's own audit block could not be run as written. Its historical-phrasing grep ended an alternation in a bare `set`, which the env-dump guard reads as a bare environment dump and denies, so an agent auditing a `wiki/**` or `.claude/skills/**` edit got a permission error instead of grep results. The check silently never ran while the rule read as though it had, which is the worst way for an audit to fail. The grep now matches the same strings without tripping the guard, and all five of the rule's audit commands execute end to end (#704)
- `.claude/hooks/block-spec-plan-chain.sh` shipped to adopters but was missing from the manifest, so `/update-gaia` could not see it: an adopter received the hook once at scaffold and never got a later fix to it. It is now registered as `owned`, like the other hooks, and updates normally. This was the first file the new distribution gate caught (#686)
- the spec-kit version check's uvx fallback could stall `/gaia-spec` on a degraded network. That fallback resolves the runtime version by fetching the pinned spec-kit ref, and it runs inside the mandatory `before_specify` gate, so on a captive portal or slow DNS a cache-miss run hung with nothing bounding it. Both of the call's network surfaces are now bounded by their own tooling's knobs rather than a `timeout(1)` that macOS does not ship: uv's HTTP read timeout, and git's low-speed abort, which is the one that matters, since uv fetches a `git+https` ref by shelling out to the system git where its own HTTP timeout never reaches. Neither is a hard wall-clock cap and the script says so; operator-set values win. The uvx route also gains the `specify version` second chance the `PATH` route already had, so a future pinned spec-kit that exposes only the bare subcommand resolves for uvx users instead of blocking them (#677)
- the spec-kit version check could not see a uvx-run spec-kit, so `/gaia-spec` was blocked for anyone who installed spec-kit the way GAIA documents. The check resolved the runtime version only from a `specify` on `PATH`, but GAIA's install route is uvx-mediated (`uvx --from git+…@vX.Y.Z specify`) and never puts one there, so a correctly installed, in-pin spec-kit reported as unresolvable drift and the mandatory `before_specify` hook halted. It now falls back to the uvx route before failing, and its day-cache (which never actually short-circuited) now works, so the fallback resolves once a day rather than on every hook (#676)
- the release pipeline never verified that the committed `.gaia/cli/gaia` and `gaia-maintainer` binaries actually reflect current `src/`, only that they exist and are executable; a maintainer who edited CLI source and forgot `pnpm bundle` could ship a stale binary with nothing to catch it. `release.yml` now rebuilds both binaries pre-publish and fails the release loudly if either differs from the committed bytes. Maintainer-only release tooling, so no adopter clone is affected (#641)
- a comprehensive-audit false-positive class and three health-audit runbook/taxonomy documentation gaps, all surfaced by the same audit run. Bucket B's source-tree grep triage had no mechanical completeness gate, so an incomplete enumeration could default every un-enumerated line to a genuine finding (one run produced 49, then 29, false findings); it now requires the triaged-line count to reconcile against the raw grep output before reporting a verdict. Bucket E's fitness categories are now pinned to the repo root so a monorepo layout can't pull in a sibling directory's `CLAUDE.md`, the marker-strip skip rule in both the runbook and the taxonomy now names all three configured transforms instead of only the HTML-comment one, and the Fixer lanes table gains `.gaia/scripts/**` under the `source-ts` lane. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#665)
- a live distribution-boundary leak, and a release-blocking manifest staleness. Two shipped `.gaia/scripts/` scripts referenced their release-excluded bats suites in unwrapped comments, and neither leak-check primitive's scope reached `.gaia/scripts/` or `.github/audit/`, so a shipped script could point at a callee absent from every adopter clone and no check would say so. Both scopes now cover those trees, the references are marker-wrapped, and the runtime-dependency check no longer mistakes a reference to a shipped directory for a leak. Separately, `release manifest --check` (a hard CI gate) was failing on a stale manifest and is clean again. Maintainer-only release tooling, so no adopter clone is affected (#663)
- the maintainer-only Comprehensive Audit phase's report-only invariant now catches a leaf mutating gitignored `.gaia/local/` state instead of only tracked files, closing the gap that let a refuter destroy a live SPEC folder by probe-running a destructive script unnoticed; refuters are now explicitly read-only. The invariant's inventory is scoped to the durable working-state directories it protects (`specs/`, `plans/`, `handoff/`) rather than all of `.gaia/local/`, so routine background churn (a debt-count refresh, telemetry appends, other audit runs) no longer false-flags as an integrity violation. Maintainer-only and release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#663)
- a shipped wiki page, the Code Audit Team ADR, leaked maintainer-only content (the `code-audit-maintainer-*` auditors and `.gaia/cli/src/` references) unwrapped and was missing from `.gaia/manifest.json`; the content is now marker-wrapped and the page is registered (#663)
- three adopter-shipped trees now fall inside the bundle-time `maintainer-paths` leak check: the composite actions under `.github/actions/`, the CI issue-body templates under `.gaia/templates/`, and the spec-kit preset under `.specify/presets/gaia/`. A maintainer-only path reference landing in any of them would have shipped to adopters pointing at a release-excluded path absent from their clone, with no check to flag it. Additive scope on an already-enforced check, and all three trees are clean today, so no adopter clone is affected (#662)
- a tech-debt fix merged from inside a linked git worktree now refreshes the main checkout's `Run /gaia-debt` statusline nudge instead of leaving it frozen for hours. Linked worktrees shared five `.gaia/local/` state paths but not `debt/`, so a worktree merge armed the worktree's own throwaway debt-count cache and sentinel while the main checkout's stayed stale until the 6h refresh TTL; `debt/` now joins the shared-state symlink set (#640)
- maintainer-only surfaces under `.gaia/tests/` no longer point at a never-authored smoke-harness convention doc. Six references cited `.claude/rules/maintainers/smoke.md` as the routing rule for classifying a verification artifact by shape (release-gate harness vs UAT runbook), but the file was missing, so every pointer dangled; it is now authored as a concise, path-scoped maintainer rule that states the shape axis and defers the authoritative decision table to `.specify/extensions/gaia/rules/smoke.md`. Release-excluded, so no adopter clone is affected (#637)
- the `Run /gaia-debt` statusline nudge no longer lingers for hours after the tech-debt backlog empties. A tech-debt issue closed outside a `/gaia-debt` PR merge (a direct `gh issue close`, the web UI, a teammate, another session) dropped no refresh sentinel, so the stale open count survived to the refresher's 6h TTL and a restart just re-read the cached value. A direct `gh issue create`/`close`/`reopen` now refreshes the count alongside `gh pr merge`, and a SessionStart hook reconciles a stale-high count on the next restart (network-free when the backlog is already empty), so the nudge clears promptly (#598)
- a `/gaia-audit` apply run no longer orphans a machine-local memory file. Stage 2 removed a `MEMORY.md` index pointer before the `promote`/`delete` that removes the file it points at, so on a delayed `--apply` where that removal skipped on drift, the pointer was already gone while the source file survived, de-indexed with no path back to it. Index-pointer removals now run last and are gated on their referenced action having landed (via a new optional `depends_on` on `delete-entry`), so a skipped `promote`/`delete` leaves both the source file and its pointer intact (#593).
- a plan executed inside a linked worktree no longer records zero execution cost. The per-commit cost tally located the active plan by globbing for its `RUNNING` sentinel relative to the current directory, but a worktree never symlinks `.gaia/local/specs`/`plans`, so from a worktree the sentinel (which lives only in the main checkout) was invisible and every execute row was silently dropped. The plan search is now anchored to the main checkout, matching how the cost ledger already resolves. This matters because `cost.jsonl` is the sole durable cost record and a merged plan/spec folder is eventually pruned (at merge for a spec-less plan, or after the retention window for a spec-tracked one), so a dropped execute row was unrecoverable; ships with worktree bats regression coverage (#587)
- the GAIA-generated wiki and update-deps CI workflows no longer fail their auto-merge `git push` with `Invalid username or token` whenever the maintenance chain produces committable changes. A `claude-code-action` step runs in OIDC mode and rewrites the checkout-persisted git credential around a short-lived App token that no longer authorizes the push; the shared auto-merge partial and the update-deps wave-B push now reset the credential to the workflow `GITHUB_TOKEN` immediately before pushing. Runs that hit the "no changes" guard exited before the push and reported green, so the defect stayed hidden until the first run that produced real edits (#584)
- the `/gaia-plan` orchestrator template now spells out the explicit worktree-creation step its worktree-mode paths reference.
